Adaptive running training-plan method — final (v5)

Generated 2026-10-22 · Level: Full + sub-councils
How this council worked · Level: Full + sub-councils
  1. Triage: 8/14 raised to Full by subject factors (health risk, automated generation, uneven evidence, ultra specificity); the user chose the larger variant (Full + sub-councils), road/flat only, self-coached runners, manual history entry.
  2. Research: 5 working groups (training science, existing methods & apps, runner profiles, safety, nutrition), each a researcher then an adversarial verifier.
  3. Experts: 3 hearings, 27 expert answers over 5 domains, each domain with 2–3 different source bases; 6 fact-desk passes.
  4. Design: 8 critic seats; an architect plus 4 module authors wrote v1; 2 sub-council rounds by distance family (5–10K, half & marathon, 50–100K) and 2 plenary rounds (architecture, adaptation & safety, runner experience, acceptance pre-check, implementation readiness, red team) produced v2 → v5.
  5. Checks: worked examples traced by hand at each version; build script and rule/message checkers on v5; independent acceptance check and internal audit (same process, not independent of it).

How to read the grades. All research for this method used web-search result summaries only. Every attempt to open a primary source (paper, book, guideline) failed, so nothing here is VERIFIED and CORROBORATED is the ceiling. Grades: CORROBORATED = several independent sources agree; CREDIBLE = one solid source, or several that are not independent (a single study repeated by many summaries counts here, never higher); ALLEGED = one source or an interested source; CONTRADICTED = better evidence says otherwise; UNKNOWN = no usable evidence. Rules also carry a basis: evidence, coaching convention, design default (the method's own cautious choice, untested) or engineering. Sources with an interest are tagged alike on every side: [corp] app and device firms, [industry] industry-funded research, [trade] trade bodies, [state] state agencies (NHS, FDA, MDCG), [author] commercial plan authors, [society] professional societies.

The 60-second version

  • What it is. A written specification (16 modules, 290 rules, 31 plan checks) for software that builds and re-plans running plans from 5K to 100 km on road or flat courses, for one week to several years.
  • Who it is for. The team that will build a website for self-coached runners. Runners never read the specification; they see plain-language cards. Coaches can check every rule's reason and grade.
  • What it does. It screens health first, says honestly whether the goal fits the time available (with ranked alternatives if not), builds the plan from a known school's template, and has a separate checker test every session against load limits; a failing plan is never shown. Missed runs or illness trigger a re-plan without "catch-up".
  • What it does not do. It does not diagnose, treat or replace a doctor. It does not claim to prevent injury: no study shows adaptive plans beat fixed ones. It has never been run, and 206 of its 290 rules are untested design defaults.
  • How to start. Developers read module 12 (data model and rule format), then 07 (generation), parameters.md and messages.md. Coaches read 00, rationale.md and the worked plans in 13. The first release (5K to half marathon, default school only) can be frozen only after release blockers B-1 to B-5 (§6) close.

Release profiles. MVP: 5K, 10K, half; default school only; plans up to 24 weeks; start ladder, return-to-run and adaptation. R2 adds the marathon and other school presets, after the 65+ and over-3-hour tests pass. R3 adds 50K and 100K and back-to-back long runs. Each profile is a subset of the full specification, not a lower bar.

2. Acceptance check

The internal audit (same process, not independent) rated the pre-fix version. The architect then applied all 15 required fixes. Nobody re-audited, so the "after fixes" column below rests on the change log plus the chairman's spot checks: the corrected plan values are in 13, the message checker passes, and the rule checker fails exactly as declared on 27 rules. Where views differ: the architect's coverage table (00 §9) points to where each of the 13 criteria is met and grades none. The auditor rated four of them Partly, and this report keeps those four at Partly.

3. Key design choices

ChoiceWhyAlternatives rejectedEvidence grade
Seven fixed rule tiers (red flags › screening › hard limits › user constraints › goal › progression › preferences), unique priorities, fail closedSafety must win the same way every time; a squeezed plan concentrates loadWeighted scoring; firing order by insertion; clamping silentlyArbitrary order of equal-priority rules: CORROBORATED (software engineering)
Effort before pace (talk test); paces only from a race or test logged at the time; no age-formula heart-rate zonesNovices have no anchor; age formulas err by ±10–12 beatsPaces from recalled times; "220 − age" zonesCORROBORATED (talk test, heart-rate error); talk test for novices UNKNOWN
Single-run spike cap: a run ≤ 1.10 × the longest run of the last 30 days, blocked above 2.0 ×; weekly 10% only a soft conventionIn the largest cohort, risk tracked single-run spikes, not weekly changeWeekly 10% rule as law (trial null); acute:chronic ratio gates (CONTRADICTED)CREDIBLE (one cohort; several summaries); applying it to duration is UNKNOWN, so a design default
No catch-up, ever: missed work is dropped; a missed hard session may only replace an easy oneCatch-up creates the spikes linked to injuryRedistributing missed volumeSpike link CREDIBLE; the rule itself is a design default
Marathon "classic-capped" default: long run ≤ 50% of weekly time and 180 min, at least 21.1 km; up to 3 optional runs to 29 km / 4 hA Daniels default refused most first-timers on 6 h a week; one cohort links an ~18-mile run to fewer race-day injuriesDaniels default; pure distance cap (penalises slow runners); 29 km as defaultShare CORROBORATED as description of practice; time cap is convention (duration–injury UNKNOWN); cohort CREDIBLE
One guard for runs over 3 hours: tissue tolerance must be measured, and the run is run-walkNo duration–injury data in any familySeparate guards per distanceUNKNOWN → design default
Ultra caps and floors: long run ≤ 60% of peak-week time and 240 min (50K) / 330 min (100K); minimum longest run 25 / 33.3 km, below which the goal is infeasibleVolume carries ultras; a floor stops "stretch" from stretching without endFail closed at the time cap (Contrarian); a 40% share (fails runners whom practice sees finish)Field practice CORROBORATED as description; no road-ultra injury data
Never-runners follow the NHS [state] Couch-to-5K steps: 9-rung start ladder, ≥ 9 weeksUse the tested convention; 8–13-week programmes show similar injury ratesThe 5-week return-to-run ladderCORROBORATED (C25K, GRONORUN); rung values CREDIBLE
Schools as whole presets (Daniels, Hansons, Pfitzinger, Higdon, Galloway, FIRST, Koop [author]); intensity mix a parameter; every change logged against the templateNo school has beaten another; changes must be visibleSilently picking one school; mixing schools; generating from scratchNo head-to-head trials: CORROBORATED; presets graded as convention
Manual entry only (owner decision): "measured" = logged within 48 h; recalled entries are "estimated", widen ranges, never unlock harder trainingSelf-reported distance is fairly reliable, pace only moderatelyTrusting claims fully; refusing to plan without logsCREDIBLE
Rule language: a CEL subset with four-valued logic (true, false, error, unknown); restrictive safety rules fire on unknown; unanswered symptom forms hold (no session, no alarm); safety fields offer no "I don't know"A blank must never unlock load, and a closed form must not raise an emergency messageJSON Logic (missing = false); DMN/FEEL; a custom language; v4's three-valued readingCORROBORATED/CREDIBLE (standards and vendor documentation)
Step-ups need answered pain on ≥ 70% of the last 14 days' sessionsPain rules are blind without answers80% (above typical prompt-answer rates of ~75%)Answer rate CORROBORATED; threshold design default
Validator separate from the generator; independence stated as partial; an oracle written by someone who has not read the generatorCode that reuses a formula cannot catch its errorA validator that imports generator functionsCORROBORATED (Knight & Leveson); metamorphic testing CREDIBLE
Wellness boundary: fixed published screen, generic "talk to a doctor" texts, outputs limited to proceed / restricted / pause / no plan; never names a conditionNaming or scoring a condition could make the tool a medical deviceSymptom triage; risk scores; in-app rehabBoundary CORROBORATED (FDA, MDCG [state]); whether a screen is a device UNKNOWN

4. Where the experts and the council disagreed

Experts (reported, not averaged).

  • Polarized vs pyramidal intensity: pyramidal default, polarized opt-in.
  • Long-run caps: Daniels' 25% or 150 min clashes with Higdon's and Pfitzinger's 20-mile runs, so each school keeps its own preset.
  • Pain during running (≤ 5/10 vs ≤ 2/10): thresholds CONTRADICTED, so a cautious 3/10 default plus a next-morning check.
  • Thirst vs a drinking plan beyond two hours: thirst, drinking caps and a hot-day card.
  • Carbohydrate above 90 g/h: opt-in, labelled unproven.
  • Deload rhythm (3:1 vs 2:1): UNKNOWN in all three source bases; a convention parameter.

They agreed that no load model is validated for prescription and that prior injury is the strongest risk factor.

Council. The 180-minute marathon cap and the one cohort that favours longer runs pull against each other. Optional extension runs leave this tension in place. Dissents on record:

  • Contrarian: would end ultra "stretch" verdicts more strictly, and would keep 50K aerobic level 4 rather than 3. The ultra distance floors were his condition for partly withdrawing the first dissent.
  • First Principles: the 65+ check-up gate is a policy rule with no comparative evidence.
  • Room C (red team): asked for 80% answered-pain coverage; v5 uses 70%.

5. Safety and limits

Risk picture (10 §0).

  • Overuse injury is common (7.7 per 1,000 h for recreational runners, 17.8 for novices; CORROBORATED) and usually mild. It concentrates in single-run spikes and in novice weeks.
  • Bone stress injury has no rate in the council files, but costs 44–127 days. Low energy intake and prior bone stress raise exposure.
  • Heat illness and over-drinking (EAH) are rare but can kill. They concentrate in hot races and events over 4 hours, and slow runners are more exposed.
  • Cardiac arrest in races occurs in about 0.54–0.60 per 100,000 participants, near 1.0 in marathons, and is far higher in men. About one in three cases is fatal (CORROBORATED, two registries).

The method claims no reduction in any of these.

Do not use it for diagnosis, injury treatment or rehabilitation; during pregnancy or after birth without the special mode and a professional's clearance; for trail, mountain or multi-day races; for runners under 14; or as a substitute for medical screening.

See a professional if the screen raises any "yes", or if any of these appear: chest pain, fainting, unusual breathlessness, palpitations, bone pain that worsens or comes at rest or at night, confusion or collapse in heat, or pain that persists or keeps returning. The plan then pauses until clearance is confirmed on a later day. Runners aged 65 and over need a check-up before hard sessions or runs over 3 hours.

Not medical advice. The tool gives general training guidance and does not replace a doctor, physiotherapist or dietitian.

6. Open issues and next version

Release blockers (MVP).

  • B-1 PAR-Q+ 2023 licence, and clinical sign-off of how its items map to plan restrictions.
  • B-2 Legal reading of whether the screen, the age-triggered check-up advice and the safety hold remain general wellness.
  • B-3 Clinical review of every safety message and of the start and run-walk ladders.
  • B-4 27 rules that MVP code reaches are prose only: key-session choice, dosing and progression of 16 session modules, distance demand for 5K–half, 3 phase rules, 4 long-run fuelling rules. The rule checker fails until they get machine records. That failure is deliberate and is the honest state of v5.
  • B-5 The hand-computed plan values and the test fixtures must be encoded as data, and the oracle written independently.

A pilot must measure (against published baselines, with no "safer" or "better" claims): prediction error, completion and drop-out, injuries per 1,000 hours, red-flag pauses, fail-closed rate, spike-cap overrides, fuelling problems and understanding. It pauses on any serious adverse event, or if injuries exceed the novice baseline.

v6 would: give the pacing/mental-skills node a decision role; formalise the 65 prose rules; tune design defaults from pilot data; encode fixtures from the primary books so other schools become executable; run the comprehension test; and revisit the 180-minute cap if duration data appear.

7. Limits of this report

  • The audit was internal (same process, never independent). Its indicative score is not a quality claim, and the post-audit fixes were not re-audited.
  • Evidence comes from search summaries only; no primary text was opened.
  • The method has never been executed. Every plan value is hand-computed. The audit found 7 arithmetic errors, now corrected, and more are likely until the oracle runs.
  • All runner data is self-report, so a false "no pain" defeats the pain rules.
  • Only the default school preset is executable. The marathon (R2) and ultra (R3) parts are specified but not ready.
  • The specification reflects council files up to 22 October 2026.

8. Glossary

TermMeaning
CELCommon Expression Language, a small published language for conditions; the method's rules use a subset of it
Couch-to-5K (C25K)The NHS nine-week programme that takes non-runners to 30 minutes of running
DeloadA planned easier week inside a training block
Design defaultA value the method chose cautiously because evidence is missing; shown to runners as "our cautious choice, not tested"
EAHExercise-associated hyponatraemia: low blood sodium, usually from drinking too much during long events
Fail closedWhen no safe plan exists, show none and offer alternatives instead
Fixture / oracleA test case with its expected answer / an independent recalculation of the generator's numbers
InvariantA condition every valid plan must satisfy, checked by the validator
MVP, R2, R3The three release steps: 5K–half, then marathon, then ultras
PAR-Q+A published pre-exercise health questionnaire
PresetA whole training school's settings (for example Daniels or Hansons) selectable as one unit
Prose only (text_only)A rule written in words but not yet as a machine record
Spike capLimit on how much one run may exceed the longest run of the last 30 days
StretchVerdict meaning the goal may be reachable but is uncertain or tight
Talk testJudging effort by how easily you can speak while running
TaperPlanned reduction in training before a race
Tissue toleranceHow much running load the body has shown it can handle; it sets volume growth, separately from fitness
ValidatorSeparate code that checks every plan before it is shown

Behind the verdict

The working files of this run, collapsed by default.

Charter & room draw

charter.md

COUNCIL CHARTER — Adaptive running training-plan method (20 Oct 2026)

Question

Design a theoretical method (a "tree of competences" plus explicit rules and tools) that generates and adapts running training plans from 5K to marathon and 50–100 km (road/flat), adapting to each runner's level, ambition, available time, background and constraints, and to any plan duration (very short to multi-year, split into phases). Fully goal-driven. The next step (not this council) is to implement it on a website used directly by self-coached runners. Reader / user: the project owner (English). Today: 2026-10-20. This is a real design project, not a test: work to professional standard.

Mode

Design / Build. Artifact: the method specification in design/ (module files), versioned v1 → v5. Acceptance criteria: criteria.md (binding; every required change must name the criterion it serves).

Expert domains

See experts.md. Five domains × three source bases (academic & official / practice & field / critical & independent). Two-source rule applies to every claim.

Quality rules (binding)

Follow the skill's quality charter. Run-specific points:

  1. Evidence: sports science mixes strong findings, small studies and coaching folklore. Grade everything: VERIFIED (primary source opened) / CORROBORATED / CREDIBLE / ALLEGED / CONTRADICTED / UNKNOWN. A single-source claim is ALLEGED and cannot by itself justify a rule.
  2. Web access: WebSearch works. Pages can be opened through the web-fetch agent type (give it full URLs and a precise question). Open primary sources (studies, position stands, guidelines) for decisive claims; only then may you write VERIFIED.
  3. Schools of thought (e.g. polarized vs pyramidal intensity, high vs moderate volume, Daniels / Pfitzinger / Hansons / Lydiard / Canova / Norwegian approaches, ultra-specific approaches) are presented neutrally and turned into explicit parameters or defaults with rationale, never silently picked.
  4. Safety first: users are self-coached. Red flags, screening and load limits must be conservative, explicit and graded; the method states clearly that it does not replace medical advice.
  5. Implementability: rules must be deterministic and testable; where judgement is needed, state the default.
  6. Plain language for runners, rationale for coaches, glossary for every term.

Seats (critics of the design)

  • Contrarian — how does the method fail? injury, drop-out, nonsense plans, edge cases, misuse.
  • First Principles — what is the method for? what must the generator really decide? what is essential vs decoration?
  • Expansionist — what would make it remarkably better or more widely useful? (upside claims need evidence)
  • Outsider — a first-time self-coached runner: can they understand the inputs, the plan and the explanations unaided?
  • Executor — can it be built, run and maintained? in what order? what is the minimum viable rule set?
  • Status-quo advocate — established plans and methods already work: what does this method really add, and what must it not break?
  • Context Realist — does it hold across profiles: beginners vs elites, age, sex, injury history, shift work, 3 h vs 12 h per week, 4 weeks vs 4 years?
  • Systems Architect (specialist) — is the model formal, consistent and implementable as a rule engine and data model? are rules deterministic, conflict-free and testable?

Configuration (as approved)

Level: Full + sub-councils. Mode: Design / Build.

  • Research: 5 working groups (researcher → adversarial verifier): WG1 training science · WG2 existing methods, plans and apps · WG3 runner profiles & constraints · WG4 safety & limits · WG5 nutrition & fuelling.
  • Experts: 5 domains × 3 (academic & official / practice & field / critical & independent): exercise physiology · coaching practice 5K→100K · sports medicine & injury prevention · nutrition & fuelling · knowledge modelling & adaptive systems.
  • Build: architect v1 → 2 sub-council rounds (3 sub-councils: 5–10K · half & marathon · 50–100K road) → v2, v3 → 2 plenary rounds (3 rooms each) → v4, v5.
  • Checks: fact desk + expert hearings between rounds; reframe loop; acceptance check; internal audit (same process — never called independent); red-team plenary room.
  • Outputs: method spec (module files), rule & data schema, worked example plans, rationale, report, process map, transcript.
criteria.md

Acceptance criteria (approved by the user, 20 Oct 2026)

Scope decisions: road / flat running only (ultras 50–100 km on road or flat courses; trail, elevation and technical terrain are out of scope for this version). Users are self-coached runners using the tool directly (no coach in the loop): safety rules and explanations must stand alone.

  1. Distances. Covers 5K, 10K, half marathon, marathon and 50–100 km (road/flat), with the performance determinants of each distance made explicit.
  2. Input model. Defines the inputs (level, training history/background, goal or target time, available days and hours, plan duration, constraints such as injury history and age) and, for each, how it changes the plan.
  3. Any duration. Handles plan durations from very short (≤ 4 weeks) to multi-year, through a phase decomposition (e.g. base, build, specific, taper, transition), including rules for when the available time is insufficient (e.g. adjust the goal).
  4. Competence tree. A competence tree/graph (aerobic base, threshold, VO2max, running economy, durability/fatigue resistance, fuelling, mental skills; terrain only as "flat/road") with prerequisites and progression levels.
  5. Explicit rules. Plan generation rules are explicit and deterministic; where judgement is unavoidable, the method says so and states the default.
  6. Adaptation. Adaptation rules for missed sessions, illness, fatigue or pain signals, race/test results and life changes, with re-planning logic.
  7. Safety. Load-progression limits and safety rules with evidence grades; red flags that mean "stop / see a professional"; pre-participation screening.
  8. Evidence. Every training principle is graded and rests on at least two independent sources; disagreements between training schools are explicit and turned into parameters where possible.
  9. Goal feasibility. A feasibility check of the goal against current performance and available time, with caveats.
  10. Modularity. A library of sessions and phase templates as reusable modules, each with goal, prerequisites, dose, progression and exit test.
  11. Implementation-ready. A data model, a rule format usable by a website rule engine, and 3–5 worked example plans for distinct runner personas.
  12. Understandable. Usable by a self-coached runner (plain explanations) and checkable by a coach (rationale), with a glossary.
  13. Validation. A plan for validating the method (expert review, simulation/test cases, pilot users, metrics).

Research

wg1.md

WG1 — Training science (FINAL, verifier pass, 20 Oct 2026)

Process. The verifier ran 14 WebSearch queries. Publisher and PubMed pages are blocked by the proxy and no primary source was opened, so the grade ceiling is CORROBORATED. Grades below reflect the verifier's re-check. Claims not re-checked keep the researcher's grade (marked [R]).

1. Determinants by distance

  • Core model (VO2max, sustainable fraction (threshold or critical speed, CS), running economy). CORROBORATED [R].
  • Durability is a fourth determinant. CORROBORATED [R] (Jones 2024, doi:10.1113/JP284205). The Valencia half→marathon paper also names durability as the reason marathon prediction degrades (fphys.2025.1718298). There is no validated field test.
  • Marathon pace vs CS: recreational runners raced at 84.8% ± 13.6% of CS, closer to CS in faster runners. The data are training logs from more than 25,000 runners (Smyth & Muniz-Pumares 2020, MSSE; link UHRA repository). CORROBORATED. The SD is large, so treat the 79–93% range as a population band, not an individual target.
  • 5K / 10K / half weightings: CREDIBLE (synthesis).
  • 50–100 km, road: two non-elite studies found weekly training volume and marathon personal best associated with 100 km time: Knechtle et al. 2010, JSCR, n = 66 men, r² 0.22 and 0.33; Tanda 2015, OAJSM, where volume mattered more than training pace for 100 km and the reverse held for the marathon (link link). Both are cross-sectional regressions with modest samples. CORROBORATED (association), upgraded from UNKNOWN. The trail-derived claim that VO2max/RE do not predict 160 km stays CREDIBLE.

2. Intensity distribution

  • Silva Oliveira 2024 (Sports Med, 17 studies, n = 437): polarized (POL) was better for VO2peak, SMD 0.24 (95% CI 0.01–0.48; 11 studies, n = 284; I² = 0%). The benefit appeared only in interventions under 12 weeks (SMD 0.40) and in highly trained athletes (SMD 0.46). Time trial, time to exhaustion and threshold speed or power did not differ (link). CORROBORATED.
  • Rosenblat 2025 (Sports Med 55:655–673, individual-participant-data network meta-analysis, 13 studies, n = 348): no overall winner. Competitive athletes may do better with POL and recreational athletes with pyramidal (PYR). The method used to set zones mattered little (stir.ac.uk/research/hub/publication/2095779; Tübingen repository). CORROBORATED. Both meta-analyses report small effects from short trials. They do not justify a strong prescription either way.
  • Muñoz 2014 and Stöggl & Sperlich 2014: CORROBORATED [R], single studies.
  • Norwegian double threshold: the elite description is CORROBORATED [R]. Efficacy in amateurs is UNKNOWN.

3. Load quantification

  • Session RPE: CORROBORATED [R].
  • Monotony and strain (Foster 1998, MSSE 30:1164, n = 25): the definitions are CORROBORATED. The abstract ties illness to individually identifiable thresholds, mostly of strain. The fixed "monotony > 2.0" cutoff appears only in coaching sources (e.g. TrainAsONE) and not in the primary abstract. ALLEGED. It must not be a hard rule.
  • ACWR as an injury predictor: CONTRADICTED [R]. As a descriptor of how fast load changes: CREDIBLE.
  • Banister model as a predictor: poor out-of-sample performance, CORROBORATED [R].

4. Intensity anchors

  • Age-predicted HRmax: Tanaka 2001 (351 studies, 18,712 subjects) gives 208 − 0.7 × age with a SEE of about 10 bpm. An independent sample found SEE 11.4 bpm for Tanaka and 12.4 bpm for 220 − age (PMC8362801; PMC3935487). CORROBORATED. The draft's "7–12 bpm" lower bound is not supported. Use ±10–12 bpm: Tanaka is only marginally better, so neither formula should anchor zones.
  • Talk test: CORROBORATED [R].

5. Periodization, taper, detraining, maintenance

  • Block and reverse periodization: no superior model. CORROBORATED [R].
  • Taper (Bosquet et al. 2007, MSSE, PubMed 17762369; 27 studies): the most effective taper lasted about 2 weeks, with volume cut exponentially by 41–60% and intensity and frequency unchanged. Effect size was 0.72 for the volume cut, against 0.33 for intensity and 0.35 for frequency changes. Heterogeneity between studies was wide. Replicated in endurance athletes by Wang et al. 2023, PLOS ONE, 14 studies (link), which also favoured overload before the taper. CORROBORATED (two independent meta-analyses, abstracts via search). Correction: the draft said frequency was "cut slightly or not at all"; Bosquet found frequency is best kept. The "2–3% gain" figure was not re-checked: CREDIBLE.
  • Detraining: CORROBORATED [R]. The "4–25% performance loss" figure is ALLEGED.
  • Maintenance (Spiering 2021, JSCR, PubMed 33629972): a brief narrative review, not a meta-analysis, of mainly general-population data. Endurance performance was held up to 15 weeks with 2 sessions a week, or with volume cut by 33–66% (as low as 13–26 min per session), if intensity is kept. The underlying Hickson trials used sessions at 90–100% HRmax. The authors say there are insufficient data for athletes. Holding VO2max is not the same as holding marathon or ultra durability. CORROBORATED for VO2max/short endurance. For long-race readiness: UNKNOWN.
  • Deload frequency (3:1, 2:1): no endurance study was found. The only trials are small resistance-training studies, such as a one-week break mid-programme, or planned breaks every 6 weeks reported as non-inferior (secondary source only). A strength-athlete survey reports deloads every 5–6 weeks in practice (Sports Med Open, s40798-024-00691-y). ALLEGED/UNKNOWN for runners. It must be a default with stated rationale.

6. Long runs, durability, strength

  • Longest run and volume associations with marathon time: CORROBORATED [R] (associations, not causation).
  • Back-to-back long runs: no peer-reviewed study. Coaching sources describe the rationale (running on tired legs, lower risk than one very long run) and also say the efficacy is "debated" and that the runs are very stressful (UltraRunning, Trail Runner, RunnersConnect). The "73%" figure is unsourced. ALLEGED (practice convention, plausible). The volume→100 km association in §1 supports volume, not this specific structure.
  • Strength/plyometrics → running economy: CORROBORATED [R].
  • Strength → injury in runners: Toresdahl 2020 (Sports Health, n = 720 first-time NYC marathoners) tested a 10-min, 3×/week, self-directed programme for 12 weeks and found no reduction in overuse injury that stopped training (journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/1941738119877180). This is a low-dose, unsupervised trial, while all-sport meta-analyses (Lauersen) show large reductions. The draft's CONTRADICTED is overgraded. Downgraded to UNKNOWN (running-specific trials null but under-dosed).

7. Race-time prediction

  • Vickers & Vertosick 2016 (BMC SSMR, N = 2,303, split 2:1 into training and validation): Riegel is well calibrated up to the half, but its marathon times were ≥ 10 min too fast for half the runners. Mean squared error was 381 for Riegel, 228 for a model with one prior race plus training data and 208 with two prior races. Sex, age, BMI and training were associated with race speed (link FiveThirtyEight). CORROBORATED.
  • "Half × 2.19": a Runner's World (Ross Tucker) piece attributes it to "a group of statisticians" who surveyed thousands of runners. That is consistent with Vickers et al., but no source names the paper. It equals a Riegel exponent of about 1.13. Fetcheveryone (n = 1,071) found fewer than 5% matched Riegel's marathon prediction. Upgraded to CREDIBLE as a conservative default, not as an established rule.
  • CS-based marathon prediction: the best model (400/800/5,000 m) had 7.67% error, and models overall 8.0% ± 0.5%. CORROBORATED.
  • Valencia regression: R² 0.85, MAE 5.9%. CORROBORATED. The paper compares itself with VDOT but the search did not show VDOT's error figures. VDOT marathon accuracy: UNKNOWN. VDOT is a "pseudo-VO2max" equivalence table, not a validated predictor.
  • Ultra prediction: for road 100 km, only regression associations exist (marathon PB, weekly km). No validated formula: UNKNOWN.

Implications for the method (corrected)

  1. Load model: volume + time-in-zone + session RPE (CORROBORATED). ACWR and Banister are descriptors only, never predictors or gates (CONTRADICTED/CORROBORATED). Monotony is a display metric with no fixed 2.0 gate (ALLEGED).
  2. About 80% easy as the default. tid_model ∈ {pyramidal, polarized}: PYR default for recreational runners, POL as an option for competitive/high-volume runners. Tell users the effects are small and mainly on VO2max, not race times (CORROBORATED).
  3. Double threshold: advanced, high-volume opt-in only, labelled untested in amateurs (UNKNOWN).
  4. Pace from a recent race or test. Talk test and RPE for easy days. HR zones only from measured HRmax or LTHR. Never use age formulas for zones, since their error is about ±10–12 bpm (CORROBORATED).
  5. Predict from 2 or more results. Marathon: default to Riegel exponent ≥ 1.06 with a conservative factor (half × about 2.1–2.2, CREDIBLE), adjusted by volume and longest run. Show error bands of at least ±6–8% (CORROBORATED). Ultra: give a wide range only, from marathon PB plus weekly volume, labelled low-confidence (UNKNOWN formula).
  6. Taper default: 2 weeks (scaled to distance), volume −41 to −60% in an exponential or step decay, intensity and frequency kept (CORROBORATED).
  7. Maintenance mode: at least 2 sessions/week with intensity kept, valid up to about 15 weeks for general fitness. Warn that it does not preserve marathon/ultra durability (CORROBORATED / UNKNOWN for long races).
  8. Return-from-break step-downs at more than 2 weeks and more than 4 weeks off. These are stated defaults (CREDIBLE).
  9. Strength and plyometrics for running economy (CORROBORATED). Injury prevention in runners is UNKNOWN: present it as plausible, never as a promise.
  10. Durability block: progressive long run (CORROBORATED association). For 50–100 km, weekly volume is the main lever (CORROBORATED association). Back-to-back long runs are an optional, practice-based tool (ALLEGED) with a cap and recovery rule.
  11. Deload: parameter deload_every ∈ {3, 4} weeks, default 4 (3 for masters, novices or high stress), about −20 to −30% volume. This is a stated convention (ALLEGED), not evidence.

Verifier notes

  • Corrected the taper rule: frequency is kept, not cut (Bosquet). Added the Wang 2023 replication, which supports CORROBORATED.
  • Added Silva Oliveira's sub-results: no time-trial difference, and the benefit sits in short studies and highly trained athletes. Confirmed Rosenblat's level-dependent finding and its sample size.
  • Corrected the HRmax error to about 10–12 bpm. The 7 bpm lower bound is unsupported.
  • Spiering scoped to the general population and high-intensity sessions, with the authors' own warning about athletes.
  • Strength→injury moved from CONTRADICTED to UNKNOWN, because the runner RCT was low-dose and self-directed.
  • "Half × 2.19" moved from ALLEGED to CREDIBLE. Road 100 km determinants moved from UNKNOWN to CORROBORATED (association).
  • Monotony > 2.0 confirmed ALLEGED: it is not in Foster 1998, which used individual thresholds.
  • Deload and back-to-back long runs searched again. Still no endurance evidence.

Still UNKNOWN

  • A durability field test, and how durability responds to training.
  • A validated performance formula for road 100 km. VDOT's marathon accuracy.
  • Optimal deload frequency in runners.
  • Whether back-to-back long runs work.
  • Double threshold in amateurs. Undulating periodization.
  • Whether adequately dosed, supervised strength training prevents injuries in runners.
  • Primary-source opening of Bosquet 2007, Tanaka 2001 and Spiering 2021. Blocked by the proxy, so these stay CORROBORATED.
wg2.md

WG2 FINAL — Existing methods, plans and adaptive apps (verified, N=2)

Date: 2026-10-20. Verifier pass on wg2-draft.md. Limits: about 14 web searches were run. Every WebFetch attempt failed with a DNS error, including PMC, sdsu.edu and trainright.com. Nothing here is VERIFIED; the ceiling is CORROBORATED, which means two or more independent sources or abstract records agree. Sections 3–4 of the draft (apps and academic systems) were not re-checked and keep the draft's grades.

1. Established methods (corrected)

MethodLong-run ruleQuality/wkGrade (after check)
Daniels≤25% of weekly volume, or 150 min, whichever comes first. The cap is 30% for runners under about 64 km (40 mi)/wk. Secondary sources cite a time cap of 2.5–3 h.2 ("2Q")%: CORROBORATED (runningmagazine.ca, outsideonline, runningwithrock). 150 min: CREDIBLE, because one forum quote may refer to easy-run volume instead. The draft's "2–2.5 h" is corrected to 150 min.
PfitzingerLong runs start at about 15 mi and peak at 22 mi in the 55→70 plans. A medium-long run of about 21–27 km sits midweek. Tiers peak at about 55/70/85 mi/wk (the 105 tier is not re-checked).2–3Tiers: CORROBORATED. 22 mi peak: CREDIBLE (one blog). The 18/55 peak of 20 mi is not confirmed.
Hansons16 mi maximum, which falls at the end of 60–63 mi weeks and so fits 25–30% of volume. No 20-miler.3 SOS (speed/strength, tempo, long run)16 mi and 3 SOS: CORROBORATED (marathonhandbook, runtothefinish, lauranorrisrunning). "≤3 h" cap: ALLEGED (only a truncated blog citing Noakes). "40–50 s/mi slower than MP": ALLEGED (not re-found).
Higdon Novice 118 wk; 4 running days plus 1 cross-training day; long run reaches 20 mi (32 km) with a peak week of about 40 mi (64 km) in week 16020 mi and 4 days: CORROBORATED. 40 mi peak: CREDIBLE (one breakdown; the TrainingPeaks listing shows "24 mi"). At peak the long run is about 50% of weekly volume, which breaks Daniels' cap.
GallowayRun-walk ratios by pace: beginner 2:1; sub-4 h marathon 6:1–9:1; at 14 min/mi, 30 s:30 sLowRatios: CORROBORATED (runnersconnect, livestrong). Long runs "up to or beyond race distance": ALLEGED, no source found. Hottenrott 2016 RCT: unchanged from the draft (CORROBORATED).
Lydiard, Canova, Norwegian——Not re-checked; the draft's grades stand. The Norwegian model remains an elite-only descriptive model (Casado 2023).

Correction to the draft's "real differences" list: the long-run cap is contested, not convergent. There are three families:

  • % of weekly volume plus a time cap (Daniels; Hansons implicitly);
  • an absolute distance near 20–22 mi (Pfitzinger, Higdon);
  • run-walk long runs (Galloway).

A novice plan with a 20-mile run is, by construction, outside Daniels' rule. min(time cap, %) is therefore a defensible conservative choice. It is not a consensus. The draft's "broadest cross-method support" is downgraded to CREDIBLE-as-a-choice.

2. Knopp et al. 2024: corpus of 92 plans (gap filled)

Sports Med Open (2024), doi:10.1186/s40798-024-00717-5. The figures come from the abstract and key-points records (DOAJ, SPOnet/IAT, PMC index, Substack summary). CORROBORATED.

  • The authors analysed 92 publicly available 12-week plans for sub-elite runners, taken from non-peer-reviewed sources, and split them by peak-week volume.
  • Mean weekly volume over the 12 weeks: high 107.7 km, middle 58.5 km, low 42.9 km.
  • Longest run: 35.2 km in high-volume plans and 30.9 km in low-volume plans. Even low-volume plans include a run of about 31 km, so the long run is about 70% of an average week (contrast Daniels).
  • Intensity distribution, pooled over 5 zones: Z1 13.2% · Z2 65.6% · Z3 15.1% · Z4 3.0% · Z5 3.0%. That is pyramidal, not polarized. The same group contrasts this with Haugen's elite data, which is polarized at about 85% Z1–2 and 8% Z4–5.
  • Limitation (the authors' own): the paper describes what plans prescribe. It gives no evidence that the plans work.
  • Taper length and days per week: UNKNOWN, not in the abstract.

3. Taper (Bosquet 2007, checked)

  • Bosquet, Montpetit, Arvisais & Mujika 2007, MSSE 39(8):1358–65 (PubMed 17762369). Of 182 candidate studies, 27 were included, from mixed sports.
  • Reported optimum: about 2 weeks; volume cut 41–60%, applied progressively or exponentially; intensity and frequency kept. CORROBORATED (two secondary summaries plus the PubMed design record). The exact effect sizes were not seen.
  • Counter-evidence for the marathon specifically: Smyth & Lawlor 2021, Front Sports Act Living doi:10.3389/fspor.2021.735220. This is an observational study of more than 158,000 recreational marathoners:
  • strict tapers (a disciplined drop in volume) of up to 3 weeks were associated with better times than shorter or relaxed tapers;
  • 4 weeks gave no further benefit;
  • 64% of runners tapered for ≤2 weeks or without discipline;
  • women benefited more.
  • Grade: CORROBORATED (abstract plus 8020endurance summary). The figure of "about 2.6% / 5–6 min" is ALLEGED (newsletter only).
  • Net: Bosquet's ~2 weeks comes from mixed sports and mostly trained athletes. For recreational marathoners, the data favour 2–3 weeks.

4. Road ultra methods (gap partly filled)

  • Koop (Training Essentials for Ultrarunning, 2nd ed.): intensity-first periodization. VO2 running intervals come furthest from the race, then tempo and steady-state work, then race-specific endurance, then the taper. Course- and race-specific work comes after the aerobic engine and fuelling are built. CREDIBLE (TrainingPeaks plan built on Koop plus a book review). The exact dose: UNKNOWN.
  • Back-to-back long runs:
  • They are widespread in practice. Examples:
  • two consecutive runs of 40–50 km at a 50-mile/100K peak;
  • a 100K plan peaking at 5:30 h plus 2:30–3:30 h the next day;
  • longest runs every 2–3 weeks rather than every weekend.
  • UltraRunning.com frames them as debated: time on feet versus added injury risk.
  • No trial was found. CREDIBLE as practice; ALLEGED as a physiological necessity.
  • Most sources are trail plans, so transfer to road is assumed.
  • 100 km plan volumes (plan publishers): peak about 90–120 km/wk, longest run 30–42 km, typical 3:1 build/recovery mesocycles. ALLEGED to CREDIBLE, because the sources are heterogeneous and mostly trail.
  • Comrades (about 87–90 km, road), Coach Parry:
  • Peak weekly km by goal: finish 45–50; Bronze 50–65; Robert Mtshali 60–70; Bill Rowan 75–80; Silver 100–120.
  • About 85% at "talking pace".
  • 10.5–12 h runners: 60–80 km/wk, although the same article also says "ideally ≥80".
  • Grade: CORROBORATED for tiers (SABC/press plus coachparry). The internal contradiction remains.
  • Comrades official advice for novices: two marathons plus one ultra of 50–56 km about 5 weeks before race day. CREDIBLE (organiser blog plus Parry).
  • Predictors (Knechtle group, OAJSM 2015, cross-sectional): for 100 km, training volume predicted performance more than training pace; for the marathon it was the reverse. Marathon PB is a strong predictor of 100 km time. CORROBORATED (DOAJ, Dovepress and PMC records). These are associations, not dose prescriptions.

Implications for the method (corrected)

  1. Long-run cap = a named parameter with three presets:
  • daniels: min(25% of week, or 30% if under 64 km/wk; 150 min)
  • hansons: 16 mi / 26 km
  • classic: 20–22 mi / 32–35 km

Default for self-coached users: daniels-style min(%, time), with an explicit note that popular novice plans exceed it. — CREDIBLE (a choice, not a consensus)

  1. Benchmark the generator's output against Knopp's corpus. Weekly means of 43/59/108 km, longest run 31–35 km and a pyramidal TID (about 79% Z1–2, about 6% Z4–5) are the "normal ranges" for 12-week sub-elite plans. Flag deviations; do not treat the ranges as targets. — CORROBORATED
  2. Intensity distribution = a parameter (pyramidal default for sub-elite, as in the plan corpus; polarized option). Do not hard-code either. — CORROBORATED
  3. Taper default: marathon 3 weeks, half marathon and below about 2 weeks. Volume cut progressively by about 40–60% in total; intensity and frequency kept; the taper must be "disciplined", with no late volume spikes. — CORROBORATED (Bosquet + Smyth)
  4. Ultra (50–100 km road):
  • weekly volume is the primary lever (Knechtle);
  • optional back-to-back weekend blocks, off by default for beginners, capped (for example day 2 ≤ 50–60% of day 1);
  • a tune-up ultra or a 50–56 km long effort about 5 weeks out (Comrades);
  • peak volume tiers by goal (Parry).

— CREDIBLE

  1. Ultra intensity ordering: offer Koop's "far = intense, near = specific" sequence as the default for ultras. It is consistent with the Canova funnel. — CREDIBLE
  2. Quality sessions: 0 (Higdon novice) / 2 (Daniels) / 3 SOS (Hansons) / 4–5 (Norwegian, elite-gated). — CORROBORATED for the first three
  3. Keep the draft's other implications: pace from current performance, a minimum starting volume per tier, run-walk as a first-class option, safety not resting on progression rate alone (GRONORUN), and transparent adaptation. Their grades are unchanged.

Verifier notes

  • Daniels time cap corrected from "2–2.5 h" to 150 min, plus the 30% tier under 64 km/wk. The draft's "CONTRADICTED" is downgraded to a range (2.5–3 h in secondary sources).
  • Hansons "≤3 h" and the long-run pace were each single-source or truncated → ALLEGED.
  • Higdon peak filled in (about 40 mi, single source). This exposed that novice plans put about 50% of weekly volume in the long run, so the "consensus" min(time, %) default was overstated → reframed as a choice.
  • Knopp 2024 filled in from abstract records. Its pyramidal TID is a school-of-thought datum missing from the draft. Its scope is plan prescriptions, not outcomes.
  • Bosquet confirmed via two secondary sources plus the PubMed design record. Added Smyth & Lawlor 2021 as missing marathon-specific counter-evidence. Scope note: it is observational and may be confounded (disciplined runners differ in other ways too).
  • Ultra section rebuilt: Koop sequence, back-to-backs (practice, debated, no trial), Comrades tiers updated (Parry's peak tiers differ from the draft's averages), Knechtle predictors. Trail-heavy sources are flagged for transfer to road.
  • Galloway "long runs ≥ race distance" → ALLEGED (no source).

Still UNKNOWN

  • Knopp: taper length, days per week, and the volume progression pattern (full text not reachable).
  • Bosquet effect sizes and dose-response detail; Smyth & Lawlor magnitude in minutes.
  • Koop's exact interval doses and back-to-back prescriptions; road 100 km specialist programs (for example Japanese or European 100 km teams); any trial of back-to-back versus single long runs.
  • Pfitzinger 18/55 and 18/85 long-run peaks; Galloway long-run schedule; Lydiard, Canova and Norwegian details (not re-checked).
  • Apps (Runna, Garmin, TrainAsONE, COROS, Polar, Strava, TrainingPeaks): not re-checked; still no independent outcome evaluation of any adaptive app.

Sources (added in this pass)
