wg3.md

WG3 FINAL: runner profiles, constraints and adherence (verified)

Process. I re-checked the researcher's draft (wg3-draft.md) with 14 WebSearch queries. Priority went to the claims the researcher marked [K] (from own knowledge, not searched). WebFetch could not resolve any host (acog.org and scottishathletics.org.uk failed with DNS errors), and publisher sites are blocked. No claim is VERIFIED. CORROBORATED is the ceiling. Source types: S = study, R = review/meta-analysis, P = position stand/guideline/federation rule, C = coach/book, B = blog/secondary.

1. Classifying runner level

  • McKay et al. 2022 (IJSPP 17:317–331) defines six tiers, 0 (sedentary) to 5 (world class): CORROBORATED. The running cut-offs are UNKNOWN. Almost all users of the tool will sit in tiers 0–2, so this scale is too coarse to be the only one the generator uses.
  • WMA age-grading: the 2023 tables, with bands ≥60 / 70 / 80 / 90 % (local / regional / national / world class): CORROBORATED. link ; link
  • Established plans are keyed to weekly mileage. Pfitzinger's tiers are by peak weekly miles (e.g. 55–70, 70–85). Hansons' advanced plan peaks at about 62 mi, with the long run capped at about 16 mi, roughly 25–30 % of weekly volume. Daniels sets training paces from VDOT. Grade: CREDIBLE. Sources are forums and secondary write-ups (e.g. link). No official tables were retrieved. No validated composite level scale exists: UNKNOWN.

2. Individual factors

  • NLstart2run (n = 1,696; 10.9 % RRI). Predictors: older age, higher BMI, earlier non-sport musculoskeletal complaints, no running experience. Grade: CORROBORATED.
  • Volume progression (Nielsen 2014, JOSPT, n = 874 novices). Injury rate did not differ overall between progression groups. Distance-related injuries at >30 % weekly progression: HR 1.59 (0.96–2.66), p = .07, CORROBORATED (link). A firm ">30 % causes injury" threshold is CONTRADICTED.
  • Added counter-evidence: single-session spikes (Frandsen et al. 2025, BJSM 59:1203–1210; ~5,200 runners, Garmin GPS). Injury risk rose when one run was more than 10 % longer than the longest run of the previous 30 days. Reported HRR: about 1.64 for small spikes and 2.28 for large ones. Week-to-week change and ACWR were not associated with injury. The cohort is observational and averaged about 10 years of running experience. Grade: CORROBORATED (link ; link). The exact bin edges have not been checked against the paper's tables.
  • Masters. Performance declines from about age 50. Part of that decline reflects less training rather than ageing. Strength training improves running economy. Grade: CREDIBLE.
  • Minors (was [K]). No World Athletics marathon minimum age was found. UK Athletics competition rules cap road-race distance by age: 15 km at 16, 25 km at 17, marathon from 18. Scottish Athletics' 2026 guidance recommends 12–14 km for ages 16–17. UTMB requires age 18 for 50 km and longer. Race minimums range from 7 (Honolulu) to 19 (Tokyo). A US commentary says AAP distance guidance is old and opinion-based. Grade: CORROBORATED that limits exist and vary, with "marathon ≥18" as the commonest federation rule. Sources: link ; link ; link The physiological basis for these limits is UNKNOWN.
  • Menstrual cycle (McNulty 2020). The effect on performance is trivial, and the evidence quality is low. Grade: CORROBORATED.
  • Pregnancy (was [K]). ACOG CO 804 (2020): in an uncomplicated pregnancy, people who already did vigorous activity such as running may continue with their clinician's agreement. Stop signs include bleeding, regular painful contractions, fluid leakage, dyspnoea before exertion, dizziness, headache, chest pain, muscle weakness affecting balance, and calf pain or swelling. Grade: CORROBORATED, from secondary summaries rather than the ACOG text (link ; link ; link). Whether CO 804 is still current was not checked.
  • Postpartum (Goom et al. 2019). Return to running from about 12 weeks after a load and impact assessment. The authors present it as a reasoning aid, not a protocol. Grade: CORROBORATED.
  • Other-sport background (was [K]). An ITU medical review names former swimmers and cyclists as a high-risk group for running injury. A 75-athlete prospective study found stiffness changes when cyclists move to running. Coaching sources describe aerobic fitness running ahead of tissue tolerance. Grade: CREDIBLE in direction (link ; link). No study quantifies the gap, so the rule "progress volume like a novice, intensity may start higher" is a design default, not evidence.
  • Responders (Montero & Lundby 2017, J Physiol). n = 78 healthy adults, 1–5 × 60-min sessions/week for 6 weeks. Non-response (Wmax change within measurement error) was 69 / 40 / 29 / 0 / 0 % for 1 to 5 sessions. Adding 2 sessions/week for 6 more weeks removed non-response. Grade: CORROBORATED (link). Scope: the training was cycling, in untrained people. The draft said "men", which is not confirmed here.

3. Time availability

  • Volume and marathon time (Doherty 2020; PMC7496388). These are associations: CORROBORATED.
  • FIRST / Run Less Run Faster (was [K]). The structure is 3 key runs (intervals, tempo, long) plus 2 cross-training sessions: CORROBORATED (link ; link). The Furman outcome figures (VO2max +4.8 %, LT pace +4.4 %, economy +2 %) appear only on a training site and come from small uncontrolled studies by the method's own authors: ALLEGED. This is a legitimate low-frequency school of thought, but there is no independent trial behind it.
  • Detraining (corrected). The draft's "4–8 % at 2 weeks, 15–20 % at 10–12 weeks" was not found. Mujika & Padilla 2000 report a 4–14 % VO2max loss within 4 weeks in highly trained athletes, and losses are smaller in people who stay partly active. A 2023 meta-analysis found no further significant change after 30 days, and continued exercise blunted the loss. Grade: CORROBORATED (link ; link). These figures are for highly trained athletes, not recreational runners.
  • Deep-water running (Bushman 1997). Grade: CREDIBLE. Minimum time per distance: UNKNOWN.

4. Goal setting and feasibility

  • Race-equivalence formulas (was [K]). Vickers & Vertosick 2016 (n = 2,303 recreational runners): Riegel is well calibrated up to the half marathon but predicts the marathon too fast, by at least 10 min for half of runners. Adding training volume improves the prediction. Grade: CORROBORATED (link via repository records; link). Limits: self-reported times and a non-random sample.
  • First-marathon readiness (was [K]). Practice sources disagree: from 3 months of injury-free running to 1 year of steady running, and 20–30 mi (32–48 km) per week before marathon-specific work. A 20–24-week plan is advised when current volume is low. Grade: CREDIBLE for the existence of such criteria (several consistent coach and health-system sources: link ; link). Specific thresholds: ALLEGED. "Half marathon first" was not found in any source: ALLEGED. First-ultra criteria were not searched: ALLEGED.
  • Yearly improvement rate and maximum goal stretch: UNKNOWN.

5. Adherence

  • Couch-to-5K (Relph et al. 2023, IJERPH 20:6682; n = 110). 27.3 % completed. 19 % were injured. Prior injury gave OR 7.56 (2.06–27.75) for a new injury, which is imprecise. There was no control group. Grade: CORROBORATED (link). A separate UK study found novices on a self-devised plan were injured more often than those on a structured plan: ALLEGED.
  • Psychosocial predictors (was [K]). A systematic review of exercise referral schemes (24 studies) links adherence to intrinsic motivation, need satisfaction, social support, self-efficacy and modest expectations at entry. A narrative review agrees on self-efficacy. Grade: CORROBORATED for exercise schemes generally; not tested in running apps (link).

6. Life constraints

  • Sleep loss impairs endurance: CORROBORATED. Shift work: there is no evidence base. Timing rules are ALLEGED defaults.
  • HRV-guided training. Vesterinen 2016 randomised 40 recreational runners and found a benefit for HRV-guided training. Meta-analyses find small and mostly non-significant performance advantages, possibly with fewer non-responders. Grade: CORROBORATED for "small or uncertain benefit" (link ; link). Travel and stress (Ruuska): UNKNOWN.

Implications for the method (corrected)

  1. Use WMA age-grade % as the performance level (CORROBORATED). Use a separate tissue-tolerance level from training age and recent km, labelled as a design choice. Present mileage-tiered plan bands (Pfitzinger/Hansons style) as a coaching convention (CREDIBLE).
  2. NLstart2run and C25K risk flags (no running history, high BMI, older age, prior complaints, prior injury) slow progression and trigger screening (CORROBORATED).
  3. Add a per-session cap: no single run more than 10 % longer than the longest run of the last 30 days, by default (CORROBORATED, observational). Do not present the weekly 10 % or 30 % rules as evidence-based (CONTRADICTED as a firm threshold).
  4. Beginner plans must be flexible, with repeatable weeks and injury-prevention content. A 27 % completion rate is the baseline (CORROBORATED). Build in self-efficacy and social features (CORROBORATED in general exercise, untested in running).
  5. Under 18: cap goal distance by age, defaulting to the UK Athletics table (marathon ≥18, ≤15 km at 16, ≤25 km at 17, shorter for younger runners). Ultras require ≥18. Require a guardian or adult (CORROBORATED that limits exist; numbers are a federation convention).
  6. Pregnancy: no performance plans. Offer maintenance only to people who already ran, with clinician approval and ACOG stop signs (CORROBORATED via secondary sources). Postpartum: block plans before about 12 weeks and require self-attestation of clearance (CORROBORATED).
  7. Before calling someone a non-responder, raise frequency toward 4 or more sessions per week (CORROBORATED; cycling, untrained adults, so the transfer to running is assumed).
  8. Feasibility: never use Riegel or VDOT alone for the marathon. Apply a volume-aware correction, or flag the prediction as optimistic when weekly km is low (CORROBORATED).
  9. Return after a break: assume aerobic loss rises over about 4 weeks and plateaus. Rebuild running volume conservatively regardless of aerobic fitness (CORROBORATED for highly trained athletes).
  10. HRV-guided adjustment can be offered as an option, not as a default (CORROBORATED for small or uncertain benefit). Other-sport background: progress volume as a novice (CREDIBLE, design default).

Verifier notes

  • Searched all 8 [K] items. ACOG, minors, FIRST structure and Riegel bias were raised to CORROBORATED. Other-sport background and first-marathon criteria were raised to CREDIBLE in direction, with specific thresholds staying ALLEGED. The FIRST outcome numbers stay ALLEGED because they come only from the method's authors.
  • Detraining figures were replaced (the draft's numbers were unsourced) and scoped to highly trained athletes.
  • Added Frandsen 2025. It is newer counter-evidence that moves the progression limit from weekly change to single-session spikes. It is the largest omission in the draft.
  • Montero & Lundby scope corrected: cycling, non-response defined by Wmax, sex not confirmed.
  • HRV regraded from ALLEGED to CORROBORATED-small, because meta-analyses temper the single trial.
  • Weaker evidence flagged: the C25K OR has a wide CI, and Vickers relies on self-reported times.

Still UNKNOWN

  • McKay running cut-offs, and any validated composite level score.
  • Minimum weekly time per distance. There is no independent trial of a 3-day/week plan.
  • Yearly improvement rates and a defensible maximum goal stretch. First-ultra readiness.
  • A quantified gap between aerobic and tissue adaptation for other-sport athletes.
  • Whether ACOG CO 804 is still current, and the physiological basis for age limits.
  • Plan-design effects on adherence in running apps, and the effects of travel and stress on adaptation.
wg4.md

WG4 FINAL: Safety and limits (verified)

Method: 18 WebSearch queries re-checked the draft. WebFetch failed with DNS errors on every host (SDU repository, fitasaphysio, pheidi), and no web-fetch agent was available, so no primary source was opened. CORROBORATED is the ceiling; nothing is VERIFIED. Source tags: [S] study, [R] review, [P] position stand, [F] federation, [B] secondary/blog.

1. Load progression: the decisive numbers

Frandsen, Nielsen et al. 2025 BJSM (doi:10.1136/bjsports-2024-109380; PMC12421110):

  • Design: 5,205 runners followed for 18 months. 1,820 (35%) reported an overuse running-related injury over 588,071 GPS-recorded sessions. Injury was self-reported.
  • Comparison: each session's distance was compared with the longest run of the previous 30 days.
  • Bands:
  • Reference: up to 10% longer than that longest run.
  • Small spike: >10–30%, adjusted HRR 1.64 (95% CI 1.31–2.05).
  • Moderate spike: >30–100%, HRR 1.52 (1.16–2.00).
  • Large spike: >100%, HRR 2.28 (1.50–3.48).
  • Weekly metrics: the week-to-week ratio showed no association, and ACWR showed a negative dose-response.
  • Grade: CORROBORATED. Sources: the abstract text via search, the Aarhus and SDU portals, physiotutors, Smagpie and Pheidi.
  • Correction: the draft said risk "rose with the size of the spike". It did not rise monotonically: the moderate band's point estimate (1.52) is below the small band's (1.64), and their CIs overlap. Every band above 10% carries risk, and the >100% band is highest. The study is observational, the injury reports are self-reported, the exposure is distance only, and the population is recreational runners, not elites or ultra-runners.
  • Critique: a critique exists (Running with Rock [B], not reviewed in detail).

Nielsen 2014 JOSPT (doi:10.2519/jospt.2014.5164):

  • Design: 874 novice runners with GPS watches over one year. The draft's "small sample" was wrong. Progression was measured over a 2-week window: <10%, 10–30% and >30%.
  • Overall result: no difference between the groups in all running-related injuries.
  • Distance-related injuries: HR 1.59 (0.96–2.66, p = .07) for >30% against <10%. The authors called the study exploratory.
  • Grade: CORROBORATED for the figures reported. The finding itself is non-significant, so it cannot justify a hard rule on its own.

Buist 2008 (10% rule ≈ standard programme, 20.8% vs 20.3% injured) and Ramskov 2018 (volume-led and intensity-led progression carried equal risk): CORROBORATED, unchanged from the draft.

ACWR: it is contested and has no validated protective threshold. Frandsen 2025 adds a negative association. CORROBORATED that it must not be used as a gate.

2. Injury epidemiology

  • Videbæk 2015 Sports Med (PMC4473093): novices 17.8 injuries per 1000 h (16.7–19.1), recreational runners 7.7 (6.9–8.7). Study-level rates ranged from 2.5 to 33.0, with marked heterogeneity. CORROBORATED.
  • Ultra-runners: the authors state that there were too few studies to pool an ultra estimate. The draft's "7.2 per 1000 h" was not found and must not be used. UNKNOWN.
  • The only ultra-specific rate found (Krabak: 65 injuries or illnesses per 1000 h) comes from a 240 km multi-day desert stage race and does not transfer to road ultras.
  • Kim 2012 NEJM (PMID 22236223): 59 cardiac arrests among 10.9 million US marathon and half-marathon runners from 2000 to 2010. That is 0.54 per 100,000 (0.41–0.70): 1.01 in marathons and 0.27 in half marathons, 0.90 in men and 0.16 in women. 42 of the 59 died (71%).
  • Causes: HCM (definite or possible) and atherosclerotic coronary disease were the most common, judged only among the 31 cases with complete data.
  • Trend: the rate in male marathoners rose from 0.71 to 2.03 per 100,000 between 2000–04 and 2005–10.
  • Grade: CORROBORATED. The mean age of 42 was not re-found (CREDIBLE).

3. Pre-participation screening

  • ACSM 2015 (Riebe, MSSE 47(11):2473–9): the screen rests on three factors: current activity, known cardiovascular, metabolic or renal disease or symptoms of it, and intended intensity. Risk-factor counting was dropped, and less medical evaluation is required for asymptomatic people. CORROBORATED (URI repository, APTA, Human Kinetics).
  • The draft's "about 41% fewer referrals" was not found. ALLEGED: delete or source it.
  • PAR-Q+: the current edition is the 2023 consensus version, which supersedes earlier ones. It is a three-step self-screen with ePARmed-X+ as the follow-up. A completed PAR-Q+ is valid for 12 months and an ePARmed-X+ for 6 months. About 1% of users were referred onward. CORROBORATED (HFJC/UBC; Can Fam Physician 2013). Upgraded from CREDIBLE.
  • ESC 2020 sports cardiology (doi:10.1093/eurheartj/ehaa605):
  • Masters athletes with SCORE <5% are low risk and, if asymptomatic and active, may train at any intensity.
  • SCORE ≥5% leads to further evaluation that includes maximal exercise testing.
  • Anyone over 65 who wants to do high-intensity exercise should have a clinical assessment.
  • The task force admits the evidence base is low.
  • Grade: CORROBORATED (ACC expert review; EMJ congress review). Upgraded from ALLEGED.
  • The exact ">35 sedentary" recommendation, with its class and level of evidence, was not retrieved. The age-35 framing is CREDIBLE.
  • The caveat on athlete ECG criteria in masters athletes stays CORROBORATED.

4. Red flags (stop and see a professional)

  • Cardiac: chest pain, syncope, palpitations or disproportionate breathlessness, especially after a viral illness. CORROBORATED.
  • Bone stress injury, Hoenig 2023 BJSM 57:427 (76 studies, 2,974 bone stress injuries):
  • Longest return to sport: navicular 127 days (102–151) and femoral neck 107 days (79–135).
  • Shortest: posteromedial tibia 44 days (27–61) and fibula 56 days.
  • Most complications: femoral neck, navicular, anterior tibial shaft and 5th metatarsal.
  • More than 90% of athletes returned to sport.
  • The authors say the high/low-risk split is not sufficiently validated.
  • Grade: CORROBORATED. The symptom list stays CREDIBLE.
  • Exertional heat stroke: CNS dysfunction together with hyperthermia. Cool first by cold-water immersion, then transport. NATA's target is core temperature <102.5°F (39.2°C) within 30 minutes; this figure is NATA's, not ACSM's.
  • Roberts 2021 ACSM (CSMR 20(9):470) was replaced in full by a corrigendum, so cite the corrected version.
  • Grade: CORROBORATED.
  • Exercise-associated hyponatremia (EAH), Hew-Butler 2015 (BJSM 49:1432; CJSM 25:303): drink to thirst. Manage by neurological symptoms, not by serum sodium alone. Give hypertonic saline when encephalopathy is present (confusion, seizures).
  • Grade: CORROBORATED for thirst-led drinking and symptom-led treatment. CREDIBLE for the full symptom list.
  • Neck check unvalidated; REDs (IOC 2023, CAT2); overtraining syndrome (OTS) (Meeusen 2013): unchanged, not re-searched.

5. Return to run

  • After illness or COVID, Elliott 2020 BJSM 54:1174:
  • Prerequisites: at least 10 days' rest and 7 days symptom-free, plus the ability to manage daily living and walk 500 m on the flat.
  • Stage 1: light activity at <70% HRmax for about 15 minutes.
  • It was later updated: people who tested positive without symptoms start at stage 3, and some people need more than 3 weeks.
  • It targets athletes after mild or moderate COVID. Applying it to other illnesses is an extrapolation.
  • Grade: CORROBORATED for the prerequisites and stage 1 (BJSM infographic; University of Michigan handout). UNKNOWN this run for the durations of later stages.
  • Postpartum, Goom, Donnelly and Brockwell 2019 (Athletics NI PDF; CSP):
  • Return to running from about 3 months after birth, after passing load and impact tests.
  • The authors call the 6-week sign-off outdated and describe the guidance as an aid to clinical reasoning, "not a protocol".
  • The evidence base is expert opinion.
  • Grade: CORROBORATED.
  • Pregnancy, ACOG CO 804 (2020):
  • Without obstetric or medical contraindications, activity is safe. Women who were active before pregnancy, including at vigorous intensity, may continue.
  • Absolute contraindications include significant heart disease, placenta previa after 26 weeks, preterm labour, ruptured membranes and preeclampsia.
  • Warning signs that mean stop: vaginal bleeding, regular painful contractions, fluid leakage, dyspnoea before exertion, dizziness, headache, chest pain, calf pain or swelling, and muscle weakness that affects balance.
  • Grade: CORROBORATED (OPQIC, Lamaze, AMSSM). Upgraded from ALLEGED. The 150 min/week figure is CREDIBLE.

6. Minors

UK Athletics, as reproduced in the Scottish Athletics 2026 sheet, has two tables:

  • Maximum (hard cap, by age on race day): 12 km at 15, 16 km at 16, 25 km at 17, marathon at 18.
  • Recommended (lower): about 8 km at 14–15 and 12–14 km at 16–17.
  • The two tables are not fully consistent.
  • Grade: CORROBORATED (several Scottish Athletics and UKA documents).

Run Canada: the draft's "marathon ≥18; 10K only after puberty" was not found. Canadian marathons set their own age minimums: 18 for the marathon and 16 for the half (Toronto, Calgary), and 19 in Ottawa. Athletics Australia publishes a "FIT model" age table.

  • "No marathon under 18": CORROBORATED.
  • "No 10K before puberty": ALLEGED. Puberty is also not a usable input for the generator.
  • The evidence base for these limits is opinion-based. CORROBORATED.

Implications for the method (corrected)

  1. Screen every user with PAR-Q+ (2023) and the ACSM 2015 three-factor logic. Any symptom, or known cardiovascular, metabolic or renal disease, blocks vigorous sessions until the user confirms clearance. Repeat the screen every 12 months. (CORROBORATED)
  2. Single-session cap as the primary load rule. By default, a session may be at most 1.10 × the longest run of the last 30 days. Above 10%, show a warning; spikes of 30% and 100% are not safer in between. Above 100%, block. Label these cut-points "observational, recreational runners". (CORROBORATED; no causal threshold proven)
  3. Weekly progression ~10% is a convention, not a safety rule. For novices, set the default ceiling at 30% over 2 weeks, labelled as exploratory and non-significant evidence. Do not hard-code it as "proven". (CORROBORATED figures, weak evidence)
  4. Never use ACWR as a gate. (CORROBORATED)
  5. Use a slower profile for an injury in the last 12 months or for novices. (CORROBORATED)
  6. Over-35 or over-65 and high intensity: if any risk factor or symptom is present, advise a cardiovascular risk assessment and exercise test before high-intensity blocks (ESC 2020). Over 65, always advise a clinical assessment before high intensity. (CORROBORATED, low evidence base)
  7. Red-flag engine: cardiac, bone-stress, heat-stroke (cool first), EAH (confusion or vomiting after drinking a lot) and pregnancy warning signs always output "stop and seek care". (CORROBORATED)
  8. Bone stress injury: a suspected injury at a high-risk site means no running until clinical clearance. Return-to-run plans must allow for 3–4+ months (navicular, femoral neck). (CORROBORATED)
  9. Return after illness: fever or systemic symptoms mean no training. After COVID-like illness, wait at least 10 days and 7 days symptom-free, then follow a staged return starting at <70% HRmax for 15 minutes. Any cardiac symptom means referral. (CORROBORATED for the gate; stage durations are a stated default)
  10. Postpartum: no running plan before 3 months, and passing the load/impact tests is advised. Pregnancy: continuing is allowed only for women who ran before pregnancy, with clinician approval and the ACOG stop signs built in. (CORROBORATED, expert level)
  11. Minors: the hard cap is UKA's maximum-by-age table (marathon and ultra at 18 or older). Default to the lower recommended table. Refer users under about 16. (CORROBORATED norm, opinion-based)
  12. REDs and OTS prompts: refer the user, as in the draft. (CORROBORATED)

Verifier notes

  • Frandsen 2025: found the exact bands, HRRs and CIs, and upgraded them from ALLEGED to CORROBORATED. Corrected the claim of a monotonic dose-response. Added the negative ACWR association and the null result for the weekly ratio.
  • Nielsen 2014: corrected "small sample" to n = 874. Added the non-significant HR and the 2-week window.
  • Ultra rate of 7.2 per 1000 h: removed and graded UNKNOWN, because the source review says no pooled ultra estimate exists.
  • ACSM "41% fewer referrals": downgraded to ALLEGED.
  • Upgrades: PAR-Q+, ESC 2020 and ACOG 804 upgraded to CORROBORATED, with concrete content added.
  • Roberts 2021: flagged the corrigendum that replaced the article.
  • Minors: replaced the unfound Run Canada claim with UKA's two tables.
  • Return after illness: added the Elliott prerequisites and the later update.

Still UNKNOWN

  • Spike thresholds for elite runners and ultra-runners. The effect of a spike in intensity, as opposed to distance.
  • Injury rates in road ultras of 50–100 km.
  • The exact ESC class and level of evidence for sedentary people over 35. Durations of Elliott stages 2–6.
  • The full Hew-Butler symptom wording. Run Canada's own youth guidance.
  • Regulatory status (EU MDR or FDA) of a plan generator (ALLEGED; needs legal review).
wg5.md

WG5 final: Nutrition and fuelling

Verified report, 20 Oct 2026. Evidence ceiling: CORROBORATED. Publisher sites were blocked and no primary source was opened. Tags: [PS] position or consensus, [R] review, [S] study, [C] practice, [B] press.

1. Daily carbohydrate and protein

  • Carbohydrate: 3–10 g/kg/day, scaled to training load; up to 12 g/kg/day at extreme load. CORROBORATED. Source: Thomas, Erdman & Burke 2016, J Acad Nutr Diet 116:501, doi 10.1016/j.jand.2015.12.006 [PS]; the same range appears in GSSI and university material.
  • Band edges are CREDIBLE: 3–5 light; 5–7 for about 1 h/day; 6–10 for 1–3 h/day; 8–12 for 4–5 h/day or more. Secondary wording varies, and 8–12 is also attributed to the ISSN stand.
  • Loading at 10–12 g/kg for 36–48 h before events over about 90 min.
  • Raises glycogen: CORROBORATED.
  • Race benefit over a moderate high-carbohydrate diet: UNKNOWN.
  • Risk of GI distress: CORROBORATED [R].
  • Protein: 1.2–2.0 g/kg/day, spread evenly through the day. CORROBORATED [PS ×2].
  • The 0.3 g/kg per-meal figure: UNKNOWN.
  • Train-low:
  • Raises signalling markers: CORROBORATED [R: Impey 2018].
  • No performance benefit: SMD 0.17 (95% CI −0.15 to 0.49), Gejl & Nybo 2021, doi 10.1186/s12970-021-00435-3 [R]. CORROBORATED.
  • A ketogenic diet impaired economy in elite race walkers (Burke 2017, doi 10.1113/JP273230, plus a 2020 replication; both small [S]). CORROBORATED.

2. Low energy availability and REDs

  • IOC 2023 REDs consensus (Mountjoy et al., BJSM 57:1073, doi 10.1136/bjsports-2023-106994) [PS]. CORROBORATED.
  • LEA lies on a spectrum from adaptable to problematic.
  • The CAT2 tool requires a clinician.
  • The 30 kcal/kg FFM/day threshold is not a valid individual cutoff. CORROBORATED. Sources: Lieberman/De Souza 2018, "Rethinking the concept of an EA threshold"; a 2025 narrative review, PMC12899827; Jeppesen 2025, doi 10.1111/sms.70089 [R].
  • It comes from short lab studies in sedentary women.
  • The onset of menstrual disruption varies widely between individuals.
  • Male cutoffs (about 9–25 kcal/kg FFM) are unsettled, and field estimates of EA are imprecise.
  • Prevalence: LEA is common in distance runners (CORROBORATED); a pooled figure is UNKNOWN.
  • Self-report warning signs (CORROBORATED [PS, R]):
  • absent or irregular periods
  • bone stress injury
  • unintended weight loss
  • persistent fatigue or underperformance
  • frequent illness
  • low libido (LEAM-Q)
  • restrictive eating

LEAF-Q (score of 8 or more = at risk) is validated in women only, and it screens rather than diagnoses.

3. Race fuelling by duration

Expected durationCarbohydrateGrade
< 45 minnoneCORROBORATED
45–75 minmouth rinse or small amountsCORROBORATED (mixed evidence in running)
1–2.5 h30–60 g/hCORROBORATED (Thomas 2016; Jeukendrup 2014, PMC4008807)
> 2.5 hup to 90 g/h, glucose + fructoseCORROBORATED (mostly one group)
> 2.5 h, trained gut90–120 g/htolerance CREDIBLE; performance benefit over 90 g/h UNKNOWN

120 vs 90 g/h

  • Podlogar 2022 (Eur J Appl Physiol, PMC9560939) [S]: 11 cyclists over 3 h.
  • 120 g/h raised exogenous oxidation compared with 90 g/h.
  • It did not further spare endogenous carbohydrate.
  • Performance was not measured.
  • Viribay 2020 (Nutrients) [S]: 26 elite trail runners. 120 g/h led to better 24 h recovery markers than 60 or 90 g/h. Race time was not the outcome.
  • No running-performance RCT was found.

Practice and caffeine

  • Ultrarunners take in 25–71 g/h; faster finishers eat more. CORROBORATED.
  • Caffeine at 3–6 mg/kg (ISSN 2021, doi 10.1186/s12970-020-00383-4) [PS]. CORROBORATED.

4. Hydration, hyponatremia and heat

  • Hew-Butler 2015 EAH consensus (Clin J Sport Med 25:303; BJSM 49:1432, doi 10.1136/bjsports-2015-095004) [PS]. CORROBORATED.
  • Drinking to thirst prevents overdrinking, the main cause of EAH, while avoiding dangerous dehydration.
  • Severe EAH is treated by medical staff with 3% saline.
  • Planned drinking: ACSM 2007 advises keeping losses under 2% of body mass. Goulet 2013 [R] found self-paced performance intact up to about 4% loss. Both positions are CORROBORATED; which suits slow runners in heat is UNKNOWN.
  • EAH risk factors (CORROBORATED):
  • exercise lasting more than 4 h
  • slow pace
  • weight gain during the race
  • female sex or low body mass
  • drinking more than about 1.5 L/h
  • NSAID use
  • heat
  • Sweat rate: weigh before and after, corrected for fluid drunk (Baker 2017 [R]). Typical range about 0.2–1.4 L/h. CORROBORATED.
  • Heat:
  • IOC heat consensus (Racinais 2022/23, BJSM 57:8, doi 10.1136/bjsports-2022-105942) [PS] targets organisers: WBGT monitoring, cooling, and treatment of exertional heat illness.
  • Racinais 2015 (BJSM 49:1164) [PS]: about 1–2 weeks of heat acclimatisation is the key protective step.
  • ACSM 2023 EHI consensus (doi 10.1249/JSR.0000000000001058) [PS]: WBGT modification tables, by region and acclimatisation.
  • Acclimatisation and WBGT-based modification: CORROBORATED.
  • Exact WBGT cutoffs: UNKNOWN; secondary sources quote 28–30 °C.

5. Fuelling as a trainable skill

  • Gut training improves GI tolerance. CORROBORATED: Costa 2017 (n = 25, 2 weeks at 90 g/h) and Miall 2018 [S]; King 2022 found minor effects in highly trained athletes.
  • The claim that it raises oxidation: ALLEGED.
  • Coach progression (CREDIBLE; several independent [C] sources):
  • start 8–12 weeks out at 30 g/h
  • add 10–15 g/h every one or two long runs
  • aim for at least 60 g/h in a marathon, or 80–100 g/h if tolerated
  • rehearse race products: "nothing new on race day"

Implications for the method

  1. Daily carbohydrate: show the band for the user's planned hours, as information only. Range CORROBORATED; edges CREDIBLE (criterion 7).
  2. Race fuelling by expected duration:
  • under 45 min → 0
  • 45–75 min → optional
  • 1–2.5 h → 30–60 g/h
  • over 2.5 h → 60–90 g/h, glucose + fructose
  • Default ceiling 90 g/h. Above that is opt-in only, requires logged tolerance of at least 90 g/h, and is labelled "benefit unproven" (UNKNOWN; criteria 5 and 8).
  1. Fuelling node: required for races expected to last more than 90 min (criteria 4 and 10).
  • Progression: +10–15 g/h per one or two long runs from 30 g/h.
  • Exit test: two race-pace long runs at the target intake with GI score ≤ 2/10.
  • If there is not enough time to progress, the race-day intake is capped at the highest level tolerated in training.
  1. Hydration (CORROBORATED, criterion 7):
  • Default to drinking to thirst; a planned schedule is offered only after a sweat test.
  • Never plan intake above the measured sweat rate or intake that would cause weight gain.
  • Without a test, cap at 800 mL/h. This is a design default (CREDIBLE).
  • Show an EAH warning for events over 4 h.
  1. Heat module (new) (CORROBORATED, criterion 7):
  • Ask about forecast heat.
  • Advise 1–2 weeks of acclimatisation and a slower goal pace.
  • Defer to race WBGT flags; hard-code no WBGT number.
  • Heat-illness symptoms are a "stop and seek help" red flag.
  1. REDs screen:
  • Ask LEAF-Q/LEAM-Q-style questions at onboarding and every 8–12 weeks.
  • A positive answer triggers a referral flag and freezes progression. The freeze is a design default (CREDIBLE).
  • Never compute EA or apply the 30 kcal/kg threshold (CORROBORATED).
  1. Train-low: opt-in for advanced runners only, never on quality sessions, and blocked when the REDs flag is on (CORROBORATED).
  2. Caffeine: optional, 3 mg/kg by default and 6 mg/kg at most. Must be rehearsed; users with declared contraindications are excluded. Dose CORROBORATED; exclusions CREDIBLE.
  3. Scope and referral rules (criteria 5 and 7):
  • Refer to a sports physician and dietitian for a positive REDs screen, disordered eating, amenorrhoea or recurrent bone stress injury. CORROBORATED (IOC 2023; AIS/NEDC).
  • Also refer for diabetes, gastrointestinal, kidney or heart disease, pregnancy, fluid- or sodium-affecting drugs, and minors. CREDIBLE.
  • Never give calorie deficits, race-weight targets or ketogenic diets. CREDIBLE.

Verifier notes

  • 120 g/h: the only 120 vs 90 g/h comparison found is an oxidation study in cyclists with no extra endogenous sparing. The performance grade changed from ALLEGED to UNKNOWN, and an "unproven" label was added.
  • LEA threshold: added the critique of the 30 kcal/kg FFM threshold and a rule banning EA computation.
  • Heat: added the IOC, Racinais 2015 and ACSM 2023 guidance and a new heat module. WBGT numbers are left unspecified.
  • Thomas 2016: citation confirmed; tiers found only in secondary sources, so the edges stay CREDIBLE.
  • Hew-Butler 2015: citation, drink-to-thirst advice and 3% saline confirmed. The bolus volume was not confirmed and is not needed.
  • 800 mL/h: unsourced in the draft, now relabelled as a design default. A "no weight gain" rule was added.
  • Length: trimmed from about 2,400 words; section 6 folded into implication 9.

Still UNKNOWN

  • Race-time effect of 120 vs 90 g/h in road marathons or 50–100 km events.
  • Exact Thomas 2016 band edges and per-meal protein.
  • Exact WBGT cutoffs.
  • LEA prevalence in recreational, male and masters runners, and a valid male EA threshold.
  • Drinking to thirst vs planned drinking for slow runners in heat.
  • Loading benefit over a moderate high-carbohydrate diet.
  • How long gut-training adaptation lasts.
  • Sodium needs in 50–100 km events (only one modelling study).
wg1-draft.md

WG1 draft — Training science (researcher pass, 20 Oct 2026)

Process note. I ran 24 searches, then the shared search budget ran out. WebFetch failed and the proxy refused PubMed, PMC, Wiley and BMC (403), so no primary source was opened. The grade ceiling is therefore CORROBORATED. [bg] marks background knowledge not re-searched, capped at CREDIBLE or ALLEGED. Source tags: S = study, R = review or meta-analysis, C = coach/practice source, B = blog.

1. Determinants by distance

  • Core model: VO2max, sustainable fraction (threshold or critical speed, CS) and running economy (RE). CORROBORATED (R, Joyner & Coyle 2008, via summaries; doi:10.3389/fspor.2021.719434).
  • Durability ("resilience") is a fourth determinant. In 18 marathoners, 90 min of running lowered VO2peak and lactate-threshold speed. Runners whose threshold speed dropped less ran faster. CORROBORATED (R, Jones 2024, doi:10.1113/JP284205; S, PMC12547624). There is no validated field test.
  • Weight by distance (synthesis, CREDIBLE):
  • 5K: about 95–100% VO2max. VO2max and RE dominate.
  • 10K: near CS. CS and RE.
  • Half: just under threshold. Threshold and RE.
  • Marathon: fraction sustained, RE, durability and fuelling. Recreational runners race at 79% of CS (6-h finishers) to 93% (2.5 h), mean 84.8%. CORROBORATED (S, Smyth & Muniz-Pumares 2020, n > 25,000; PMC7664951).
  • 50–100 km: about 40–70% VO2max. VO2max and RE predict 50–68 km but not 160 km results. Threshold VO2, fat use, volume and conservative pacing gain weight. CREDIBLE, from trail samples (R, ResearchGate 349974208; S, PMC8144128). Road 100 km: UNKNOWN.

2. Intensity distribution

  • Meta-analysis (17 studies, n = 437): polarized (POL) gave a small VO2peak benefit (SMD 0.24, CI 0.01–0.48), mainly in short studies and highly trained athletes. Time trials did not differ. CORROBORATED (R, Silva Oliveira 2024, doi:10.1007/s40279-024-02034-z).
  • Individual-data network meta-analysis: POL = pyramidal (PYR) for VO2max and time trial. Subgroups: competitive athletes favour POL, recreational runners possibly PYR. CORROBORATED (R, doi:10.1007/s40279-024-02149-3).
  • Recreational 10K study: POL −5.0% vs between-thresholds −3.6%, difference not significant. POL won only among adherent runners. CORROBORATED (S, Muñoz 2014, IJSPP). Stöggl & Sperlich 2014 (mixed sports): POL +11.7% VO2peak (S, doi:10.3389/fphys.2014.00033).
  • Common ground: about 75–85% easy. The schools differ on threshold vs above-threshold hard work.
  • Norwegian double threshold: two lactate-guided sessions at about 2–4 mmol/L per day, 2–4 times a week, on high easy volume (elites run 120–180 km/week). The elite description is CORROBORATED (R, Casado 2023; R, sjsp.aearedo.es; C, mariusbakken.com). The reviewers call for trials, and efficacy in amateurs is UNKNOWN.

3. Load quantification

  • Session RPE (CR-10 × minutes) is valid and reliable. It correlates with heart-rate TRIMP at r = 0.65–0.91. CORROBORATED (R, Haddad 2017, doi:10.3389/fnins.2017.00612; S, PMC5873345).
  • Monotony and strain. Monotony = mean daily load ÷ SD. Strain = weekly load × monotony. The definitions are CORROBORATED. The "> 2.0" danger threshold is ALLEGED [bg].
  • ACWR: it has mathematical coupling (Lolli 2019, BJSM), arbitrary decay constants and no causal basis (Impellizzeri 2020; "time to dismiss", 2021). A counterpoint finds coupling effects trivial (ECU 2020). The 0.8–1.3 "sweet spot" as an injury predictor is CONTRADICTED. As a descriptor of how fast load changes it is CREDIBLE (PMC7534938; ro.ecu.edu.au/ecuworkspost2013/7321).
  • Banister fitness–fatigue model: good fit to past data, poor out-of-sample prediction. Its parameters are poorly identifiable, and the fatigue term added nothing under cross-validation. CORROBORATED (S, Marchal 2025, Sci Rep, hal-04999009; R, arXiv 2505.20859; Gindre 2009 thesis).
  • Volume, frequency and longest run each correlate with marathon time. CORROBORATED (R, meta-regression of 85 studies, PubMed 31704026; S, PMC7496388).

4. Intensity anchors

  • Pace from a race, test or CS is objective, but heat, wind and fatigue degrade it, and it needs re-testing. CREDIBLE.
  • Age-predicted HRmax: standard error about 7–12 bpm for 220 − age. Tanaka's 208 − 0.7 × age is somewhat better. CORROBORATED (PMC8362801; Tanaka 2001). Use a measured maximum or threshold heart rate. Cardiac drift is a further limit [bg].
  • Talk test: reproducible. The stages before speech becomes uncertain are at RPE below 4/10, suitable for Zone 1. At the "equivocal" stage, intensity drifts to about 98% HRmax and 6 mmol/L lactate. CORROBORATED (S, Cortis & Fusco 2024; S, JSSM 2014). It is a good anchor for easy days and poor for threshold work.

5. Periodization, taper, detraining

  • Block periodization: small VO2max benefit (SMD 0.40), but the studies were low quality (PEDro 3.7). A 2022 randomised trial found no performance difference. No model is shown to be superior. CORROBORATED (R, Mølmen 2019, doi:10.2147/OAJSM.S180408; S, Almquist 2022).
  • Reverse periodization: no significant advantage at 5K, and no marathon trials. CORROBORATED (R, doi:10.1186/s40798-022-00445-8; C, USATF). Undulating: UNKNOWN.
  • Taper: about 2 weeks, −41 to −60% volume, intensity kept, frequency cut slightly or not at all. Gain about 2–3%. CORROBORATED via secondary sources (Bosquet 2007 MSSE; Mujika & Padilla 2003). The original abstract was not opened.
  • Detraining: under 4 weeks of rest, VO2max falls 4–14% (blood volume). Beyond 4 weeks the losses are larger but fitness stays above untrained levels. CORROBORATED (R, Mujika & Padilla 2000; R, doi:10.3389/fphys.2023.1334766). The 4–25% performance loss figure is ALLEGED.
  • Maintenance: fitness held up to 15 weeks with 2 sessions/week or −33 to −66% volume, if intensity is kept. The data are from general populations. CORROBORATED (R, Spiering 2021, JSCR, PubMed 33629972; C, trainright).
  • Deload rhythm (3:1, 2:1): coaching convention only. ALLEGED [bg].

6. Long runs, durability, strength

  • Longest run under 25 km went with slower marathons, and weekly volume above 65 km with faster ones. Neither was linked to injury. These are associations. CORROBORATED (S, PMC7496388; R, PubMed 31704026).
  • Back-to-back long runs for ultras: coaching consensus with no controlled data. The "73%" claim is untraceable. ALLEGED (C/B).
  • Strength and running economy: heavy strength training, plyometrics and combined methods improve RE. Plyometrics help most at 12 km/h or less, heavy loads in fast, high-VO2max runners. CORROBORATED (R, Llanos-Lagos 2024, doi:10.1007/s40279-023-01978-y; R, Balsalobre-Fernández 2016).
  • Strength and injury: across all sports, injuries fell to about one third and overuse injuries by half (R, Lauersen 2014; 2018 update). In runners, a randomised trial of 720 novice marathoners was null, and so was a 2024 pilot (S, Toresdahl 2020; S, PMC10820279). For runners, injury prevention is CONTRADICTED/mixed.

7. Race-time prediction

  • Riegel formula (exponent 1.06): accurate up to the half. For the marathon it is too fast by 10 minutes or more for half of recreational runners. Models with training volume do better (mean squared error 228 vs 381). CORROBORATED (S, Vickers & Vertosick 2016, doi:10.1186/s13102-016-0052-y; Slate). The "half × 2.19" rule is ALLEGED.
  • Valencia half-to-marathon regression (n = 8,261): R² 0.85, mean absolute error 5.9%. The authors cite durability for the loss of precision at the marathon. CORROBORATED (S, doi:10.3389/fphys.2025.1718298).
  • Critical speed from 400/800/5000 m bests: marathon error about 7.7%. Starting faster than 94% of CS predicted a >25% slowdown. CORROBORATED (S, PMC7664951).
  • VDOT marathon validation: not found, UNKNOWN. Ultra prediction: UNKNOWN.

Implications for the method

  1. Load model: volume + time-in-zone + session RPE (CORROBORATED). Neither Banister nor ACWR should act as a predictor (CORROBORATED/CONTRADICTED).
  2. About 80% easy as the default. POL vs PYR as a parameter: PYR default for recreational, low-volume runners, POL as an option for trained runners (CORROBORATED, small effects).
  3. Double threshold only for advanced, high-volume runners, labelled as untested in amateurs (UNKNOWN).
  4. Anchor on pace from a recent test. Use the talk test and RPE for easy days. HR zones only from a measured maximum or threshold heart rate, never 220 − age (CORROBORATED).
  5. Predict from 2 or more results. Add a marathon/ultra penalty scaled to volume and longest run, and show error bands of about 6–8% (CORROBORATED).
  6. Taper default: about 2 weeks, −40 to −60% volume, intensity kept (CORROBORATED, secondary).
  7. Maintenance mode: 2 or more sessions a week with intensity kept (CORROBORATED, extrapolated from non-athletes).
  8. Return-from-break step-downs at more than 2 weeks and more than 4 weeks off. These are stated defaults (CREDIBLE).
  9. Strength and plyometrics for running economy (CORROBORATED). Do not promise injury prevention (CONTRADICTED in runners).
  10. An explicit durability block (long-run progression; back-to-back runs for ultras), flagged as practice-based (ALLEGED).

Still UNKNOWN

  • A durability field test, and how durability responds to training.
  • Determinants and prediction for road 100 km.
  • Double threshold in amateurs.
  • Optimal deload frequency.
  • Undulating periodization.
  • VDOT marathon accuracy.
  • Primary-source checks of the Bosquet taper figures and the HRmax error SDs. The verifier should open these.
wg2-draft.md

WG2 draft — Existing methods, plans and adaptive apps (researcher, N=2)

Date: 2026-10-20. Process limits (read first): 20 web searches were run before the shared search budget ran out. WebFetch and curl could not reach any primary source (DNS failure / proxy 403), so no claim here can be VERIFIED; the ceiling is CORROBORATED. Many method details come from fan or coach blogs because the books themselves were not reachable. Items marked (background, not re-searched) come from prior knowledge and are graded no higher than CREDIBLE. The adversarial verifier should check them first.

Grades: VERIFIED / CORROBORATED / CREDIBLE / ALLEGED / CONTRADICTED / UNKNOWN. Source types: [book-summary], [blog], [app-doc], [study], [review], [press].

1. Established methods: structure and comparison

MethodPhase logicQuality sessions/wkLong-run ruleDistinctive ideaGrade
Daniels (VDOT)4 phases of about 6 wk: I base (easy + strides), II repetitions (R), III intervals (I), IV threshold (T) and race-specific work; Phase I can be skipped by consistently trained runners2 ("2Q")≤25% of weekly volume; time cap 2–2.5 h (sources disagree)Paces from a VDOT table based on a race resultPhases and 25%: CORROBORATED [blog ×3]; time cap: CONTRADICTED in detail (2 h vs 2.5 h)
Pfitzinger (Advanced Marathoning)18- or 12-wk plans; first 5–6 wk build volume, peak held about 8–9 wk, then taper2–3 (LT, VO2, MP long runs) plus a midweek medium-long run (11–12 mi)Peaks at about 20–22 mi; marathon-pace segments in long runsTiers peak at about 55 / 70 / 85 / 105 mi per week; starting volume is required (for example, about 30 mi/wk for the 55→70 plan)CORROBORATED [blog ×2, unofficial PDFs]
HansonsMoves from speed work to "strength" work at MP−10 s/mi; 6 days of running3 "SOS" (speed/strength, tempo at goal pace, long run)Capped at 16 mi (about ≤3 h), run 40–50 s/mi slower than MP"Cumulative fatigue": no 20-mile run; the long run is done on tired legsCORROBORATED [blog ×2]
LydiardMarathon conditioning ≥10–12 wk (the famous "100 mi/wk" was for elites) → hills 4–6 wk → anaerobic 4 wk (2–3×/wk) → coordination/sharpening 4–5 wk → freshening0 in base, 2–3 in anaerobic phaseLong aerobic run kept throughoutAerobic base first; "anaerobic running can tear down aerobic condition"CORROBORATED [blog ×3, forum]
Canova"Funnel": general speed and general endurance converge toward race pace; specific period of 6–8 wk before the race; earlier qualities are maintainedSpecific sessions, for example 4×5 km at about MP with 1 km float at 85–95% MP, or special blocks (double sessions at 85–90% MP plus ≥MP)Long runs at or near MP in the specific phaseSpecificity measured as % of race paceCORROBORATED [magazine + coach site]; the primary source (Arcelli & Canova 1999) was not seen
Norwegian double-thresholdHigh volume of easy running plus 3–4 lactate-guided threshold sessions (often two on one day) and 1 VO2max session4–5Not centralPace set by lactate (2–3 mmol/L in practice; 2–4.5 in the paper), measured every 1–3 reps; 150–180 km/wk of easy volumeCORROBORATED (Casado, Foster, Bakken & Tjelta 2023, IJERPH 20:3782, doi:10.3390/ijerph20053782 [review/position] + press). Proposed for world-class athletes; it is a descriptive model, not a trial
HigdonNovice 1: 18 wk, 4 running days plus 1 cross-training day0 (no speed work)Builds to one 20-mile runSimplicity, low barrier to entryCORROBORATED [plan listing + blog]; peak weekly volume UNKNOWN
GallowayRun-walk ratios set by pace (beginner 2:00/1:00; sub-4 h marathon 6:1–9:1)LowVery long run-walk long runs (reported up to or beyond race distance; schedule not confirmed)Walk breaks from the startRatios: CORROBORATED [blog ×2]. RCT (Hottenrott 2016, JSAMS; n=42): run-walk finish time not significantly different, less muscle pain reported (the muscle-pain result is from a secondary summary) — CORROBORATED [study + blog]
Ultra (road)Koop: course- and race-demand-driven, citing literature heavily—Back-to-back long runs: not found in sources this session—Structure UNKNOWN (only blurbs found)
Comrades (Coach Parry, Williamson)January–June seasonLow–moderateWeekend totals about 38–42 km at Comrades pace (Williamson)Average weekly km by goal: finisher 39–45; Bronze 43–50; sub-10 h 55–64; peak 60–80 km for 10.5–12 h runners. Parry sources disagree with each other ("≥80 km/wk")CORROBORATED [coach blog + press], internally CONTRADICTED on peak

Common core across all methods (CORROBORATED by convergence of the table rows): (a) most volume is easy; (b) progression runs from general to specific, with race-pace work concentrated in the last 6–10 weeks; (c) 0–3 quality sessions per week, with beginners at 0–1; (d) a weekly long run that is capped (by % of volume, time or distance); (e) a taper before the race; (f) paces come from a recent race or test rather than from the goal (Daniels, Runna, Pfitzinger) — except Hansons, which uses goal pace for tempo work.

Real differences (to become parameters):

  1. Long-run cap: 16 mi or 3 h (Hansons) vs 20–22 mi (Pfitzinger, Higdon) vs 25% of weekly volume or 2–2.5 h (Daniels).
  2. Number of quality sessions: 0 (Higdon) → 2 (Daniels) → 3 (Hansons) → 4–5 (Norwegian).
  3. Order of intensities: Lydiard and Daniels go from aerobic to fast to specific; Canova works in parallel and funnels toward race pace.
  4. Intensity control: by pace (VDOT) vs by lactate or heart rate (Norwegian).
  5. Walk breaks as a legitimate strategy (Galloway).
  6. Required starting volume, explicit in Pfitzinger and implicit elsewhere.

2. Typical parameters for a generator

  • Plan length: 12 or 18 wk (Pfitzinger), 18 wk (Higdon, Hansons), 24 wk (Daniels in 4 phases of 6 wk), about 20–22 wk (Comrades season, January to June). CORROBORATED.
  • Marathon peak volume: novice plans about 30–40 mi/wk (Higdon; exact figure UNKNOWN); intermediate 55–70 mi/wk; advanced 85–105 mi/wk (Pfitzinger); elite Norwegian 150–180 km/wk. CORROBORATED for the tiers.
  • Comrades (about 87–90 km): average 39–64 km/wk depending on goal, peak 60–80+ km/wk. That is about as much as, or less than, advanced marathon plans. CORROBORATED, with internal contradiction.
  • Long-run cap: see the differences above. For the default, a time cap (2.5–3 h) together with a %-of-weekly-volume cap (≤25–30%) has the most cross-method support. CREDIBLE.
  • Taper: Bosquet et al. 2007 meta-analysis (MSSE): about 2 weeks, volume cut about 41–60%, intensity and frequency kept. CREDIBLE (background, not re-searched — the one search returned forestry papers).
  • External benchmark: Knopp et al. 2024, Sports Medicine – Open (doi:10.1186/s40798-024-00717-5), analysed 92 publicly available 12-week sub-elite marathon plans. It was surfaced by search but could not be opened, so its numbers are UNKNOWN here. WG verifier should extract it: it is the best data source for parameter ranges.

3. Adaptive apps: documented logic

  • Runna: plans are written by coaches and then "tailored" by an algorithm that has not been published. The help centre says plans change only through Pace Insights, which compares paces achieved in intervals, tempo runs and time trials with the targets. It suggests faster or slower paces only when there is a clear trend, and the user must accept the change. Runna does not train to a goal time; it gives an estimated race time. "Adapt for Heat" uses only temperature, duration and session type. An independent check found it more cautious than published heat models. CORROBORATED [app-doc + blog ×2].
  • Garmin (Daily Suggested Workouts / Garmin Coach): DSW inputs are VO2max estimate, Training Status, acute and chronic load (based on EPOC), and recovery time; suggestions update after each activity. Training Readiness combines sleep score, recovery time, HRV status, acute load, 3-night sleep history and 3-day stress history (bands from Poor 1–24 to Prime 95–100); the weights are undisclosed. The HRV baseline needs about 3 weeks of wear. The models come from Firstbeat (acquired 2020). Readiness: CORROBORATED [app-doc manual + blog]. DSW internals: ALLEGED (third-party only).
  • TrainAsONE: machine learning on user data plus "human-in-the-loop" review. Each planned run is scored for benefit against injury risk, with a target of about 3% seven-day injury risk during active training. It often prescribes shorter long runs than traditional plans, tracks acute and chronic load, has injured / return-to-run modes, and re-plans automatically after missed runs. Its own FAQ admits there is "little to no existing science to fall back on". No independent validation was found. ALLEGED (vendor only).
  • COROS EvoLab: load, fatigue and a recommended 7-day load range; "Intensity Trend" 100–150% counts as "Optimized". No evidence was found that it rewrites plans automatically. CORROBORATED [app-doc + press]. Polar FitSpark, Strava, TrainingPeaks / Final Surge: not researched (search budget ran out) — UNKNOWN.
  • No peer-reviewed evaluation of any commercial adaptive running app was found. UNKNOWN / absent.

4. Academic systems

  • Case-based reasoning / recommender work (Smyth, Cunningham, Feely; UCD): marathon time prediction and pacing from similar runners (ICCBR 2017, IJCAI 2018, London Marathon data). Training-plan recommendation from runners who reached the same goal time (ICCBR 2020; 21,000+ runners, 1.5 M sessions). Explainable version (RecSys 2020). Smartwatch-based novice recommendations giving 3–5 incremental changes, possible for up to 80% of 300,000+ runners (UMAP 2023). These are evaluated on prediction accuracy and coverage, not on injury or outcome trials. CORROBORATED [study ×several].
  • LLM plans: Düking et al. 2024, JSSM 23:56. Ten expert coaches rated ChatGPT 6-week novice plans on 22 criteria. The median rose from 2 to 4 as input detail increased. Volume progression was rated below neutral, and health screening and testing were rated neutral; the authors advise against using the plans without coach review. Similar findings exist for swimming and kickboxing. CORROBORATED [study + press].
  • A 2025 systematic review (Appl. Sci. 15:10158) counts only about 14 "training planning and adaptation" studies across computational intelligence, machine learning and deep learning. The field is thin. CORROBORATED [review].

5. Failure modes and criticisms

  1. Progression too fast / injuries: press reports (WSJ, relayed by the5krunner and TechRadar) describe physiotherapists seeing Runna-linked stress fractures and shin splints. No injury-rate data exists. ALLEGED.
  2. A graded plan alone does not prevent injury: the GRONORUN RCT (Buist 2008, AJSM; n=532 novices) found injury rates of 20.8% with a 13-week plan following the 10% rule vs 20.3% with a standard 8-week plan. CORROBORATED [study record + design paper]. This weakens "slow progression" as the only safety lever.
  3. Generic plans ignore the individual (fitness, changing goals, preferences): the stated motivation for the UCD recommenders. CREDIBLE (an author claim rather than a measured finding).
  4. Weak volume progression and missing screening in AI-generated plans: Düking 2024. CORROBORATED.
  5. Opaque adaptation: Garmin readiness weights are undisclosed; Runna's algorithm is unpublished; heat adjustment ignores heart rate. CORROBORATED.
  6. Pace targets set from a goal instead of current ability: Runna explicitly avoids this, citing injury risk; Hansons uses goal pace. Disagreement, presented neutrally. CREDIBLE.
  7. Rigid schedules / poor handling of missed sessions: vendors market automatic re-planning as the fix, but no independent evidence was found. UNKNOWN.

Implications for the method

  • Model method differences as parameters with named defaults: long-run cap (Hansons 16 mi/3 h; Daniels 25%/2–2.5 h; Pfitzinger 20–22 mi), number of quality sessions (0–5), intensity ordering (linear vs funnel), intensity control (pace / HR / RPE) — CORROBORATED.
  • Make the default long-run cap min(time cap, % of weekly volume), since this has the broadest cross-method support — CREDIBLE.
  • Require a minimum starting volume per plan tier (Pfitzinger-style entry criteria) and offer a lower tier or a longer plan when it is not met — CORROBORATED.
  • Set paces from current performance (race or test, as in VDOT or Runna's estimated time), not from the goal; re-test and update only when there is a clear trend, with the user confirming (Runna Pace Insights pattern) — CORROBORATED.
  • Treat the Norwegian model as an elite/advanced option only, gated by volume and experience — CORROBORATED (the paper describes world-class athletes).
  • Offer run-walk as a first-class strategy for beginners and ultras — CORROBORATED (RCT + practice).
  • Do not rely on progression rate alone for safety (GRONORUN); combine it with screening, pain rules and load monitoring — CORROBORATED.
  • Make adaptation transparent and explainable (UCD explainable recommenders; criticism of opaque apps) — CORROBORATED.
  • Use a taper default of about 2 weeks with volume cut 40–60% and intensity kept, adjusted by distance — CREDIBLE (needs verification).
  • Validate against plan corpora (Knopp 2024's 92 plans) and expert ratings (Düking 2024's 22-criterion protocol) — CORROBORATED.

Still UNKNOWN

  • Exact peak volumes for Higdon Novice; the full Daniels long-run cap; Knopp 2024 parameter ranges; Bosquet taper figures (not re-checked).
  • Structure of road ultra methods (Koop, 100 km specialists); back-to-back long runs; Comrades peak-week figures (Parry's own sources disagree).
  • Internals of Polar FitSpark, Strava, TrainingPeaks and Final Surge adaptation, and of Garmin Coach / DSW.
  • Any independent outcome or injury evaluation of an adaptive running app, or any trial comparing named methods.
