wg3-draft.md

WG3 draft: runner profiles, constraints and adherence (researcher, v0)

Process note. I sent 13 WebSearch queries. 9 returned results, 3 failed with rate limits, and 1 hit the shared search budget, which stopped further searching. Primary sources could not be opened: pmc/pubmed DNS failed, and the proxy refused mdpi, bmj, europepmc and crossref. No claim is graded VERIFIED. CORROBORATED is the ceiling. Items marked [K] come from my own knowledge and were not searched in this run. They are graded at most ALLEGED and need checking by the verifier. Source types: S = study, R = review/meta-analysis, P = position stand/guideline, C = coach/club, B = blog/secondary.

1. Classifying runner level

  • McKay et al. 2022, IJSPP 17(2):317–331 (DOI 10.1123/ijspp.2021-0451) [R/framework]. Six tiers: 0 sedentary, 1 recreationally active, 2 trained/developmental, 3 highly trained/national, 4 elite/international, 5 world class. Tiers combine training volume and performance. Other studies already use it, e.g. PMC12152093. The tiers exist: CORROBORATED (several repositories plus a citing study). The numeric cut-offs for running were not retrieved: UNKNOWN. Almost every user of the tool will fall in tiers 0–2, so the scheme is too coarse to be the generator's only scale.
  • WMA age-grading [P/club]: 2023 tables, ages 8–100, road events included. Bands: ≥60 % local, ≥70 % regional, ≥80 % national, ≥90 % world class, 100 % ≈ age world record. Grade: CORROBORATED (USATF Masters, BMAF, WMA-derived calculators). Clubs also use finer scales, e.g. 50–86 % "star" levels. This is the only scheme that is sex- and age-neutral and computable from a single race time. link ; link
  • Training-history descriptors. No validated scale combines training age, weekly volume history and performance. Apps and coaching books each use their own ad-hoc scales (Pfitzinger and Hansons key plans to weekly mileage; Daniels uses VDOT) [K]. UNKNOWN whether any composite is validated. For risk, see the NLstart2run predictors in §2: "no previous running experience" is a predictor.

2. Individual factors

  • Novices / training age. NLstart2run followed 1,696 people in a 6-week supervised programme. 10.9 % had a running-related injury (RRI). Predictors: higher age, higher BMI, previous non-sport musculoskeletal complaints, and no previous running experience. Grade: CORROBORATED (two repository records, plus the BMC Public Health protocol: link). A secondary analysis tied higher intensity the previous week to more RRI and suggested ">60 min at low intensity" was least injurious [S]: ALLEGED (one source).
  • Volume progression in novices. Nielsen et al. 2014 (JOSPT, n = 874, GPS): no overall difference in injury rate between <10 %, 10–30 % and >30 % weekly progression. For distance-related injuries at >30 %: HR 1.59 (0.96–2.66), p = .07 [S]. Grade: CORROBORATED for the result. The popular claim that ">30 % causes injury" is CONTRADICTED as a firm threshold; it rests on a non-significant result.
  • Masters. Speed declines steadily from about age 50 (Willy & Paquette 2019 [R]). Part of the decline reflects less training stimulus, injuries and life demands rather than ageing itself (NSA 2015 [R]). Strength training improves running economy by about 3–6 % (2016 review [R]). Grade: CREDIBLE (one review each, consistent direction). Per-year decline rates were not retrieved. Age-grade tables encode them implicitly: CORROBORATED as a usable proxy.
  • Adolescents / minors. I could not search this (rate limit). [K] Federations and many marathon organisers set a minimum age of about 16–18 for the marathon and longer events. The rules vary by country and race: ALLEGED. Safety default: no plans above the half marathon for under-18s, and parental consent plus a coach or clinician for any structured plan. The WG4 verifier must check this.
  • Menstrual cycle. McNulty et al. 2020, Sports Med 50:1813–1827, 78 studies [R]. Effect on performance is trivial: ES −0.06 overall, largest contrast early vs late follicular −0.14. Evidence quality is low. The authors advise against group-level cycle-based prescription and favour an individualised approach. Grade: CORROBORATED (abstract via several sources). Implication: no cycle-phasing by default. An optional symptom log may drive the same fatigue rules as other symptoms.
  • Pregnancy. I could not search this. [K] ACOG Committee Opinion 804 (2020): previously active women can generally continue running in an uncomplicated pregnancy, with clinician approval and warning signs listed: ALLEGED in this run. The generator should not create performance plans during pregnancy.
  • Postpartum. Goom, Donnelly & Brockwell 2019 [P, clinical guideline]: return to running no earlier than about 12 weeks postnatal, after passing a load and impact test battery and with pelvic-floor screening. The authors call it an aid to clinical reasoning, not a protocol. Grade: CORROBORATED (CSP, Athletics NI, Moving Medicine). Uptake is partial: in a 2024 ICS abstract, 49 % of pelvic-health physiotherapists used a return-to-run guideline, and 52 % of those used this one.
  • Body mass. Higher BMI predicted RRI in NLstart2run: CORROBORATED (see above).
  • Other-sport background (cyclists, team sports). No targeted search was possible. [K] Common coaching practice: aerobic fitness transfers but tissue tolerance to impact does not, so runners with another sport background should progress running volume like novices while intensity can start higher: ALLEGED. The cross-training evidence in §3 is consistent with this.
  • Responders and genetics. Montero & Lundby 2017, n = 78 men, 6 weeks [S]. VO2max non-response fell as training rose from 60 to 300 min/week. Everyone training 4–5 sessions per week responded, and adding 120 min/week for another 6 weeks removed non-response. Grade: CORROBORATED (PMC record plus secondary reviews). HERITAGE (473 sedentary adults): 21 SNPs explain up to about 50 % of the variance in VO2max response: CORROBORATED. Only 1 of 564 people was a uniform high or low responder across 7 traits [S]: ALLEGED. Genetic testing has no role in a consumer generator. The practical message is "raise the dose before concluding you don't respond."

3. Time availability and minimum effective training

  • Volume is associated with marathon time. Doherty et al. 2020, JSAMS 23:182–8 [R]: 85 studies, 137 cohorts. Weekly distance, peak week, longest run and weekly hours all relate to faster finishes (R² 0.38–0.81). Grade: CORROBORATED. Recreational cohort (556 half-marathon, 441 marathon runners, PMC7496388) [S]: more than 65 km/week went with faster times, and a longest run under 25 km with slower times. Neither was linked to more injuries: CORROBORATED (two sources agree in direction). These are associations, not dose prescriptions.
  • Three days per week. I could not search the FIRST / Run Less Run Faster programme (Furman). [K] It prescribes 3 quality runs plus 2 cross-training sessions, supported by small uncontrolled studies from the authors: ALLEGED. Montero & Lundby suggests that 1–3 sessions/week leaves some people not responding.
  • Cross-training substitution. Bushman et al. 1997 [S]: 4–6 weeks of deep-water running kept 5K time, VO2max and lactate threshold in trained runners. Small sample; the authors call for replication: CREDIBLE. Several sources agree that cycling and elliptical keep aerobic fitness but not impact tolerance [C/B]: CORROBORATED in direction only. Detraining figures in the secondary literature: 4–8 % VO2max loss after 2 weeks off and 15–20 % after 10–12 weeks: ALLEGED.
  • Per-distance minimums (e.g. "3 h/week for a marathon"): UNKNOWN. No study was found that defines them.

4. Goal setting

  • Improvement rate per year by level. No study was retrieved: UNKNOWN. A usable proxy is the change in age-grade %, which normalises for age and sex. The verifier should search the longitudinal masters data and the race-result databases (e.g. RunRepeat, Strava/Runalyze analyses).
  • How far a goal can stretch from current performance. Common coach rules, e.g. "≤ 2–5 % for trained, more for novices," were not found in any source during this run: UNKNOWN. Race-equivalence formulas (Riegel, VDOT) [K] tend to predict marathons too fast for low-volume runners. Tanda 2022 [S] predicts marathon time from weekly distance and mean training pace: CREDIBLE as a second feasibility check that accounts for volume.
  • First-marathon / first-ultra readiness criteria. Not searched. Practice criteria seen in plans [K]: about 6–12 months of consistent running, comfortable 10–15 km long run, and a prior half marathon before a marathon. For a first ultra: a marathon finish and a sustained base of about 50+ km/week: ALLEGED throughout.

5. Adherence and drop-out

  • Couch-to-5K. UK, n = 110, mean age 47, 82 % female, modified 9-week programme [S]. Only 27.3 % completed, 19 % were injured, and prior injury gave OR 7.56 (2.06–27.75) for new injury. Dropout interviews showed three themes: injury, negative emotions, and rigid programme design. The authors recommend injury-prevention advice and more flexible designs. Grade: CORROBORATED (the single study is indexed in several places, and NLstart2run independently names RRI as a main cause of dropout).
  • Plan design (flexibility, number of sessions, feedback). Beyond the above, no trial was retrieved: UNKNOWN. [K] Wider exercise-adherence literature: self-efficacy, social support and past behaviour predict adherence: ALLEGED here. Social support as a C25K factor appears only in a blog: ALLEGED.
  • Masters drop in training stimulus. Life demands and injury drive the drop (NSA 2015): CREDIBLE.

6. Life constraints

  • Sleep. Meta-analyses from 2022 (31 studies) and 2025 (45 studies; Frontiers Physiol, 10.3389/fphys.2025.1544286) [R] find that sleep loss impairs aerobic endurance and raises perceived exertion. The effect is larger for efforts over 30 minutes, and it is unclear for well-trained athletes: CORROBORATED.
  • Shift work. A 2025 systematic review of RCTs found evidence on exercise for shift workers is lacking. The advice to keep high intensity away from post-shift sleep comes only from practitioners: CREDIBLE for "no evidence", ALLEGED for the timing rules.
  • Stress / HRV. Some evidence suggests HRV-guided training improves endurance running performance more (Vesterinen 2016, cited second-hand) [S]: ALLEGED/CREDIBLE, to be verified. Aerobic training dampened exam-stress responses (RCT, n = 61; PubMed 26582310): CREDIBLE. Ruuska 2012 (stress reduces adaptation) was not retrieved: UNKNOWN.
  • Travel. Not searched: UNKNOWN.

Implications for the method

  1. Use the WMA age-grade % as the primary sex- and age-neutral performance level (bands 60/70/80/90) (CORROBORATED). Pair it with a separate tissue-tolerance level built from training age and recent volume (no validated composite exists, so this is a design choice and must be labelled one).
  2. Novice risk flags from NLstart2run should slow progression and trigger screening: no running history, higher BMI, older age, prior musculoskeletal complaints, prior injury (OR 7.56 in C25K) (CORROBORATED).
  3. Do not hard-code "10 %" or "30 %" as evidence-based thresholds. Any progression cap is a conservative default, not a validated limit (Nielsen 2014: CONTRADICTED as a firm threshold).
  4. Beginner plans must be flexible: repeatable weeks, no penalty for repeating, injury-prevention content. A 27 % completion rate is the baseline to beat (CORROBORATED).
  5. No menstrual-cycle phasing by default; offer optional symptom logging (CORROBORATED, low-quality evidence base).
  6. Postpartum: block plan generation before about 12 weeks and require self-attestation of pelvic-health clearance (CORROBORATED). Pregnancy: no performance plans (ALLEGED, needs verification).
  7. Before concluding "no response", raise frequency or time toward 4+ sessions or about 300 min/week, if time allows (CORROBORATED, single trial in young men).
  8. The goal-feasibility check should use volume-aware prediction (weekly km, longest run) alongside equivalence formulas (CORROBORATED for associations; the formula choice is a design default).
  9. When sleep is reduced, downgrade long or hard sessions, since endurance over 30 min is impaired (CORROBORATED). Shift-work timing rules are defaults only (ALLEGED).
  10. Cross-training may substitute for aerobic sessions, but impact tolerance must still be built by running. Deep-water running is the best-supported substitute during injury (CREDIBLE).

Still UNKNOWN

  • McKay tier cut-offs for running, and any validated composite level score.
  • Minimum effective weekly time or frequency per distance. The FIRST 3-day evidence was not searched.
  • Realistic yearly improvement rates by level, and a defensible maximum goal stretch.
  • First-marathon and first-ultra readiness criteria (practice only, not searched).
  • Age limits for minors by federation, and ACOG pregnancy guidance (not searched; knowledge only).
  • Plan-design factors (flexibility, number of sessions, feedback/app nudges) and their effect on adherence.
  • Effects of travel and psychological stress on adaptation (Ruuska 2012 not retrieved). Adjustment for other-sport background.
wg4-draft.md

WG4 draft: Safety and limits

Method note: 16 searches, then the search budget ran out. The proxy blocked PubMed, PMC and BMJ, so nothing is VERIFIED; the ceiling is CORROBORATED. "(memory)" means prior knowledge not re-found here. The verifier should open the sources marked *.

Source tags: [S] study, [R] review, [P] position stand, [F] federation guidance, [B] blog/press.

1. Injury epidemiology

  • Novices are injured more often per hour of running: 17.8 injuries per 1000 h (95% CI 16.7–19.1), against 7.7 (6.9–8.7) in recreational runners. *Videbæk 2015 Sports Med [R] doi:10.1007/s40279-015-0333-8. CORROBORATED. Ultra-runners: about 7.2 per 1000 h (memory). ALLEGED.
  • Most common diagnoses: medial tibial stress syndrome, Achilles tendinopathy, plantar fasciitis and patellofemoral pain, mostly at or below the knee. Lopes 2012 [R] doi:10.1007/BF03262301; a review of injuries by anatomical location [R]. The rank order varies between studies. CREDIBLE.
  • Race cardiac arrest: 0.54 per 100,000 overall; 1.01 in marathons, 0.27 in half marathons; 0.90 in men, 0.16 in women. 71% were fatal. Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy was the leading cause and the mean age was 42. *Kim 2012 NEJM [S] PMID 22236223; press [B]. CORROBORATED.

2. Risk factors

  • Previous injury in the last 12 months is the most consistent predictor (5 of 8 cohorts). Saragiotto 2014 [R] PMID 24809248; van Poppel 2021 [R]. CORROBORATED.
  • Novice status: CORROBORATED (above).
  • Prior use of orthotics: CREDIBLE as a marker of risk.
  • Sex: the reviews conflict (men at higher risk, or no difference). REDs and female sex add risk in collegiate cross-country runners. CONTRADICTED.
  • BMI and footwear type: results are inconsistent. UNKNOWN.
  • No single factor has been shown to cause a specific injury (van Poppel 2021). CORROBORATED.

3. Load progression

  • The 10% rule. In novices (n = 532), a graded 13-week programme at about 10% per week injured 20.8% of runners, against 20.3% on a standard 8-week programme (p = .90). Buist 2008 AJSM [S]; PEDro record; ACU repository. CORROBORATED. The control arm was itself gradual (23.7% against 10.5% per week), so a 10% cap was no better than a moderate progression. That does not make rapid progression safe.
  • Weekly increases above 30% were linked to distance-related injury in novices (Nielsen 2014 JOSPT, small sample, memory). ALLEGED.
  • Progressing volume and progressing intensity gave equal injury risk over 16 weeks (n = 447). Run Clever, Ramskov 2018 BMJ Open SEM [S]; RunSafe [B]. CORROBORATED.
  • Single-session spikes. *Frandsen, Nielsen et al. 2025 BJSM, doi:10.1136/bjsports-2024-109380: 5,205 runners in 87 countries, followed for about 18 months. A run longer than the longest run of the previous 30 days predicted overuse injury better than weekly-volume change or ACWR-type metrics, and risk rose with the size of the spike. A spike of about 10% or more raised the hazard by about 60%, and a spike above 100% by about 128%. Press coverage [B]. CORROBORATED for the direction and for the 30-day reference. ALLEGED for the exact hazard ratios and cut-points, because secondary reports differ. The study is observational and uses GPS distance only.
  • ACWR (acute:chronic workload ratio). The IOC endorsed it in 2016.
  • Lolli et al. showed mathematical coupling and proposed an "uncoupled" ratio.
  • Impellizzeri et al. found that ACWR assumes causation that no study has estimated (IJSPP 2020; J Athl Train 2020, PMC7534938). In Sports Med 2021 they showed that ratios built with made-up chronic loads predicted injury just as well, and called for ACWR to be dismissed.
  • Coyne et al. found only trivial differences between the coupled and uncoupled versions (g = 0.04–0.21).
  • CORROBORATED that ACWR is contested. UNKNOWN whether any threshold (for example "0.8–1.3") protects against injury.

4. Pre-participation screening

  • ACSM 2015 algorithm (Riebe, MSSE 47:2473). It asks three things: is the person currently active (structured exercise in the past 3 months)? Do they have known cardiovascular, metabolic or renal disease, or symptoms of it? What intensity do they intend to train at? It dropped risk-factor counting and cut referrals by about 41%, but has not been validated. ACSM blog [B]. CORROBORATED.
  • PAR-Q+ and ePARmed-X+ form the standard self-screen. Not searched this run. CREDIBLE (memory).
  • ESC 2020 sports cardiology guideline: for those over 35, risk assessment, plus exercise testing if the person is at higher risk (memory). ALLEGED. ECG criteria validated for athletes ≤35 may not apply to masters athletes (Eur J Prev Cardiol 2020 [S]). CORROBORATED.

5. Red flags: stop and seek care

  • Cardiac: chest pain or pressure, fainting or near-fainting, palpitations, or breathlessness out of proportion to effort, especially after a viral illness. Symptoms trigger referral under ACSM 2015; chest pain on return to training means a cardiac work-up (ACC 2021 COVID consensus). CORROBORATED.
  • Bone stress injury: focal bone pain, pain that appears earlier in each run, pain at rest or at night, pain on hopping. High-risk sites are the anterior tibia, femoral neck, navicular and 5th metatarsal. Return to sport takes about 127 days at the navicular and about 107 days at the femoral neck. A normal X-ray does not rule it out. *Hoenig 2023 [R] (76 studies); Warden clinic pages [B]; AMSSM case. CORROBORATED for sites and timelines. CREDIBLE for the symptom list.
  • Neck check: the rule was never tested. A 2022 Sports Med review says it should be abandoned, and a South African review found no data. Fever or systemic symptoms mean rest. CORROBORATED that the rule is unvalidated.
  • Exertional heat stroke: hyperthermia with confusion or collapse. Stop, then give whole-body cold-water immersion within 30 minutes: cool first, transport second. *Roberts 2021 ACSM consensus, CSMR 20:470 [P]; JAT 2023 [S]; NAEMSP [B]. CORROBORATED.
  • Exercise-associated hyponatremia (EAH): drink according to thirst; overdrinking is the cause. Symptoms are headache, vomiting, confusion and seizures (memory). *Hew-Butler 2015 BJSM 49:1432 [P]; press [B]. CORROBORATED for drinking to thirst. CREDIBLE for the symptoms.
  • REDs: the IOC 2023 consensus treats low energy availability as a spectrum. Its CAT2 tool uses green, yellow, orange and red categories, and diagnosis requires a physician. Flags include menstrual disruption, recurrent bone stress injury, weight loss and falling performance. CORROBORATED for the framework. CREDIBLE for the flags.
  • Overtraining syndrome (OTS): a long-lasting performance drop despite rest, with fatigue and mood change. It is diagnosed by excluding other causes, and no biomarker is valid. Meeusen 2013 ECSS/ACSM [P]. CORROBORATED.

6. Return to run

  • After injury: pain-free walking, then cross-training, then a graded walk–run, progressing only while the bone site stays pain-free. High-risk sites need clinical clearance (Warden [B]; Hoenig 2023). CREDIBLE.
  • After illness or COVID: a staged return (Elliott 2020 BJSM): rest, then light activity under 70% HRmax for 15 minutes, then stepped stages. Cardiac assessment is needed if symptoms appear (ACC 2021; ESC 2025 on myocarditis). CORROBORATED for the staged approach. ALLEGED for the day counts.
  • Postpartum: at least 12 weeks of progressive low-impact rehabilitation before running, plus a pelvic-health assessment. Goom, Donnelly and Brockwell 2019 [F]; NHS Wales [F]; a survey of 881 women (mean return at 12 weeks). This is expert opinion. CORROBORATED.
  • Pregnancy: women who ran before pregnancy may continue with clinician approval (ACOG, memory). ALLEGED.

7. Special populations

  • Minors:
  • Run Canada: marathon at 18 or older, and 10K or more only after puberty.
  • UK Athletics (Scottish Athletics 2026 table): up to 25 km at age 17, the marathon from U20.
  • A 2021 BJSM consensus calls these limits opinion-based.
  • CORROBORATED as a federation norm. UNKNOWN as evidence.
  • Older runners and chronic disease: see the masters ECG caveat and the ACSM rule. Known disease or symptoms means clearance before vigorous training. CORROBORATED.

8. Legal and ethical framing

No diagnosis. Show a disclaimer, require screening first, record acceptance, and refer rather than adapt on red flags. Suggesting treatment may trigger medical-device rules such as the EU MDR (memory). ALLEGED; get legal review.

Implications for the method

  1. Screen every user (ACSM 2015 and PAR-Q+ logic). Any symptom or known disease blocks vigorous sessions until clearance is confirmed. (CORROBORATED)
  2. Make the single-session cap the primary load rule. Long run ≤ longest run of the last 30 days + 10% by default. Warn above 30%. (direction CORROBORATED; thresholds ALLEGED)
  3. Keep weekly progression around 10% as a labelled convention. For novices, hard-block weekly increases above 30%. (CORROBORATED / ALLEGED)
  4. Never use ACWR as a gate. Show it for information only, if at all. (CORROBORATED)
  5. Slower progression profile for an injury in the last 12 months or novice status. (CORROBORATED)
  6. Red-flag engine: cardiac, bone-stress, heat-stroke and EAH signs always output "stop and see a professional". (CORROBORATED)
  7. Replace the neck check: fever or systemic symptoms mean no training, followed by a staged return. (CORROBORATED)
  8. Minors: no marathon or ultra under 18, and no race of 10K or more before puberty. Refer users under about 16. (CORROBORATED norm)
  9. Postpartum: no running plan before 12 weeks, and a pelvic-health check is advised. (CORROBORATED, expert level)
  10. REDs and OTS prompts: after repeated performance drops, menstrual change or a recurrent bone stress injury, refer the user. (CORROBORATED)

Still UNKNOWN

  • The exact cut-points in Frandsen/Nielsen 2025, and whether they hold for elite runners and ultra-runners.
  • Whether a weekly progression cap protects against injury.
  • Injury rates and long-run limits in 50–100 km road ultras.
  • Exact wording of ESC/AHA screening for over-35s, current PAR-Q+ items, ACOG guidance (not retrieved).
  • Day counts for return after illness, and the regulatory status (EU MDR or FDA) of a plan generator.
wg5-draft.md

WG5 draft: Nutrition and fuelling

Researcher draft, 20 Oct 2026. Evidence ceiling: CORROBORATED. No primary source could be opened. The session's egress proxy refused PubMed, PMC and Dietitians of Canada, and WebFetch could not resolve those hosts. Grades rest on search results that quote abstracts, PMC records and position-stand summaries. The web-search budget ran out after 16 queries, so three planned queries were not run (120 vs 90 g/h oxidation, critiques of the LEA threshold, and IOC heat consensus). Source types: [PS] position stand or consensus, [R] review or meta-analysis, [S] study, [C] coach or practice, [B] blog or press.

1. Daily energy, carbohydrate and protein

  • Carbohydrate should scale with training load. The range is about 3–10 g/kg/day, and up to 12 g/kg/day for extreme or prolonged training. CORROBORATED. Source: Thomas, Erdman & Burke 2016, joint ACSM/AND/DC position [PS] (dietitians.ca noap-position-paper.pdf; the search found secondary summaries agreeing). The commonly quoted bands are below. Their exact boundaries are CREDIBLE because the full text was not opened.
  • light/skill training: 3–5 g/kg
  • about 1 h/day moderate training: 5–7 g/kg
  • 1–3 h/day: 6–10 g/kg
  • more than 4–5 h/day: 8–12 g/kg
  • Carbohydrate loading: 10–12 g/kg/day for 36–48 h before events longer than about 90 min. CORROBORATED for the guideline and for the rise in muscle glycogen. The size of the race-time benefit for trained marathoners is CONTRADICTED / UNKNOWN. Secondary sources dispute it, and a large load can trigger GI distress [B: marathonhandbook; R: PMC10164509]. Well-trained runners do not need a depletion phase. CREDIBLE.
  • Protein: 1.2–2.0 g/kg/day. CORROBORATED. Sources: ISSN, as summarised [PS]; Thomas 2016 [PS]. A 2025 review argues for about 1.8 g/kg/day, and more than 2.0 g/kg/day during carbohydrate-restricted or low-EA training [R, PMC12152099]. That higher target is ALLEGED (one source). Per-meal dose: about 0.3 g/kg is the figure widely attributed to Thomas 2016. Searching did not confirm it, and the 2025 review gives about 0.5 g/kg after exercise. Status: UNKNOWN pending verification.

Periodised carbohydrate ("train low", "sleep low", "fuel for the work required")

  • Mechanism: starting selected sessions with low glycogen raises cell-signalling and gene-expression markers [R: Impey 2018, Sports Med, s40279-018-0867-7]. CORROBORATED.
  • Performance: Gejl & Nybo 2021 meta-analysis [R, JISSN, doi 10.1186/s12970-021-00435-3] pooled 9 studies of trained athletes. The effect on performance was SMD 0.17 (95% CI −0.15 to 0.49; p = 0.29), which is not a benefit. CORROBORATED.
  • Risks:
  • Chronic restriction lowers training quality, particularly peak interval intensity. CORROBORATED [R].
  • In elite race walkers, a ketogenic low-carbohydrate high-fat diet worsened exercise economy and removed the performance gain from training. Sources: Burke 2017, J Physiol, doi 10.1113/JP273230 [S], replicated in PLoS ONE 2020 [S]. Both studies were small and not randomised. CORROBORATED.
  • Restriction also overlaps with the REDs risk described in section 2. CREDIBLE.

2. Low energy availability and REDs

  • Reference: IOC 2023 consensus on REDs, Mountjoy et al., Br J Sports Med 57:1073–97, doi 10.1136/bjsports-2023-106994, PMID 37752011 [PS]. CORROBORATED.
  • LEA lies on a spectrum from adaptable (short-term, quickly reversed) to problematic (sustained, with health or performance effects).
  • The consensus introduces CAT2, a three-step process (screen → severity/risk assessment → clinical diagnosis). It grades athletes on a green-to-red traffic light using primary and secondary indicators.
  • CAT2 needs clinicians and objective measurements. It is not a self-assessment tool. CORROBORATED [S: Frontiers 2023 fspor.2023.1254210].
  • Prevalence in runners:
  • About 25% of male middle- and long-distance runners and race walkers had LEA across the cross-sectional studies in a 10-study systematic review (2020, Univ. Limerick) [R].
  • Screening studies of female track and field athletes found 49–83% at "high risk" (n = 392 and n = 12) [S].
  • Mountjoy reports 15–80% across sports [B/press].
  • Grade: CORROBORATED that LEA is common. Any single pooled figure is UNKNOWN, because estimates depend on the tool and cutoff and most samples are small cross-sectional ones.
  • Warning signs that are relevant to a self-report tool. CORROBORATED [PS, R]:
  • missed, irregular or absent periods, or none since starting training (the most-used marker; LEAF-Q weights it heavily, and a score of 8 or more means at risk)
  • a history of bone stress injury, or repeated ones
  • unintended weight loss
  • persistent fatigue or underperformance despite rest
  • frequent illness
  • low libido or loss of morning erections in men (LEAM-Q)
  • restrictive eating or eating-disorder behaviours
  • Questionnaires only screen. They do not diagnose. LEAF-Q is validated for women only.

3. Race and long-run fuelling by duration

DurationCarbohydrate during the raceGradeSources
< 45 min (5K, most 10Ks)none neededCORROBORATEDACSM bands as summarised [R, s40279-022-01658-3]; Jeukendrup 2014 [R, PMC4008807]
45–75 minmouth rinse or small amountsCORROBORATED (benefit of the rinse while running is mixed: two studies, one positive, one null)same
1–2.5 h (half marathon, fast marathon)30–60 g/hCORROBORATEDThomas 2016 [PS]; Jeukendrup 2014 [R]
> 2.5–3 h (most marathoners, 50–100 km)up to 90 g/h, from multiple transportable carbohydrates (glucose:fructose)CORROBORATEDJeukendrup [R, GSSI SSE 108; PMC4008807]; Thomas 2016 [PS]
very long or eliteup to 120 g/hALLEGED for performance, CREDIBLE for tolerance in trained athletessee notes below

Notes on the table:

  • One carbohydrate type vs two. A single type (glucose) is oxidised at up to about 60 g/h. Adding fructose raises oxidation by up to about 65%. The guidance on absolute intake is not scaled to body mass. CORROBORATED, but mostly from Jeukendrup's own group, so it is not fully independent. Targets may be lower when absolute intensity is low (slow runners) [R, GSSI].
  • 120 g/h in a mountain marathon (Viribay 2020; Urdampilleta 2020, Nutrients) [S]. Compared with 60 or 90 g/h, 120 g/h gave lower markers of muscle damage and better recovery 24 h later. These were small elite trail samples (n ≈ 20–26) and the outcomes were not race time.
  • Delivery format. Fluid, gel, chew and mixed formats gave similar oxidation at 120 g/h (2022) [S]. ALLEGED (one source).
  • Ultra practice.
  • Ultrarunners take in about 25–71 g/h during competition, and faster runners and finishers eat more (Stellingwerff 2016, cited in Frontiers 2021) [R]. CORROBORATED.
  • Nausea and vomiting are a main reason 100-mile non-finishers give for dropping out [S]. CREDIBLE.
  • Serious GI symptoms: about 4% of marathoners vs about 31% of Ironman athletes, and higher carbohydrate intake went with more nausea in that dataset (Pfeiffer 2012) [S]. CREDIBLE.
  • Caffeine: 3–6 mg/kg about 60 min before, or during via gel or gum. Aerobic endurance shows the most consistent benefit, but individual responses vary. CORROBORATED. Sources: ISSN position stand, Guest 2021, doi 10.1186/s12970-020-00383-4 [PS], plus press summaries. Side effects (sleep, GI, anxiety) and medical contraindications argue for a conservative default and a rehearsal requirement. CREDIBLE.

4. Hydration and hyponatremia

  • Drink to thirst vs planned drinking (a real disagreement):
  • Thirst side: The Hew-Butler 2015 EAH consensus (Clin J Sport Med; BJSM 49:1432) [PS] recommends drinking to thirst to avoid overdrinking, which is the main cause of exercise-associated hyponatremia (EAH). It also says treatment should follow symptoms, with 3% hypertonic saline for severe cases.
  • Supporting meta-analyses: Goulet 2013 [R, BJSM 47:679] found that losses up to about 4% of body mass did not impair self-paced time-trial performance. A 2% loss impaired only fixed-intensity lab protocols (−1.91%).
  • Planned-drinking side: ACSM (2007, and its president in 2015) holds that thirst alone may be inadequate and advises keeping losses under 2% of body mass. Reviews still describe 2% as the consensus threshold.
  • Conflicts of interest: Critics point to sports-drink sponsorship of ACSM. The 2015 conference was funded by CrossFit, which had no editorial role.
  • Grade: CORROBORATED that the two positions exist. Which is better for slow recreational marathoners in heat is UNKNOWN.
  • EAH risk factors: weight gain during the race, more than 4 h of exercise, slow pace, female sex, low body mass, drinking more than about 1.5 L/h, hyperhydrating beforehand, NSAID use, and extreme temperatures. CORROBORATED [S: 100 km study 2010; Boston data].
  • Gaining 3–4.9 kg during a marathon raised the risk of hyponatremia (sodium below 135 / below 130 mmol/L) by 30% and 70% respectively.
  • Incidence at 161 km races: 15% (range 4.6–51% by year), rising with temperature [S].
  • Sweat rate: the best field method is body mass before and after a run (correcting for fluid drunk and urine passed) [R, Baker 2017, PubMed 28332116]. CORROBORATED.
  • Rates vary widely: about 0.2–1.4 L/h in runners, men about 1.3 vs women about 0.9 L/h in heat. CORROBORATED.
  • Sweat sodium measurements depend heavily on method. CORROBORATED.
  • Sodium: modelling suggests targeted sodium replacement is unnecessary for marathons. It may matter in 160 km events, but only when sweat sodium is at least 40 mmol/L and fluid replacement exceeds 80% of losses [S, PubMed 35616504]. ALLEGED (one modelling study). Body weight on a scale does not show electrolyte status [R]. CREDIBLE.

5. Fuelling as a trainable competence

  • Evidence:
  • Costa 2017 (APNM 42; n = 25) [S]: runners did a 2 h run at 60% VO2max taking 30 g carbohydrate every 20 min (90 g/h), then a 1 h distance test. After 2 weeks of daily gut challenges, GI symptoms fell and performance improved. Only the glucose+sucrose form also reduced malabsorption.
  • Miall 2018 (n = 18) [S] replicated the symptom reduction.
  • King 2022 [S] found only minor effects in highly trained athletes.
  • Grade: CORROBORATED that gut training improves tolerance and symptoms. ALLEGED that it raises carbohydrate oxidation. Press figures (−60% symptoms, +5% performance) are [B].
  • What coaches do in practice [C, two or more independent sources; grade CREDIBLE]:
  • start 8–12 weeks out at about 30 g/h (one gel on long runs)
  • add 10–15 g/h every one or two long runs or weeks, rehearsing on long runs and race-pace sessions
  • target at least 60 g/h for a marathon, or 80–100 g/h for those who tolerate it
  • increase fluid alongside carbohydrate
  • back off if symptoms are severe
  • Sources: Precision Hydration / Reed Fischer; RunnersConnect; Marathon Handbook; Campus Coach.
  • "Nothing new on race day": rehearse the exact products, caffeine and drinking schedule in at least two or three race-specific long runs. CREDIBLE (practice consensus; no trial).

6. Scope limits for an automated tool

  • Safe for the tool to give:
  • general ranges from position stands
  • in-race carbohydrate targets by duration
  • gut-training progressions
  • sweat-rate self-test instructions
  • EAH education ("don't drink to gain weight")
  • These are based on [PS] guidance and are CORROBORATED as consensus content.
  • Must refer to a professional:
  • Positive LEA/REDs screen, eating disorder or disordered eating, amenorrhoea, or repeated bone stress injuries → sports physician plus registered sports dietitian. The AIS/NEDC framework says to work "up to, but not beyond" your scope and to refer through a multidisciplinary team [PS/practice]. CORROBORATED.
  • Diabetes, coeliac or IBD, kidney or heart conditions, pregnancy, medicines that affect fluid or sodium balance, and minors.
  • Weight-loss goals combined with high training load.
  • Grade for these other medical categories: CREDIBLE. They follow from position-stand scope statements, but no single source lists them.
  • The tool should not give calorie deficits, weight targets or "race weight" advice, should not prescribe ketogenic or LCHF diets, and should not prescribe train-low sessions by default. CREDIBLE, inferred from the REDs and LCHF evidence above.

Implications for the method

  1. Daily carbohydrate guidance: add a load-based daily carbohydrate band (3–5 / 5–7 / 6–10 / 8–12 g/kg) shown as information, keyed to planned weekly hours (CORROBORATED range; band edges CREDIBLE). The tool should not compute meal plans.
  2. Race fuelling rule by expected duration:
  • under 45 min → none
  • 45–75 min → optional small amounts
  • 1–2.5 h → 30–60 g/h
  • over 2.5 h → 60–90 g/h with glucose+fructose
  • Default ceiling 90 g/h. Allow 120 g/h only if the user has logged tolerance of 90 g/h or more in training (CORROBORATED / ALLEGED).
  1. Gut-training module: make "Fuelling" a competence node, needed for half marathons over 90 min, marathons and ultras.
  • Progression: +10–15 g/h every one or two long runs, from 30 g/h to the target, starting 8–12 weeks out.
  • Exit test: two long runs at target intake and race-like pace with GI score ≤ 2/10 (CREDIBLE practice; tolerance effect CORROBORATED).
  • Rule: if the plan is too short to reach the target, cap the race-day intake at the highest level tolerated in training.
  1. Hydration default: drink to thirst, with an optional planned schedule based on a sweat-rate test. Hard rule: never plan intake above measured sweat rate or above about 800 mL/h without a test. Always warn about EAH for events over 4 h (CORROBORATED; the school disagreement becomes a user parameter).
  2. REDs screen: at onboarding and every 8–12 weeks, ask a short set of LEAF-Q/LEAM-Q-style questions (periods, bone stress injury history, unexplained weight loss, fatigue, illness). Any positive answer → "see a professional" flag and progression frozen (CORROBORATED as warning signs; the tool cannot diagnose).
  3. Train-low: not a default. If offered at all, it is an advanced, opt-in option, never on quality sessions, and blocked when the REDs flag is on (meta-analysis shows no benefit, CORROBORATED).
  4. Caffeine: optional, 3 mg/kg default (upper limit 6 mg/kg). It must be rehearsed. Exclude users who declare cardiac, pregnancy or anxiety contraindications (CORROBORATED dose; exclusion list CREDIBLE).
  5. Scope disclaimer and referral triggers: use the triggers from section 6 as deterministic rules (CORROBORATED / CREDIBLE).

Still UNKNOWN

  • The verified wording and band boundaries of Thomas 2016 (per-meal protein of 0.3 g/kg, exact carbohydrate bands). These need opening the primary paper.
  • Whether 120 g/h improves race times in road marathons or 50–100 km events compared with 90 g/h.
  • The best minimum gut-training duration and how long the adaptation lasts after training stops.
  • A pooled prevalence of LEA/REDs in recreational road runners, especially men and masters runners.
  • Drinking to thirst vs planned drinking for slow recreational runners in heat; the IOC heat consensus was not searched.
  • Whether loading at 10–12 g/kg adds race-time benefit over a moderate high-carbohydrate diet in trained runners.
  • Individual sodium needs in 50–100 km road events (evidence is limited to modelling).

Expert panel & hearings

experts.md

Expert panel

Rules (binding): experts INFORM, never recommend; ≥2 independent sources per claim (single-source = ALLEGED); state strength of evidence (consensus / majority view / contested / emerging / unknown); state what your source base cannot see; answer independently.

Source bases:

  • acad — academic & official: peer-reviewed studies, meta-analyses, systematic reviews, position stands (ACSM, IOC, ISSN…), official guidelines.
  • field — practice & field: published methods of established coaches, coaching federations' education material, practitioner books and guides, practitioner-run data, experienced coaches' documented practice.
  • crit — critical & independent: replication studies, methodological critiques, independent reviewers, evidence-based debunking, dissenting researchers.

Domain 1: Exercise physiology & training science (slug: phys)

Opening questions:

  1. Which physiological determinants matter at 5K, 10K, half, marathon and 50–100 km road, and how does their relative weight shift with distance?
  2. Intensity distribution (polarized, pyramidal, threshold-heavy): what does the evidence show, for whom (level, volume), and how settled is it?
  3. Training-load models and progression (volume, intensity, density; ACWR, monotony, Banister fitness-fatigue): which are usable for plan generation and what are their limits?
  4. Intensity anchoring: HR zones, pace zones, RPE, lactate/critical speed — which are robust for self-coached runners?
  5. Taper, recovery, deload frequency and periodization models (linear, block, undulating): what is established?

Domain 2: Coaching practice 5K → 100K road (slug: coach)

  1. How do established methods (Daniels, Pfitzinger, Hansons, Lydiard, Canova, Norwegian/double-threshold, Higdon, Galloway, ultra coaches such as Koop and others) structure plans per distance? Common core vs real differences.
  2. How do coaches set weekly volume, long-run length and key sessions per distance and level, and how do they progress them?
  3. How do coaches adapt plans to limited time (3–4 h/week), very short or very long horizons, and multi-season/multi-year development?
  4. How do coaches adapt when sessions are missed, after illness, or when a race result differs from the goal?
  5. What do coaches consider the main failure modes of generic/online plans?

Domain 3: Sports medicine & injury prevention (slug: med)

  1. What is known about running injury risk factors (load changes, prior injury, volume, sex, age, biomechanics, RED-S/LEA)? Strength of evidence?
  2. Are there evidence-based load progression limits (e.g. the "10% rule", session-based spikes, ACWR) — what holds, what doesn't?
  3. Pre-participation screening for self-coached runners (PAR-Q+, cardiac red flags, age, pregnancy/postpartum, chronic conditions): what is recommended?
  4. Red flags during training that should stop training or trigger referral (pain patterns, bone stress, cardiac symptoms, illness "neck check", heat).
  5. Return to running after injury or illness: established graded protocols?

Domain 4: Nutrition & fuelling (slug: nutr)

  1. Daily energy and carbohydrate needs by training load; RED-S / low energy availability risks and warning signs.
  2. Race and long-run fuelling by distance (carbohydrate per hour, gut training, fluids, sodium) — evidence and practice for marathon and 50–100 km road.
  3. Hydration strategies (thirst vs planned drinking), hyponatremia risk, heat.
  4. Which fuelling competences should be trained progressively, and how?
  5. What nutrition guidance can safely be given by an automated tool vs needs a professional?

Domain 5: Knowledge modelling & adaptive systems (slug: sys)

  1. How can training knowledge be formalised for automatic plan generation: rule-based expert systems, constraint satisfaction/optimisation, competence/skill graphs, ontologies? Trade-offs.
  2. How do existing adaptive training-plan products (e.g. Runna, TrainAsONE, Garmin Coach, TrainingPeaks-based tools, academic prototypes) generate and adapt plans, as far as publicly documented?
  3. What is known about the effectiveness and safety of algorithmic/AI-generated training plans (evaluations, user outcomes, injury, adherence)?
  4. How to make a rule set deterministic, conflict-free, explainable and testable (rule priorities, conflict resolution, test suites, simulation)?
  5. What data model is typical (athlete profile, goals, sessions, phases, load, events) and what interoperability standards exist?
hearing-1.md

Hearing 1: synthesis (5 domains × 3 source bases)

Read first. All 15 hearings ran degraded. The shared search budget ran out after 2–9 queries each, and every primary-source fetch failed (DNS or proxy). No claim in hearing 1 is VERIFIED. Best grade is CORROBORATED (two search abstracts agree); many field "two sources" echo one origin. Disagreements are shown, not resolved. Grades: COR / CRED / ALL / CONTRA / UNK.

GAP LIST FOR THE FACT DESK

Each needs two independent sources, primary opened, before any rule uses it. Ordered by importance.

  1. Does Frandsen et al. 2025 (BJSM, doi:10.1136/bjsports-2024-109380, Garmin-RUNSAFE) define the single-session spike as distance >10% above the longest run of the previous 30 days, what are the exact bin cut-offs and hazard ratios, and has any independent cohort replicated it?
  2. In Nielsen et al. 2014 (JOSPT 44:739, DANO-RUN), what weekly-volume progression (% over 2 weeks) was linked to which injury types, with what hazard ratio?
  3. What did Buist et al. 2008 (AJSM 36:33, GRONORUN) report as injury incidence per arm, and how gradual was the control programme?
  4. What decision inputs and referral rules does the current ACSM pre-participation algorithm (Riebe 2015) plus PAR-Q+ use for starting vigorous running?
  5. Which exertional cardiac symptoms do current ACSM/ESC/AHA guidance list as stop-and-refer criteria, and what race cardiac-arrest incidence did Kim 2012 (NEJM) and the 2025 RACER update report?
  6. Which bone stress injury signs and high-risk sites, and which return-to-run timelines, does Warden et al. 2021 (BJSM) give, and does a second guideline agree?
  7. What minimum symptom-free period and stage length does Elliott 2020 (BJSM) or another guideline set for return after viral illness, and is the "neck check" endorsed by any official body?
  8. What pain-score thresholds (≤2/10, ≤5/10, next-morning rule) does the Silbernagel/Thomeé pain-monitoring model actually specify, in the primary paper?
  9. What return-to-running timing and screening tests does Goom, Donnelly & Brockwell 2019 set postpartum, and what does ACOG 804 (2020) recommend during pregnancy?
  10. What warning signs and referral criteria does the IOC 2023 REDs consensus (Mountjoy, BJSM 57:1073) list for self-screening, and does it retain any energy-availability threshold?
  11. What drinking guidance and EAH risk factors does the 2015 EAH consensus (Hew-Butler) give, and which risk factors did Almond 2005 (NEJM) quantify?
  12. What WBGT thresholds do ACSM (Roberts 2023) set for modifying or cancelling running training and races?
  13. What carbohydrate-per-hour targets by event duration do ACSM/AND/DC 2016 and the ISSN ultra stand (Tiller 2019) give, and what fluid/sodium numbers does Tiller 2019 state?
  14. Does any controlled evidence (Smyth & Lawlor 2021, Bosquet 2007 or a running-specific trial) support a specific taper length and volume cut for marathon and half?
  15. Is there any controlled or cohort evidence for deload frequency (3:1 vs 2:1 vs none) in endurance runners?
  16. What did the 2025 Rosenblat/Seiler IPD network meta-analysis (Sports Med 55:655) find for polarized vs pyramidal vs threshold, by athlete level and study duration?
  17. How accurately do critical-speed estimates from 2–3 race results or VDOT tables predict threshold and race pace in recreational runners, versus %HRmax zones?
  18. What exact volume and pace reductions does Daniels' Running Formula prescribe after 5, 6–28 and >28 days off, and does detraining evidence for runners support them?
  19. What long-run caps (Daniels 25–30% / 150 min; Hansons 16 mi; Pfitzinger 20 mi) do the primary books state, and is there any injury or performance evidence for long-run length relative to weekly volume?
  20. What is known, from any controlled source, about injury and adherence outcomes of adaptive app plans versus static plans (Runna/Loughborough review, TU/e app RCT n=425, NCT06538363)?
hearing-1-coach-acad.md

Hearing 1 — Coaching practice 5K→100K road — source base: ACADEMIC & OFFICIAL

Witness informs only; no recommendation, no school preferred. Process limits (read first): 6 WebSearch queries were run before the shared search budget ran out. Fetching primary sources failed (DNS errors on NCBI, Springer, Frontiers). No claim below is VERIFIED. Grades rest on abstracts and repository records. Rule: a single-origin claim = ALLEGED.

Q1. How established methods structure plans per distance

  • Named methods (Daniels, Pfitzinger, Hansons, Lydiard, Canova, Norwegian, Higdon, Galloway, Koop) have almost no peer-reviewed outcome trials. The academic base describes them but does not test them. Evidence: unknown. Grade: UNKNOWN. The field base will see this better.
  • Common core, elite: the integrated review of world-class runners reports 11–14 sessions/week and ≥80 % of running volume at low intensity across the year. Race-pace volume rises as the main race nears. Grade: CORROBORATED. The same low-intensity majority shows up in a separate analysis of 92 sub-elite marathon plans (Knopp et al., 2024). Evidence: majority view. Haugen et al. 2022, Sports Med Open 8:18, doi:10.1186/s40798-022-00438-7, integrative review; Sports Med Open 2024, doi:10.1186/s40798-024-00717-5, systematic plan analysis
  • Real difference by distance (elite): marathoners ran about 160–220 km/week in mid-preparation, against 130–190 km for 5K/10K runners. The gap comes mostly from longer sessions. Marathoners race 6 ± 2 times a year, track runners 9 ± 3. Grade: ALLEGED (single review, abstract). Note: the review mixes science with results-proven practice by design.
  • 50–100 km road: training volume predicted 100 km time more than training pace did. For the marathon the order was reversed. Grade: ALLEGED (one research group; small pooled cohorts). Tanda & Knechtle 2015, OAJSM 6:129, cohort. Marathon PB predicts 100 km time (Rüst et al. 2012, J Sports Sci). Longest training run predicts 24 h performance (Knechtle et al. 2011). Both come from the same group, so they are not independent: ALLEGED. No systematic review of ultra-road training structure was found. Evidence: emerging.

Q2. Volume, long run and key sessions per level, and their progression

  • Sub-elite marathon plans (92 plans): mean weekly volume was about 43 / 59 / 108 km in low / middle / high-volume plans. Long runs were about 31 km in low-volume plans and about 35 km in high-volume plans. Grade: ALLEGED (one study; these are prescriptions, not outcomes). Sports Med Open 2024
  • Dose-response: a 2020 meta-analysis with meta-regression tested whether marathon training behaviours relate to performance. I could not retrieve its effect sizes. Grade: UNKNOWN. PubMed 31704026. One review of recreational runners reports VO2max and time-trial gains with volume increases of up to 42 % in the preparatory phase. Grade: ALLEGED. This figure must not be read as a safe weekly progression rate (that question belongs to the med domain). JHSE review
  • Progression rules as coaches use them: no peer-reviewed source on this was retrieved. Grade: UNKNOWN.

Q3. Limited time, short or long horizons, multi-year development

  • Reduced training: endurance is better protected by keeping frequency above about 80 % of normal and cutting volume than by stopping. Grade: CORROBORATED (Mujika & Padilla 2000–01 reviews, plus an independent 2023 systematic review). Evidence: majority view, mostly from older studies of highly trained athletes. Frontiers Physiol 2023, doi:10.3389/fphys.2023.1334766; PMC9398774
  • 3–4 h/week plans, very short horizons, multi-year development of non-elites: no academic source was retrieved. Grade: UNKNOWN. Elite multi-year data (altitude, years of build-up) are descriptive only (Haugen 2022). Grade: ALLEGED.

Q4. Missed sessions, illness, race result off target

  • Detraining time-course (trained athletes): VO2max falls about 4–14 % after ≤4 weeks off and about 6–20 % after >4 weeks off. Early losses come from blood and plasma volume. Muscle oxidative and capillary losses appear after about 2–8 weeks. Recently gained VO2max is lost completely after a long break. Grade: CORROBORATED (two independent reviews). Evidence: majority view. The reviews themselves call endurance-specific data scarce.
  • Rebuild time: one master cyclist lost 26 % VO2max after 4 weeks off and regained it in 6 weeks (peak power in 11). Grade: ALLEGED (n=1). Frontiers Physiol 2024. Retraining data for runners: unknown.
  • Adjusting a plan after a race result differs from the goal: no academic source was found. Grade: UNKNOWN.

Taper (cross-cutting, Q1/Q2)

  • Elite: taper starts about 7–10 days before the main race. Grade: ALLEGED (Haugen).
  • Recreational marathon (Strava, n = 158,117): longer tapers with a steadier volume cut were associated with faster times. Secondary reports put the gain at about 2.6 % (about 5–6 min) for a strict 3-week taper. This is observational and the figure is not checked against the paper. Grade: ALLEGED. Smyth & Lawlor 2021, Front Sports Act Living 3:735220. The two findings cover different populations; they do not contradict each other. A classic taper meta-analysis (Bosquet 2007) could not be retrieved: UNKNOWN this session.

Q5. Failure modes of generic/online plans

  • No academic source was retrieved. Grade: UNKNOWN. The field and crit bases are better placed to answer.

What this base cannot see

  • Coaches' actual decision rules, including how they adapt plans. These are mostly unpublished.
  • Outcome comparisons between named methods.
  • Data on recreational ultra-road runners.
  • Female and masters-specific coaching data.
  • Adherence to self-guided plans.

The academic literature overweights elites and small cohorts. Plan-content studies describe what plans prescribe, not whether they work.

hearing-1-coach-crit.md

Hearing 1 — Coaching practice 5K→100K road — CRITICAL & INDEPENDENT base (coach-crit)

Process note (read first). 6 of the planned searches ran (1 errored, 4 more were blocked because the shared web-search budget was used up). The egress proxy refused PubMed, PMC, Springer and MDPI, so no primary source could be opened: nothing below is VERIFIED. Grades rest on search-result abstracts and summaries. Q3, Q4 and the ultra half of Q2 are mostly UNKNOWN for this base in this hearing. A rerun with fetch access is needed before any of this justifies a rule.

Q1. How established methods differ: what critics say

  • No head-to-head trials of named plans exist (Daniels / Pfitzinger / Hansons / Lydiard / Higdon / Galloway). Reviews say a controlled trial of a 3-month marathon programme is "practically impossible", and a systematic review of 92 marathon plans (Appelhans et al., cited therein) says the same. Consensus within the critical literature. CORROBORATED. Sports Med Open 2024, s40798-024-00717-5 (review); mediaTUM/TUM record (thesis/review).
  • Popularity is not efficacy. Plan rankings come from forum mention counts and book sales, and their authors say these are not peer-reviewed. CORROBORATED. runningwithrock.com (blog); r/running wiki (community).
  • Where the methods actually differ (descriptive only). Pfitzinger starts with threshold work and moves to VO2max work. Daniels places VO2max/faster work earlier and uses 2 quality sessions a week ("2Q"). Hansons sets every pace from goal pace. CREDIBLE. Non-expert secondary sources only. Same sources as above.
  • "Results-proven practice" as a replacement for trials. Haugen et al. 2022 combine studies with elite training logs, mostly taken from websites. Elite marathoners run 160–220 km/week and track runners 130–190 km/week, with 11–14 sessions/week and ≥80% of volume at low intensity. CORROBORATED for elites. Transfer to amateurs is UNKNOWN, and the authors aim the work at world-class runners. PMC8975965, doi:10.1186/s40798-022-00438-7 (review); sciencepod summary.
  • Norwegian / double threshold. The systematic evidence comes from elites only: Kelemen et al. 2023 covers 7 studies on 13 elite runners. Critics report that amateurs often misapply it as "two tempo runs a day". Proponents claim more threshold volume for the same stress, a mechanism that has not been tested in amateurs. Contested. CREDIBLE. Commercial coaching sites. marathonhandbook.com; runnersconnect.net.

Q2. Volume, long run and progression rules

  • The "10% rule" has failed its only large RCT. Buist et al., AJSM 2008;36:33–39 (GRONORUN), randomised 532 novices (4-mile event) to a graded 13-week programme (≈10%/week) or a standard 8-week programme. Injury rates were 20.8% vs 20.3%, with no effect. CORROBORATED. PEDro record (database); BJSM summary 43/2/155 (journal).
  • Critique of that null result: both groups had low injury rates (other reports put novices at 29–58%), and the "standard" arm was itself gradual. The trial therefore does not show that progression doesn't matter. It only shows that 10% vs a moderate 8-week build made no difference. CREDIBLE. running-physio.com (clinician blog) plus the BJSM summary.
  • Single-session spikes: a recent large cohort (Nielsen et al., BJSM 2025, Garmin-RUNSAFE) reportedly links a session longer than ~10% above the longest run of the previous 30 days to injury. ALLEGED: from the witness's prior knowledge, not retrieved in this hearing. Must be checked.
  • Long-run caps (Hansons ~16 mi, % of weekly volume) and ultra volume/long-run predictors: UNKNOWN (searches blocked).

Q3. Limited time, short or long horizons, multi-year development

UNKNOWN for this base. Nothing retrieved. Blind spot: independent critiques of low-volume plans (3–4 h/week) and of multi-year progression barely exist outside practitioner writing.

Q4. Missed sessions, illness, race result below goal

UNKNOWN. Nothing retrieved. Independent evaluations of adjustment rules appear absent. The only published coaching literature on this is practitioner opinion, which belongs to the field base.

Q5. Failure modes of generic or online plans

  • Attrition is high. In a 2024 randomised pilot of novices (n=74, strength plus running vs running only), attrition was 41.7–52.6% and adherence was 46–57%, with no difference in injuries. The trial was underpowered: the authors estimate n=194 is needed. CORROBORATED. PMC10820279 / Sports 2024;12:25 (RCT pilot); DOAJ.
  • Injury is a major cause of drop-out in novices. CREDIBLE. BMC Public Health 13:685 protocol and the 2024 pilot above.
  • Copying elite structures (Norwegian method, high frequency) onto amateurs is flagged as a misuse. CREDIBLE, commercial sources (see Q1).
  • No study isolates generic online marathon plans. Trials of online programmes are registered but have published no results: NCT06538363 and ISRCTN37259753. UNKNOWN.

What this base cannot see

  • Coaches' tacit adjustment rules.
  • Ultra-specific coaching practice: Koop and others.
  • Proprietary app outcome data.
  • Any amateur RCT comparing named schools.

Overall strength of evidence for "method X beats method Y" for non-elites: unknown. Evidence for "a specific progression percentage prevents injury": contested, with weak support.

hearing-1-coach-field.md

Hearing 1 — Coaching practice 5K→100K road · base: FIELD

The witness informs only and prefers no school. Limits:

  • 7 searches ran before the shared search budget ran out.
  • Every page fetch failed (DNS), so nothing here is VERIFIED.
  • Sources are mostly secondary (coach blogs, summaries, forums), not the books themselves.
  • [mem] = the witness's own knowledge, unsourced, so graded ALLEGED.

Q1. How the methods structure plans: shared core vs. real differences

Shared core (majority view, CORROBORATED): most volume is easy; 1–3 key sessions a week; a long run; specificity rises as race day approaches; then a taper. For Daniels, at least 80% of weekly volume is at E/M pace, with T/I/R making up the rest (forum quote of the book, runningahead.com/forums/post/e989f57406784efdabd375505274c770; goodreads notes).

SchoolDistinctive parameterGrade
Hansons"Cumulative fatigue". Long run about 16 mi (25.7 km), at the end of 60–63 mi weeks. 3 key ("SOS") sessions a weekCORROBORATED (marathonhandbook.com/hansons-marathon-method/; lauranorrisrunning.com). Peak long run reported as 16, 16–18 or 16–20 mi → contested
CanovaGeneral → specific phases. "Special block" = two sessions in one day around marathon pace (MP). Specific work at 95–105% of goal pace, "special speeds" at 90% and 110%CREDIBLE (runnersconnect.net/special-block-training/; fastrunning.com/training/marathon-training/building-special-blocks/33861). The percentages vary; fastrunning says it does not suit non-full-time athletes
Norwegian / double thresholdLarge volume at or just below threshold, with every other run easy. Lactate target: 2–3, 2–4 or about 2 mmol/L. "Singles" variant = 2–3 sub-threshold sessions a weekCREDIBLE as a description (scienceofrunning.com 2026 Bakken interview; marathonhandbook.com/norwegian-singles-training/). Lactate numbers contested. Effect in amateurs: unknown
Koop (ultra)Intensity → specificity: VO2 → tempo/steady → race-specific endurance → taper. Back-to-back long runs (e.g. 4 h + 2.5 h)CORROBORATED (trainingpeaks.com plan tp-684231; strengthrunning.com/2022/02/ultrarunning-jason-koop/)
Lydiard, Pfitzinger, Higdon, GallowayLydiard: base → hills → track → taper. Pfitzinger: medium-long midweek runs, long run about 20 mi. Higdon novice: about 4 runs a week, long run peaks at 20 mi. Galloway: run-walkALLEGED [mem]

Q2. Volume, long run and key sessions, and how they progress

  • Daniels long-run cap: 25–30% of weekly volume or about 150 min, whichever is lower. CORROBORATED (irun.ca/?p=24743; fwdmotionsthlm newsletter; runningahead forum).
  • Some coaches apply it as an average over several weeks (ALLEGED).
  • The cap conflicts with practice: at 40 mi a week it allows a long run of only about 12.5 mi, which is below the 20 mi long runs found in Higdon-type plans.
  • Koop on volume: gains flatten beyond about 9 h a week.
  • 50K / 50 mi: about 9 h a week for 6–8 weeks.
  • 100 mi: 10–12 h a week.
  • CREDIBLE (suunto.com 2016 interview + strengthrunning), but possibly dated. No 100K-specific figure was found: unknown.
  • Quality share: a "10–15% quality" rule of thumb appears on forums. ALLEGED.
  • Progression rates (+10% a week, step-back every 3–4 weeks) were not sourced from the field base: UNKNOWN. See the med and phys witnesses.

Q3. Limited time, short or long horizons, multi-year development

  • Koop's diminishing returns above about 9 h a week favour time-efficient designs (CREDIBLE).
  • The Norwegian "singles" variant is presented as less time-consuming than doubles (CREDIBLE: marathonhandbook; runnersconnect).
  • Nothing was retrieved on weeks of 3–4 h, plans of 4 weeks or less, or multi-year arcs: unknown in this hearing.

Q4. Missed sessions, illness, results that differ from the goal

  • Daniels: after time off, paces (VDOT) and volume are reduced according to how long the break lasted.
  • The scheme's existence is CREDIBLE (Runner's Tribe, "Returning to Training", runnerstribe.com/?p=8591, could not be opened).
  • The exact figures are UNKNOWN. [mem] recalls three bands (5 days or less, 6–28 days, more than 8 weeks) with cuts of about 3–20%. Check against the book.
  • Race result off-goal: practice re-derives paces from the latest result (VDOT logic). CREDIBLE.
  • How a goal is renegotiated: not found.

Q5. Failure modes of generic plans, as coaches report them

  1. Amateurs copy elite methods (Canova "not for non-full-time athletes"). CREDIBLE.
  2. Age-groupers overestimate their threshold, so "sub-threshold" sessions run too hard (triathlete.com coach). CREDIBLE.
  3. The long run is out of proportion to weekly volume (the Daniels cap debate). CREDIBLE.
  4. A fixed template ignores fatigue and life load. ALLEGED.

What this base cannot see

  • Outcome or injury data comparing the schools.
  • Survivorship bias: published plans show successes.
  • The primary books themselves, which were not opened.
  • Practice for road 100K, which is documented far less than trail.

Overall: the shared core is a majority view. School-specific numbers are contested. Adaptation rules range from emerging to unknown.

hearing-1-med-acad.md

Hearing 1: Sports medicine & injury prevention (acad)

Access limits: No primary source could be opened. PubMed, PMC and BJSM were blocked by the proxy, and the shared search budget ran out after 3 successful queries. So nothing here is VERIFIED. Items marked [S] come from this session's search results. Items marked [M] come from the witness's prior knowledge with the citations given from memory. They count as ALLEGED until the fact desk opens them, and they cannot support a rule on their own. What this source base cannot see: coaching practice, unpublished app data, and the experience of runners who are not in cohort studies.

Q1 Risk factors

  • Previous injury is the strongest and most consistent predictor in long-distance runners (moderate-quality evidence). Non-running injury is the strongest predictor in short-distance runners (high quality). [S] van Poppel et al. 2021, Scand J Med Sci Sports, systematic review of 29 studies (link). Saragiotto 2014 and Hulme 2017 (Sports Med) [M] say the same. Majority view, CORROBORATED.
  • BMI, age, sex, experience and volume: findings are inconsistent across reviews. Earlier reviews found women at lower risk; later reviews mostly found no sex effect. Low running experience is linked to higher risk in short-distance runners. [S] van Poppel 2021, and the same pattern in its cited older reviews. Contested. All multivariable prediction models are low quality [S]. Injury is multifactorial.
  • Biomechanics (foot strike, pronation, footwear): no consistent prospective link [M] (Nielsen 2014 BJSM pronation cohort, about 900 novices). Contested / weak.
  • REDs/LEA: linked to bone stress injury and menstrual dysfunction [M] (IOC REDs consensus, Mountjoy 2023, BJSM 57:1073). The causal direction and the threshold (the historical figure of under 30 kcal/kg FFM/day) are debated. Majority view on the association, contested on thresholds.

Q2 Load progression limits

  • 10% weekly rule: in an RCT of novices preparing for a 6.7 km race (GRONORUN, Buist 2008, AJSM, n=532), a 13-week graded plan using the 10% rule gave 20.8% injury incidence vs 20.3% for a standard 8-week plan (p=0.90). [S] (PEDro record 20220; link). CORROBORATED: the rule has no proven protective effect.
  • Weekly spikes over 30%: in the 2-week window, novices who progressed more than 30% had more "distance-related" injuries (HR about 1.6) [M] (Nielsen 2014, JOSPT 44:739). ALLEGED.
  • Single-session spikes: in Frandsen et al. 2025, BJSM (5,205 runners, 588,071 sessions, 35% injured), risk rose once a run was more than 10% longer than the longest run in the previous 30 days. HRRs by spike size: small 1.64, moderate 1.52, large 2.28. Band definitions differ between secondary summaries and are unverified. In the same data, ACWR showed a negative dose-response and the week-to-week ratio showed no relationship. [S] doi:10.1136/bjsports-2024-109380, plus two secondary summaries. Emerging (one cohort). CREDIBLE.
  • ACWR (the "sweet spot" of 0.8–1.3, danger above 1.5): criticised for mathematical coupling and spurious correlation [M] (Lolli 2019 BJSM; Impellizzeri 2020 IJSPP 15:907). The 2025 cohort [S] contradicts the expected direction for runners. CONTRADICTED / contested.

Q3 Pre-participation screening [all M, ALLEGED]

  • PAR-Q+ (Warburton 2011) is the standard self-screen. A "yes" answer leads to follow-up questions and then to a professional. ACSM 2015 algorithm (Riebe, MSSE 47:2473): the deciding factors are current exercise habit, known cardiovascular, metabolic or renal disease, signs or symptoms, and the intended intensity. Age alone no longer triggers a medical check. Consensus among official bodies.
  • Sudden cardiac arrest in races: about 0.5 per 100,000 participants, higher in marathons and in men (Kim 2012, NEJM). CREDIBLE.
  • Pregnancy: about 150 min/week of moderate activity is recommended unless there are contraindications (ACOG Committee Opinion 804, 2020; IOC 2016–18). Postpartum: running no earlier than about 12 weeks, after a pelvic-floor assessment (Goom, Donnelly & Brockwell 2019, an expert guideline). Consensus for pregnancy; expert opinion for postpartum.

Q4 Red flags [all M, ALLEGED; mostly consensus or expert opinion, few trials]

  • Cardiac: chest pain or pressure, fainting during or right after exercise, disproportionate breathlessness, palpitations. These mean stop and get urgent assessment. Consensus.
  • Bone stress: focal bony tenderness, pain that develops earlier in each run or appears at rest or at night, a hop test that provokes pain. High-risk sites are the femoral neck, pelvis/sacrum, navicular, anterior tibial cortex and the base of the 5th metatarsal (Warden 2021, BJSM; Hoenig 2022). Majority view.
  • Illness "neck check" (keep exercise light if symptoms are only above the neck; stop if there is fever, body aches or chest symptoms): expert opinion only, never trialled. Unknown evidence base. Myocarditis means no competitive sport for 3–6 months (AHA/ACC 2015).
  • Heat: confusion or collapse with high core temperature means exertional heat stroke, which needs cold-water immersion. WBGT-based race and activity cut-offs exist (ACSM consensus, Roberts 2023). Consensus.

Q5 Return to running [all M, ALLEGED]

  • Bone stress injury: pain-free walking and hopping first, then walk–run intervals, then continuous running over roughly 4–12 weeks for low-risk sites. High-risk sites need longer and specialist care (Warden 2021). Expert consensus; no RCTs.
  • Post-viral (including COVID): at least 7 days symptom-free, then a staged return at about 2 days per stage (Elliott 2020, BJSM). Expert opinion.
  • Graded criteria-based return-to-running protocols are mostly expert opinion. No validated universal protocol was found. Emerging / unknown.
hearing-1-med-crit.md

Hearing 1 — Sports medicine & injury prevention — CRITICAL base (med-crit)

Process caveat (read first). The shared web-search budget ran out after 2 of the planned 6–10 queries, and WebFetch could not resolve any host (DNS failure), so I opened no primary source. Nothing below is VERIFIED. Claims marked "(s)" were confirmed by this session's searches. All others are well-known literature I name from prior knowledge but did not re-retrieve here. I grade those no higher than CREDIBLE, and the fact desk should check them before they ground any rule.

Q1 Risk factors

  • Previous injury is the most consistent predictor. Several systematic reviews agree: Saragiotto 2014 Sports Med, van der Worp 2015 PLoS One, Hulme 2017 Sports Med. Evidence: majority view. Grade: CREDIBLE.
  • Biomechanics (foot strike, pronation, footwear): the critical reviews find limited or conflicting evidence. Ceyssens 2019 Sports Med; Nigg 2015 BJSM "mythbusting" on shoes. Evidence: contested. Grade: CREDIBLE.
  • Sex, age and BMI: findings are inconsistent across reviews and confounded by exposure. Evidence: contested.
  • RED-S/LEA: the IOC 2023 consensus (Mountjoy, BJSM) treats it as a syndrome. Jeukendrup et al. 2024 (Sports Med) dispute that it is a distinct syndrome and criticise its diagnostic tools. The two sources disagree, so the syndrome's construct is contested. LEA → bone stress in women is majority view. Grade: CREDIBLE.
  • What my base cannot see: large prospective data on women (cohorts are often ~80% male; Garmin-RUNSAFE is 22% female (s)), on ultra distances, and on non-Western runners.

Q2 Load progression limits

  • The "10% weekly rule" has no RCT support. Buist 2008 (AJSM, GRONORUN, novices) found similar injury rates with a graded 10% programme and a standard one (~20% in each arm; figures to be confirmed). Damsted 2018 and Fredette 2022 systematic reviews call the load–injury evidence limited. Evidence: majority view that the 10% figure is folklore. Grade: CREDIBLE.
  • Week-to-week ratio vs single-session spike. Frandsen et al. 2025 BJSM used the Garmin-RUNSAFE cohort: n = 5,205 runners, 588,071 sessions, 1,820 injuries (35%) (s). Risk rose when a session's distance exceeded >10% of the longest run in the previous 30 days. Hazard ratios were about 1.64, 1.52 and 2.28 for small, moderate and large spikes, against ≤10% (s). The week-to-week ratio showed no association. ACWR showed an inverse association (s). Caveats: exploratory design, self-reported injuries, mean age 46 (s), and secondary summaries disagree on the bin cut-offs. Evidence: emerging. Grade: ALLEGED (single cohort) until replicated.
  • Older cohort signal. Nielsen 2014 (JOSPT, DANO-RUN, novices) found more distance-related injuries when weekly volume rose more than 30% over 2 weeks. Grade: CREDIBLE. Together with the Frandsen cohort, this suggests that very large increases matter, but no exact threshold is established.
  • ACWR: the critical literature argues that it should be dismissed as a model.
  • Impellizzeri 2020 (IJSPP): its causal assumptions have never been tested (s).
  • Impellizzeri 2021 (Sports Med 51:581–592): the ratio rescales acute load, which inflates effect sizes without improving prediction (s).
  • Lolli 2019 (BJSM): it suffers from mathematical coupling. Grade: CREDIBLE.
  • Counter-position: Gabbett and the IOC 2016 load consensus endorsed ACWR (s).
  • Evidence: contested, with the critical base largely negative. Grade: CORROBORATED (two independent critiques).

Q3 Pre-participation screening

  • PAR-Q+ and ACSM 2015 algorithm (Riebe, MSSE): the update was designed to reduce unnecessary medical referral. The decision inputs are current activity, known cardiovascular, metabolic or renal disease, and symptoms. Evidence: consensus as practice. Validation data are thin. Grade: CREDIBLE.
  • Cardiac risk in races is low. Kim 2012 (NEJM, RACER) found about 0.54 cardiac arrests per 100,000 participants, higher in marathons than half-marathons and in men. A later RACER update (Kim/Baggish, ~2025, JAMA) reports similar incidence and improved survival. Evidence: consensus. Grade: CREDIBLE; the 2025 update is ALLEGED (unretrieved).
  • Routine ECG screening for recreational runners is contested (false positives versus benefit).
  • Pregnancy and postpartum: the postpartum return-to-run guidance (Goom, Donnelly and Brockwell 2019; suggests about 12 weeks plus graded tests) is expert opinion, not trial-based. Grade: CREDIBLE as an expert statement.

Q4 Red flags during training

  • Cardiac symptoms (exertional chest pain, syncope, palpitations) leading to stop and refer: consensus. Grade: CREDIBLE.
  • Bone stress (focal bony tenderness, pain that worsens during a run or at rest or at night): consensus clinical teaching (Warden 2014). Grade: CREDIBLE.
  • Illness "neck check" (Eichner 1993; train with symptoms above the neck only, no training with fever or systemic symptoms): expert opinion with no trial behind it. Evidence: emerging/unknown. Grade: CREDIBLE as a convention only.
  • Heat: exertional heat stroke is a medical emergency. Rule thresholds such as WBGT cut-offs come from ACSM position stands (acad base), not from my base.

Q5 Return to running

  • Graded walk-run protocols after bone stress injury (Warden 2014; similar military protocols) and graded return after viral illness or COVID (Elliott 2020 BJSM): both are expert consensus with no RCTs. Evidence: consensus of opinion, weak empirical base. Grade: CREDIBLE.
  • The critical base finds no validated return-to-run criteria and notes that time-based rules are arbitrary. Grade: CREDIBLE.

Evidence summary

ClaimStrengthGrade
Prior injury predicts injurymajorityCREDIBLE
10% weekly rulenot supportedCREDIBLE

ACWR not valid for predictioncontestedCORROBORATED
