Sources

  • Frandsen 2025 BJSM summary: link (blog); link (repository)
  • Impellizzeri 2021 Sports Med: link
  • ACWR editorial (Frontiers 2021): link
  • Unretrieved citations (to be checked by the fact desk): Buist 2008 AJSM doi:10.1177/0363546507307505; Nielsen 2014 JOSPT; Kim 2012 NEJM doi:10.1056/NEJMoa1106468; Lolli 2019 BJSM; Jeukendrup 2024 Sports Med; Mountjoy 2023 BJSM; Riebe 2015 MSSE; Warden 2014; Elliott 2020 BJSM; Goom et al. 2019.
hearing-1-med-field.md

Hearing 1 — Sports medicine & injury prevention — FIELD base

Read first, this is a degraded hearing. I ran 5 searches. Two worked, two hit the rate limit and the fifth hit the shared per-turn search cap. Opening pages failed: the egress proxy refused every host I tried (403 / DNS). No claim here is VERIFIED. Claims tagged [search] come from the search results I got back (snippets only). Claims tagged [prior] rest on practitioner sources I know but did not open this session. Where I can name two independent practitioner origins, I capped the grade at CREDIBLE. Otherwise the claim is ALLEGED. The fact desk should re-run this hearing with working web access before any rule rests on it.

What the field base can't see: effect sizes, trial quality, or anything about whether field rules actually reduce injury. Practitioners mostly pass on heuristics.

1. Injury risk factors

  • Prior injury is the risk factor practitioners cite most often, ahead of load errors ("too much, too soon"). Field consensus. CREDIBLE [prior]: physio and coaching literature, for example Running Clinic courses and Pfitzinger's Advanced Marathoning.
  • Sex and RED-S: coaches treat missed or absent periods, repeated bone stress injuries and unexplained performance decline as low energy availability (LEA) warning signs. Majority view. CREDIBLE [prior].
  • Biomechanics (foot strike, cadence, shoes): the field is split. Retraining clinicians use cadence +5–10% as a tool for specific pathologies. Many coaches see no general prevention value. Contested. CREDIBLE [prior].
  • Age: coaches give masters runners more recovery days and longer step-backs. This is practice, not measured risk. ALLEGED [prior].

2. Load-progression limits

  • The "10% weekly rule" is everywhere in field material but is acknowledged as a heuristic without a validated threshold. Strength: contested/folklore. CREDIBLE [search + prior].
  • Field alternatives:
  • Daniels: hold a new load 3–4 weeks before increasing. Add about 1 mile (1.6 km) per weekly session, with a stated cap of about 10 miles a week.
  • Cutback or deload weeks every 3–4 weeks, near-universal in published plans (Pfitzinger, Higdon, Hansons).
  • Strength: majority practice. CREDIBLE [prior].
  • Single-session spikes: coaches now widely quote the 2025 Garmin-RUNNING cohort (Nielsen et al., BJSM). It reports higher injury risk when one run exceeds the longest run of the previous 30 days by more than about 10%. That is an academic source reaching the field second-hand. Strength: emerging. CREDIBLE [prior]; the acad witness should verify it.
  • ACWR: used in team-sport practice, but runners' tools and coaches use it little. It faces strong critique (see the crit hearing). Contested. CREDIBLE [prior].

3. Pre-participation screening

  • Running-club and coach-education practice: a PAR-Q+-style questionnaire, with medical clearance if anything is flagged. Majority practice. CREDIBLE [prior].
  • Postpartum: practitioners use the UK physio guideline by Goom, Donnelly & Brockwell (2019).
  • No running before about 12 weeks postpartum.
  • Then a load-and-impact test battery: walk 30 min, single-leg stand 10 s, jog 1 min, 10 single-leg hops, and others.
  • Pelvic-floor symptoms (leaking, heaviness, pain) mean referral.
  • It is a single guideline, but widely adopted. Strength: majority practice. CREDIBLE [prior].
  • The field base can't see cardiac screening yield or age cut-offs. Refer to acad.

4. Red flags (stop and/or refer)

  • Pain monitoring: physios widely use the Thomeé/Silbernagel model. Pain during running is allowed up to ≤5/10, provided it settles soon after and is back to baseline by next morning, and does not rise from week to week.
  • Stricter clinic protocols use ≤2/10, and repeat the previous step if pain goes >3/10 or symptoms last into the next day.
  • Pain of 7–10/10 means stop.
  • Strength: contested thresholds, shared structure. CORROBORATED [search]: RunnersConnect, Spectrum Health IE and a Pabau clinic guide (likely sponsored).
  • Bone stress signs: focal bony tenderness, pain that worsens during the run, pain at rest or at night, limping, pain on hopping. Practice is to stop running and refer. Consensus in practice. CREDIBLE [prior].
  • Cardiac: chest pain or pressure, fainting or near-fainting during exercise, palpitations, breathlessness out of proportion to effort. Practice is to stop and get urgent medical review. Consensus. CREDIBLE [prior].
  • Illness, the "neck check":
  • Symptoms above the neck (runny nose, mild sore throat): easy, shorter running is acceptable.
  • Symptoms below the neck (chest, GI, muscle aches) or fever: rest.
  • Sources agree the rule is not a medical test and has thin evidence.
  • Strength: field consensus, weak evidence. CORROBORATED [search]: USA Triathlon, Next Clinic AU, Spooner PT, runtothefinish, realbuzz.
  • Heat: confusion, collapse or loss of coordination means stop and get emergency care. Consensus. CREDIBLE [prior].

5. Return to running

  • Common clinic structure: pain-free walking (about 30 min) and hop or calf-capacity criteria first, then a walk-run progression.
  • Typical start is about 1 min run / 1–2 min walk.
  • Running time grows across sessions, usually on alternate days.
  • Intensity is added only after continuous easy running is tolerated.
  • The pain-monitoring rule above applies throughout.
  • Strength: majority practice; protocols vary and are not standardised.
  • CREDIBLE [prior + search]: Pabau protocol, MGH/academic-clinic return-to-run sheets [prior].
  • After illness: practice is to come back gradually after a fever-free period. Numbers vary by source. UNKNOWN in this base; refer to acad (for example the post-viral graded protocols).

Sources

URLs come from search results. None was opened.

  • link (federation article)
  • link (clinic blog)
  • link (physio blog)
  • link (coach blog)
  • link (coaching site)
  • link (physio clinic)
  • link (clinic-software guide, likely sponsored)
  • [prior, not opened] Daniels, Daniels' Running Formula; Pfitzinger & Douglas, Advanced Marathoning; Goom, Donnelly & Brockwell, Returning to running postnatal (2019).
hearing-1-nutr-acad.md

Hearing 1 — Nutrition & fuelling (nutr) — academic & official base (acad)

Access limits (read first): 7 web searches succeeded; the shared search budget then ran out and every primary-source fetch failed (DNS/proxy 403). No claim below is VERIFIED. Grades rest on abstracts, records and secondary summaries. Numbers marked (memory) were not surfaced by any search this session and are ALLEGED until a fact desk opens the full text. What acad cannot see: real race tolerance, coach heuristics, adherence; trials skew to trained young men, cycling, lab settings (running-specific GI limits under-studied).

Q1. Daily energy/CHO; REDs / LEA

  • Daily CHO scales with load: 3–10 g/kg/day, up to ~12 g/kg for extreme prolonged work (ACSM/AND/DC joint position, Thomas, Erdman & Burke 2016, MSSE 48(3):543–568). CORROBORATED (secondary summary + slide deck; same origin, so effectively one statement). Strength: consensus among position stands.
  • REDs: 2023 IOC consensus (Mountjoy et al., BJSM 2023;57:1073–98, doi:10.1136/bjsports-2023-106994). LEA is a spectrum: short/transient = "adaptable", harm arises when prolonged or severe. Low carbohydrate availability adds risk on its own. Prevalence 15–80% depending on sport. LEA should be ruled out before diagnosing overtraining. Assessment tool IOC REDs CAT2: a 4-colour traffic light run by clinicians. CORROBORATED. Strength: consensus on concept; emerging on thresholds.
  • EA threshold "<30 kcal/kg FFM/day" (memory): ALLEGED. The 2023 statement moves away from a single cut-off (CREDIBLE).
  • Warning signs to verify against the full text: menstrual dysfunction, bone stress injury, low libido/testosterone, recurrent illness, mood change, stalled performance (memory, partly matches the summaries). CREDIBLE.

Q2. Race and long-run fuelling

  • 30–60 g CHO/h for long events; up to 90 g/h for events >2.5 h, using multiple transportable CHO (glucose:fructose) (Burke 2011 abstract; GSSI SSE #108; ACSM 2016). CORROBORATED. Strength: consensus.
  • Glucose:fructose blends give up to ~65% higher exogenous oxidation than glucose alone (GSSI SSE #108, Jeukendrup). CREDIBLE (industry-funded).
  • >90 → 120 g/h: emerging/contested. Some reviews propose it for trained athletes. Others find little evidence that it beats 90 g/h. CONTRADICTED between sources.
  • Ultra: ISSN position stand (Tiller et al. 2019, JISSN 16:50, doi:10.1186/s12970-019-0312-9) supports an individualised, periodised, food-first approach, raised fluid/electrolyte needs in heat, gut training, low-FODMAP for GI distress and caffeine late in the race. CORROBORATED (abstract + repository records). Its numeric targets ~150–400 kcal/h, ~450–750 mL/h, sodium ~500–700 mg/L (memory): ALLEGED.

Q3. Hydration, hyponatremia, heat

  • ACSM fluid stand (Sawka et al. 2007, MSSE 39(2):377–390, PMID 17277604): aim to prevent >2% body-mass loss and large electrolyte shifts. Individualise, because sweat rate and sweat sodium vary widely. Estimate sweat rate by weighing before and after exercise. CHO-electrolyte drinks beat water in some conditions. CORROBORATED (abstract + several records).
  • EAH consensus (Hew-Butler et al. 2015, CJSM 25(4):303–320; BJSM 49(22):1432–46): overdrinking (dilution) is the main cause, and drinking to thirst is the primary prevention. CORROBORATED (records + press coverage). Strength: consensus for EAH prevention.
  • "2% rule" vs "drink to thirst": contested framing (performance vs safety); shared ground: no weight gain during a race.
  • Risk factors (weight gain during the race, finishing time >4 h, low body mass, NSAIDs) (memory): ALLEGED pending full text.

Q4. Trainable fuelling competences

  • The gut adapts: gastric emptying, comfort and absorption improve with repeated intake, and adaptations are nutrient-specific. Few direct trials (Jeukendrup 2017, Sports Med 47 S1, PMC5371619). Systematic review (Sports Med 2023, doi:10.1007/s40279-023-01841-0): 8 studies, gut training reduces GI discomfort and CHO malabsorption. CORROBORATED. Strength: emerging (small n, short protocols).
  • Protocol dose/duration: unknown (not standardised).
  • Sweat-rate self-testing (Sawka 2007) and race-day rehearsal (ISSN 2019) are endorsed. CORROBORATED.

Q5. Automated tool vs professional

  • The acad base has no study of nutrition advice from automated or self-coaching tools: unknown.
  • Position stands place diagnosis and management with professionals: the REDs CAT2 is clinician-administered, and EAH treatment is medical. The ACSM 2016 statement names the sports dietitian's role. CORROBORATED.
  • Generic ranges (g/kg/day, g/h, thirst-guided drinking) are published for athletes as general guidance. Individual energy prescription, LEA/disordered-eating screening and clinical conditions (diabetes, GI disease, pregnancy) fall outside general guidance. CREDIBLE (inferred from the stands' scope, not stated as a rule for tools).

Sources (type)

  • Thomas, Erdman, Burke 2016, MSSE 48(3):543 (position stand), via link (secondary)
  • Mountjoy et al. 2023, doi:10.1136/bjsports-2023-106994 (IOC consensus), link
  • Tiller et al. 2019, doi:10.1186/s12970-019-0312-9 (ISSN stand), link
  • Burke et al. 2011 (review), link
  • GSSI SSE #108 (industry review), link
  • Sawka et al. 2007 (ACSM stand), link
  • Hew-Butler et al. 2015 (consensus), link
  • Jeukendrup 2017 (review), link
  • Gut-training SR 2023, link
hearing-1-nutr-crit.md

Hearing 1 — Nutrition & fuelling — critical & independent base (nutr-crit)

Scope and limits. 8 WebSearch queries (1 failed with a rate-limit error; the session search budget then ran out). Opening primary sources failed: WebFetch could not resolve the hosts (PMC, doi.org, SpringerOpen), and no web-fetch agent was available. So no claim below is VERIFIED. Grades rest on abstracts, repository records and search summaries. This base sees critiques, replications and dissent. It does not see the consensus numbers themselves (acad) or coaching practice (field). The "train-low" and low-carbohydrate/high-fat critiques (e.g. Burke 2017) could not be retrieved: UNKNOWN from this base.

Q1 — Energy/CHO needs; RED-S / low energy availability (LEA)

  • The 30 kcal/kg FFM/day LEA cut-off comes from short (≤5 day) lab studies, mostly in sedentary women. Menstrual disturbance rises linearly as energy availability (EA) falls, with no threshold, and disturbances occur above 30 as well. — Lieberman et al. MSSE 2018; De Souza/Williams critical review (PSU repository: link); Curr Opin Physiol 2019 (link). Peer-reviewed critique. CORROBORATED; majority view among critics, contested by IOC authors.
  • EA cannot be measured reliably in the field. Athletes under-report intake (≈19% on average, ALLEGED as a figure), and FFM methods (DXA vs BIA) classify people differently near the threshold. — mysportscience.com (independent reviewer); LJMU repository review. CORROBORATED.
  • The RED-S construct was disputed when it appeared (De Souza et al., BJSM 2014, link IOC reply, Mountjoy 2015). A later critical review says many RED-S components "need more robust scientific justification". Male thresholds (e.g. 40 kcal/kg FFM) are "hotly debated". Contested; CORROBORATED that a dispute exists.
  • Not covered here: daily carbohydrate g/kg bands (acad base).

Q2 — Race and long-run fuelling

  • The 120 g/h evidence rests on one small RCT (Urdampilleta et al. 2020, n=26 elite trail runners, 20 analysed, PMC7400827). It measured recovery markers, not race performance. Every group had GI symptoms, and they were worst at 120 g/h. Dose was confounded with the glucose:fructose ratio. The race was organised by the researchers. — Trial record plus independent commentaries (Jeukendrup, mysportscience; bornonthetrail substack). CORROBORATED; emerging/contested. No road-running performance trial at ≥90 g/h was found from this base: UNKNOWN.
  • The view that "70–90 g/h with gut training is a better tolerability trade-off" is commentator opinion. ALLEGED.

Q3 — Hydration, hyponatremia, heat

  • Overdrinking, not low sodium intake, is the main driver of exercise-associated hyponatremia (EAH).
  • Boston Marathon 2002 (Almond et al., NEJM 2005, PMID 15829535): 13% of 488 sampled finishers had sodium ≤135 mmol/L, and 0.6% were critical (≤120).
  • Weight gain during the race: OR 4.2.
  • Finish time >4:00 vs <3:30: OR 7.4.
  • BMI extremes were also a risk factor.
  • Fluid type (water vs sports drink) made no difference. Univariate: >3 L drunk.
  • Western States 161 km (Hoffman/Stuempfle, MSSE 2015; Sports Med Open 2015, PMC4688305): sodium supplement intake was unrelated to EAH, cramping, dehydration or nausea, with temperatures up to 39 °C.
  • CORROBORATED (two independent cohorts, different groups); consensus among critics.
  • Ad libitum (thirst-led) vs programmed drinking: in a meta-analysis of crossover trials (Goulet & Hoffman, Sports Med 2019, doi:10.1007/s40279-018-0905-5), performance was similar or slightly better with ad libitum drinking. This applies to efforts of about 1–2 h only. — Plus the WEM 2016 pro/con debate (Hoffman/Cotter/Goulet/Laursen vs Armstrong/Johnson/Bergeron). CORROBORATED for ≤2 h; contested in heat; UNKNOWN for 50–100 km road (few direct comparisons, per a 2025 review).

Q4 — Trainable fuelling competences

  • Gut training: the systematic review by Martinez et al. (Sports Med 2023, s40279-023-01841-0) found only 8 trials, lasting 4–28 days.
  • 2 of the 8 showed GI symptom reduction.
  • 2 studies found 45–54% less carbohydrate malabsorption after about 2 weeks of repeated feeding; 1 found no change.
  • No change in gastric emptying or gut-injury markers.
  • There is a running-specific trial (Costa et al. 2017).
  • Ultra review (Arribalzaga 2021): GI symptoms vary with altitude, environment and speed.
  • CORROBORATED that the evidence is small and mixed; emerging. No dose-response data were found, so progression rates are UNKNOWN.

Q5 — Automated tool vs professional

  • Calorie-tracking apps are associated with disordered eating, especially in people with current or past eating disorders. The data are retrospective and self-reported, so causation is unproven. — MyFitnessPal studies (general and male samples), a 2024 review (PMC11587730), and a University of Alberta thesis. CORROBORATED (association); contested (causation).
  • No study evaluating automated or AI-generated nutrition advice for runners was found: UNKNOWN.
  • Because EA cannot be measured reliably (Q1), any automated LEA screening based on intake logging inherits that measurement error. This is an inference from Q1 sources, CREDIBLE.

Evidence summary

TopicStrengthGrade
EAH ← overdrinking, slow finish, weight gainconsensusCORROBORATED
Sodium pills do not prevent EAH or crampsmajority viewCORROBORATED
Thirst ≈ plan for ≤2 hmajority viewCORROBORATED
Hard 30 kcal/kg FFM thresholdcontestedCONTRADICTED as a cut-off
120 g/h benefitemergingCORROBORATED as weak
Gut training efficacyemergingCORROBORATED as mixed
Apps → disordered eatingcontested causationCORROBORATED association
hearing-1-nutr-field.md

Hearing 1 — Nutrition & fuelling — FIELD base (practice & field)

Role: inform only; no recommendation, no school picked. Searches: 9. Primary-page fetches tried (mysportscience, RunnersConnect) failed (DNS), so no claim here is VERIFIED. The field base mostly repeats academic numbers, so "two sources" here often means two practitioners citing the same underlying papers. Treat that as echo, not independence.

Q1. Daily energy and carbohydrate; RED-S / LEA warning signs

  • Field guides scale daily carbohydrate to training load in bands of about 5–7 g/kg/day for moderate training, 7–10 g/kg/day for 1–3 h/day, and 10–12 g/kg/day for very high volume. These bands are attributed to Burke et al. 2001 (Marathon Handbook). The ultra practice consensus gives 5–8 g/kg/day (JISSN 2019 ultra position stand, as relayed by practitioners). CORROBORATED; majority view.
  • In practice runners do not match intake to load: one 12-week study of 55 athletes averaged 3.9 g/kg/day (range 1.2–7.2) (medRxiv 10.1101/2022.06.21.22276725; Marathon Handbook summary). CREDIBLE (single study, preprint, relayed twice).
  • Practitioners list these warning signs: repeated colds or illness, persistent fatigue, falling performance, GI bloating, repeated bone stress injuries, menstrual change, and in males reduced morning erections. Performance can rise for a while and mask LEA, and normal body weight does not rule it out (McGregor via Runner's World AU, Fuel for Thought extract; Coach Athletics AU). CORROBORATED; consensus among practitioners. One cited threshold, 3 consecutive missed periods or a change in a previously regular cycle, comes only from the McGregor extract: ALLEGED (single field source). Coaching material says coaches should recognise and refer, never diagnose (Coach Athletics AU; NSCA): CORROBORATED.

Q2. Long-run and race fuelling (marathon, 50–100 km road)

  • Field targets are about 30–60 g/h for 1–2.5 h, up to 60 g/h for 2–3 h, and up to 90 g/h of glucose+fructose (about 2:1) beyond about 2.5 h. The single-transporter ceiling is about 60 g/h (Jeukendrup/mysportscience; Precision Fuel & Hydration; Multiday Running). CORROBORATED; majority view. Jeukendrup also says 30–60 g/h is enough for most marathoners: CREDIBLE.
  • Real intake is low: marathoners averaged about 35 g/h in a 2010 study, and a fixed plan of about 60 g carbohydrate + 60 mg sodium + 90 mg caffeine per hour (about 3 gels) gave runners about 4.7% faster times than self-selected intake (mysportscience, relayed by fastrunning.com). CREDIBLE (one study, page not opened).
  • Ultra practice adds real food. Practitioners say liquids/gels are gentler for the first ≤3 h, while fibre, fat and protein raise GI risk (Runner's World SA; Skratch/Allen Lim; Multiday Running). CORROBORATED; consensus in practice, weak data.
  • The field base cannot see controlled 50–100 km road fuelling trials.

Q3. Hydration, sodium, hyponatremia, heat

  • "Drink to thirst" (Noakes, Waterlogged) is accepted by practitioners for most athletes in normal conditions, but they dispute it as an absolute rule for long or hot events (iRunFar review; TrainingPeaks; Precision Hydration). CONTESTED.
  • Overdrinking is the main hyponatremia risk. One practical ceiling given is kidney excretion of about 800 mL/h for men and about 600 mL/h for smaller women (RunnersConnect). That figure is ALLEGED as stated. The mechanism (overdrinking above losses) is CORROBORATED (Noakes; TrainingPeaks).
  • Sweat sodium varies by about 200 to more than 2,000 mg/L (Precision Hydration). One pro ultra runner logged about 120–370 mg/h (Precision case study). These are vendor sources with a conflict of interest: ALLEGED/CREDIBLE. Practitioners do not agree on fixed sodium-per-hour numbers: CONTESTED.
  • The field base found no specific practice protocol for heat beyond "drink more, individualise".

Q4. Fuelling competences to train progressively

  • Practitioners agree on this sequence: practise race products in training, then build g/h on long runs, then rehearse at race pace, and never try anything new on race day (Precision; RunnersConnect; Multiday Running; RUN247). CORROBORATED; consensus.
  • Progression numbers vary by source. One plan starts at 30 g/h and adds about 15 g/week (Sports Medicine Weekly): ALLEGED. Others give 4–6 weeks (ctyeh) or 8–12 weeks before an ultra (Multiday): ALLEGED and inconsistent. Studies relayed by the field show 2 weeks of daily gut training at about 90 g/h cut GI symptoms by about 60% (Canadian Running; Run Waterloo): CORROBORATED (relay).
  • Other competences named by practitioners: estimating sweat rate, choosing sodium, mixing real food (ultra), reading thirst, and carbohydrate loading at about 8–10 g/kg/day for 2–3 days (calculator sites: ALLEGED).

Q5. What an automated tool vs a professional should handle

  • Certification bodies (NSCA, ACE, NASM, UESCA) agree on this split. General education (ranges, timing, practice principles) is within a coach's scope. Individual meal plans, supplements, medical conditions, deficiencies, eating disorders and RED-S management go to a registered dietitian or clinician, and in some places this is set by law. CORROBORATED; consensus (industry bodies, not regulators).
  • By analogy, practitioners treat these as refer-out triggers: LEA signs, amenorrhoea, recurrent bone stress injuries, disordered eating, diabetes or GI disease, and pregnancy.

What the field base cannot see

  • Effect sizes, injury and health outcomes, sex-specific data, and masters runners.
  • How independent these sources really are: vendor bias (Precision Hydration, Skratch) and many sources relaying the same papers.
  • Regulatory scope law by country.
hearing-1-phys-acad.md

Hearing 1 — phys / acad

Limits: 6 searches were run before the shared search budget ran out. I could not open any source (PubMed, PMC, Springer and EuropePMC gave DNS errors), so nothing is VERIFIED. Blind spots: coaching practice, and data on beginners and ultra runners (most trials study trained men).

Q1. Determinants by distance

  • VO2max, the sustainable fraction of VO2max (lactate threshold or critical speed) and running economy explain about 70% of the variance in marathon time (di Prampero 1986, cited in Hunter & Muniz-Pumares 2025; Joyner 1985, PubMed 3890068). Consensus. CORROBORATED.
  • Longer distances: the weight of VO2max falls but stays significant at 84 km. The sustained fraction drops from about 82% (marathon) to about 67% (84 km) (Davies & Thompson, doi:10.1007/BF00429740). Ultra fatigue has several causes, and experience also predicts performance (J Hum Kinet 2020 systematic review). Majority view. CREDIBLE.
  • 5–10K: VO2max and the capacity above critical speed (W′) weigh more. Majority view. CREDIBLE (no second source in this run).
  • Durability / resilience (how much the markers drift over long efforts) is proposed as a fourth determinant (Jones 2023, J Physiol; Hunter & Muniz-Pumares 2025, n=18). Emerging. CORROBORATED as a concept. Its size is UNKNOWN.

Q2. Intensity distribution

  • Pyramidal and polarized training beat threshold-heavy training (2018 review, PubMed 29863593; Rosenblat 2019 meta-analysis).
  • The 2025 individual-participant network meta-analysis (Rosenblat, Seiler et al., Sports Med 55:655–673, doi:10.1007/s40279-024-02149-3) is reported to find polarized and pyramidal broadly similar. Contested / emerging. CREDIBLE. Its subgroup effects (by level, by study length) are ALLEGED (paper not opened).
  • Scope: trials last 6–16 weeks in trained athletes. Evidence for beginners, under about 3 h/week and ultra is UNKNOWN.
  • Elites do about 75–80% of sessions at low intensity. Majority view. CREDIBLE. Some world-class marathoners train threshold-heavy; this is practice data, not causal evidence.

Q3. Load models

  • ACWR (acute:chronic workload ratio): it suffers from ratio artefacts. With random chronic loads swapped in, the injury association still appeared (Impellizzeri, Sports Med 2021; IJSPP 2020; Lolli and Curran-Everett, cited secondarily). As an injury predictor it is CONTRADICTED. Contested. Gabbett's defence was not retrieved.
  • Banister fitness-fatigue model: it assumes linearity, independence and determinism (Swinton 2021). Its parameters are ill-conditioned and individual (Hellard 2006). It describes load but is not validated for prospective prescription. Majority view. CORROBORATED.
  • Monotony, strain and the "10% rule": not searched. UNKNOWN (see the med hearings).

Q4. Intensity anchors

  • HRV-derived thresholds agree with the ventilatory threshold on average. Individual agreement is wide: −11 to +13 bpm at the lower threshold, −18 to +10 bpm at the upper (27 studies, n=461, Sports Med Open 2023). Emerging. CREDIBLE.
  • Critical speed (CS) sits close to the maximal metabolic steady state (Jones et al., Physiol Rev 2019). The moderate–heavy boundary sits at a consistent percentage of CS (26 studies, n=527; Hunter et al.). CS can be estimated from 2–3 race results, with no lab. Majority view. CORROBORATED.
  • Fixed %HRmax zones and RPE: no agreement study was found. The claim that fixed percentages misclassify individuals is ALLEGED.

Q5. Taper, deload, periodization

  • Taper: about 2 weeks, cut volume by 41–60%, keep intensity, keep frequency at about 80% or more. Expected gain about 2–3% (Bosquet 2007, MSSE; Mujika & Padilla 2003). Consensus. CREDIBLE (numbers seen via a secondary source).
  • Block vs traditional periodization:
  • VO2max: standardized mean difference 0.40 (95% CI 0.02–0.79).
  • Maximal workload: standardized mean difference 0.28.
  • Based on 6 pooled studies of low quality (PEDro 3.7/10) (Mølmen, OAJSM 2019).
  • A 2022 RCT found no difference (Almquist, Front Physiol).
  • Contested. CORROBORATED.
  • Deload ratios (e.g. 3:1) and undulating models in runners: UNKNOWN (not retrieved).

Sources

  • Rosenblat 2025 network meta-analysis: doi:10.1007/s40279-024-02149-3
  • 2018 review: link
  • Impellizzeri: link · link
  • Mølmen 2019: link
  • HRV systematic review: link
  • Moderate–heavy boundary vs CS: link
  • Durability study: link
  • Ultra systematic review: link
  • Davies & Thompson: link
  • Fitness-fatigue model limits: link
hearing-1-phys-crit.md

Hearing 1 — Exercise physiology (phys) — critical & independent base (crit)

Method note: I ran 8 WebSearch queries before the shared search budget ran out. WebFetch could not reach Springer or PMC (DNS failure), so no primary source was opened and nothing here is VERIFIED. Items marked [bk] come from my background knowledge of the critical literature and could not be re-checked this session. What this base cannot see: what elite coaches actually do and why (field), positive evidence syntheses (acad), and any data from apps or proprietary platforms.

Q1. Determinants by distance

The core model (VO2max × fractional utilisation × running economy; Joyner & Coyle 2008 [bk]) is consensus. Critics point out that it was built from short laboratory tests on fresh runners. "Durability", meaning how far economy and thresholds drift after 2+ h of running, is proposed as a separate determinant for the marathon and 50–100 km (Maunder et al. 2021, Sports Med; Jones 2023 "physiological resilience" [bk]). Emerging; CREDIBLE. No validated field test for durability exists, and its exact weight at each distance is unknown.

Q2. Intensity distribution

  • Polarized and pyramidal models both outperform threshold-heavy training in integrative reviews of runners (PubMed 29182410, 2018; elite review 2022 found 75–80% of volume at low intensity, 120–180 km/wk). Majority view; CORROBORATED.
  • "Polarized is optimal" is contested. Burnley, Bearden & Jones (MSSE 2022) argued there is "no evidence" of polarized superiority (sponet 4076014). The 2019 Rosenblat meta-analysis rested on only 3 RCTs. The 2025 IPD network meta-analysis (Rosenblat et al., Sports Med 55:655–673, doi:10.1007/s40279-024-02149-3; ~13 studies, ~350 athletes per a secondary write-up) could not be opened. Its exact conclusions are therefore UNKNOWN to me. I have background knowledge, not checked, that it found polarized may help VO2max mainly in higher-level athletes and found no clear difference in time-trial performance. ALLEGED.
  • Methodological critiques (CREDIBLE, ≥2 sources): (a) results depend on how zones are counted, whether by time-in-zone, session goal or 3- vs 5-zone schemes; (b) studies are short (6–16 wk) and small, and use mostly sub-elite or untrained participants who "respond to anything"; (c) volume is often not matched.

Q3. Load models and progression

  • ACWR: the IOC 2016 consensus endorsed it. Critics later showed several problems. First, mathematical coupling: the acute week sits inside the chronic average and produces a spurious correlation of about 0.5 (Lolli et al. 2019 BJSM 53:921). Second, ratio artefacts and no causal estimates (Impellizzeri et al. 2020 IJSPP 15:907). Third, replacing chronic load with contrived values left the "predictions" unchanged, which led to the call to "dismiss ACWR" (Impellizzeri et al. 2021 Sports Med 51:581). Defenders (Coyne et al.) found coupled and uncoupled ratios highly correlated. Contested; CORROBORATED that ACWR has no established causal validity as an injury predictor. No thresholds (e.g. "0.8–1.3 sweet spot") survive this critique as validated cut-offs. CONTRADICTED as validated.
  • Banister fitness-fatigue: its parameters are poorly identifiable and ill-conditioned. Fits depend on the starting values and the load metric, and goodness-of-fit is not the same as prediction (Sci Rep 2025, hal-04999009; arXiv 1902.02061, arXiv 2505.20859; Gindre thesis 2009). It is a data model, not a physiological one, and it assumes linearity, so double the load means double the gain. Majority view among critics; CORROBORATED. Individual fits usually need dozens of performance tests per athlete [bk].
  • Monotony/strain (Foster): little independent validation [bk]. ALLEGED.
  • The "10% rule" is a medical-domain question. Defer to med.

Q4. Intensity anchoring

  • Fixed %HRmax or %HRR bands match individual thresholds poorly. In secondary reports of Iannetta et al. 2020 (MSSE), lactate threshold fell anywhere between ~60% and 90% of HRmax. Jamnick et al. 2020 (Sports Med) [bk] showed that threshold definitions disagree with one another. CORROBORATED, though the primaries were not opened.
  • Age formulas: in an ACE-funded study, predicted HRmax (207 − 0.7×age) placed HR in the correct zone 82% of the time, with individual error in both directions (acefitness.org 7466). CREDIBLE.
  • Critical speed: 3MT and multi-trial field estimates are reliable (ICC ~0.95 for CS; Vassallo 2021; Brazilian n=7 study). Reliability depends on standardised conditions (Frontiers 2025 systematic review, 19 studies). In swimmers, the 3MT underestimated D′. Majority view; CORROBORATED. All samples were small.
  • Zone names are not interchangeable across systems: "Zone 2" differs, and Daniels easy is 65–79% HRmax versus Polar's 60–70%. CORROBORATED.
  • RPE: no critical-base evidence was retrieved. UNKNOWN from this base.

Q5. Taper, deload, periodization

  • Taper: Bosquet 2007 (MSSE) [bk] reports ~2 weeks, a 41–60% volume cut with intensity maintained, and a small improvement (~2–3%). Critics note it draws mostly on swimming and cycling, uses short studies, and cannot isolate the taper effect. Majority view; CREDIBLE.
  • Block vs traditional periodization: Kiely 2010 described block periodization as "a new variation, not a new horizon". The Dantas meta-analyses were criticised for including non-peer-reviewed sources. Mølmen 2019 found a small VO2max benefit for block periodization but drew on low-quality studies. Almquist 2022 (load-matched cyclists) found no performance difference. Contested to weak evidence; CORROBORATED.
  • Deload frequency (e.g. 3:1 vs 2:1): no controlled evidence was found. UNKNOWN. Undulating periodization in runners: UNKNOWN from this base.

Key sources

Lolli 2019 BJSM 10.1136/bjsports-2017-098110 · Impellizzeri 2020 IJSPP 15:907 · Impellizzeri 2021 Sports Med 51:581 (iris.univr.it/handle/11562/1139126) · Coyne et al. (ro.ecu.edu.au/ecuworkspost2013/7321) · Rosenblat 2025 doi:10.1007/s40279-024-02149-3 · PubMed 29182410 · Burnley et al. 2022 MSSE (sponet.de 4076014) · hal.inrae.fr/hal-04999009 · arxiv.org/abs/1902.02061 · Kiely 2010 (clok.uclan.ac.uk/5139) · Almquist 2022 doi:10.3389/fphys.2022.837634 · Frontiers 2025 doi:10.3389/fspor.2025.1520914 · acefitness.org/continuing-education/certified/research/7466

hearing-1-phys-field.md

Hearing 1 — Exercise physiology & training science (phys) — FIELD base

Witness informs only; no recommendation. Base = coaches' published methods, practitioner sites, platform docs, forums. Process limits: 8 web searches run; the shared search budget then ran out (2 queries blocked: deload practice, critical-speed field tests). Opening pages failed (DNS errors on every fetch), so no claim is VERIFIED; all grades rest on search-result text, mostly secondary summaries of books. Book figures (Daniels, Pfitzinger, Hansons, Koop) should be checked in the books.

Q1. Determinants by distance

  • Field practice says specificity rises with distance and toward the race. Canova's "funnel": general work first, narrowing toward race pace. Marathon "special" blocks of 6–8 weeks centre on 90% and 110% of marathon pace (MP); long runs go up to 35–40 km with large parts near MP. CORROBORATED (two secondary sources). Strength: majority view among coaches.
  • Ultra (Koop): "an ultra is not a longer marathon". Ordered priorities are aerobic engine → terrain → fuelling → course specifics. Koop's own sequencing runs from the least race-like work to the most race-like. CREDIBLE (book reviews + interview); the reverse-sequencing detail is ALLEGED (one interview).
  • Base gap: the field base does not quantify how much VO2max, threshold, economy or fat oxidation matter at each distance. That needs the acad base.

Q2. Intensity distribution

  • 80/20 (Fitzgerald, 80/20 Running, 2014) turns Seiler's observations of elite athletes into a rule of about 80% low and 20% moderate-to-high intensity, treated as flexible (78/22 vs 82/18 said to make little difference). CORROBORATED that this is the practice; the claim that it is better for amateurs is ALLEGED in field sources. The "amateurs train too much in the middle" claim is CREDIBLE.
  • Threshold-heavy / Norwegian (Bakken): lactate is held at 2.3–3.0 mmol/L (one source says ≤3), with double threshold twice a week plus one harder "X" session. CORROBORATED (LetsRun, Science of Running, Marathon Handbook). It needs a lactate meter, which most runners lack, and no one has shown whether HR can stand in for it: ALLEGED (RunnersConnect only). Whether it suits low-volume amateurs is UNKNOWN in this base.
  • Strength: contested. Schools coexist; field sources do not settle the question.

Q3. Load models and progression

  • TrainingPeaks PMC: CTL is an exponentially weighted ~42-day average of TSS, ATL ~7 days, TSB = CTL − ATL. CORROBORATED.
  • CTL ramp rate: TrainingPeaks says 4–8 points/week is "safe" and >8 often leads to injury. Friel gives 5–8; one coach uses 3–5 long-term. Couzens scales with CTL: about 4–6 at low CTL (<45), 8–10 at high CTL (>70). CORROBORATED as practitioner convention. None of these thresholds is validated for injury. Strength: practitioner majority, not science.
  • TSB targets: race day +10 to +25; normal training −10 to −30. ALLEGED (one third-party guide plus forums). TrainingPeaks itself publishes no table and says "let your body be the guide".
  • ACWR, monotony: none of my searches found field material on them → UNKNOWN in this base.

Q4. Intensity anchoring

  • Race-result anchoring (Daniels VDOT): paces E/M/T/I/R come from a recent race. Approximate %VO2max: E ~59–74%, M ~82%, T ~83–88%, I ~100%, R faster than I. CORROBORATED (several secondary sources, numbers vary).
  • HR anchoring (Friel): a solo 30-min all-out run; the average HR of the last 20 min is taken as LTHR, and the average pace as threshold pace. Run zones as % of LTHR: Z1 <85, Z2 85–89, Z3 90–94, Z4 95–99, Z5a 100–102, Z5b 103–106. CORROBORATED. Known errors: starting too fast; a 95% correction appears in the book but not on the blog (ALLEGED). Field-test accuracy against the lab is UNKNOWN here.
  • Lactate: practical only with a meter (CREDIBLE). RPE/talk test and critical speed: UNKNOWN (searches not run).

Q5. Taper, deload, periodization

  • Taper length: Pfitzinger about 3 weeks; Hansons about 10 days (shorter last long run, speedwork dropped in the final week). ALLEGED (forum only).
  • Volume cut: a total of 41–60%, applied progressively (first week only −10–15%), with intensity and frequency kept. CORROBORATED (RunnersConnect citing a 2023 review; a UK coaching page gives 40–60%). The older rule of 60% then 30% of normal volume in the last two weeks is ALLEGED.
  • Periodization: linear base→specific (Lydiard lineage) versus funnel (Canova) versus reverse (Koop). Only funnel is CORROBORATED here. Block and undulating models: UNKNOWN in this base.
  • Deload frequency (3:1, 2:1): UNKNOWN; the search was blocked.

What this base cannot see

Controlled comparisons, injury outcomes, effect sizes, applicability by sex and age, and validation of PMC/ramp thresholds. All of these belong to the acad and crit witnesses.

Sources (all secondary or practitioner; none opened)
