hearing-1-sys-acad.md

Hearing 1 — Knowledge modelling & adaptive systems (sys) — academic & official base

Access limits (read first): 5 WebSearch queries ran before the shared search budget ran out (the brief asked for 6–10). Primary sources could not be opened because the egress proxy blocked PubMed, NCBI, BMC, JSSM and MDPI. No claim below is VERIFIED. Abstracts plus prior knowledge give at most CREDIBLE; (prior knowledge) marks items not re-checked.

What this base cannot see: product internals, user-outcome data, coach heuristics, grey literature (field/crit bases).

Q1 — Ways to formalise training knowledge

  • Ontologies + rules (OWL/SWRL): used for exercise prescription: UFIT (plans from profile + screening) (PMC4003957); EXMO (434 classes, ~9.7k axioms, reasoner-checked); a weightlifting ontology (1,761 axioms, 23 SWRL rules; sport ontologies "rare") (ceur-ws Vol-1486, minerva.usc.gal). Strength: shared vocabulary, consistency checks. Weakness: weak at numeric optimisation; mostly clinical/elderly, not endurance. Emerging; CREDIBLE.
  • Constraint and optimisation methods: Kumyaito et al. 2018 used adaptive particle swarm optimisation with ε-constraints on monotony, CTL ramp rate and daily TRIMP. In simulation it beat a British Cycling plan (doi:10.1186/s13104-017-3120-9). Others: adaptation as optimal control via evolutionary computation (J Sci Med Sport 25(4); UCD NCRA) or dynamic programming on a response model (Springer 2015). Limit: only as good as the load-response model (Banister/Busso); validated in simulation only. Emerging; CREDIBLE.
  • AI planning and case-based reasoning: Škeřík, Chrpa, Faber & Vallati (IEEE SMC 2018) used automated planning (kickboxing). Plans more detailed than experts', faster (Huddersfield). Genetic algorithms imitated one swim coach (PACSS 2021); a hybrid expert-system/case-based reasoner (scitepress 2018). All are small-scale. ALLEGED to CREDIBLE.
  • Competence/skill graphs for running: I found no academic source. UNKNOWN.
  • Trade-off: rules/ontologies describe and screen; optimisation sets numbers. No head-to-head comparison exists. Unknown.

Q2 — How products work (Runna, TrainAsONE, Garmin Coach, TrainingPeaks)

No peer-reviewed description or evaluation of these algorithms found. Garmin Coach as coach-authored (Galloway, McMillan, Parkerson-Mitchell) and TrainAsONE as ML-based are vendor statements (prior knowledge). UNKNOWN in this base; refer to field.

Q3 — Effectiveness and safety of algorithmic or AI-generated plans

  • LLM-generated running plans: Düking et al., J Sports Sci Med 2024;23:56–72 (jssm-23-56). 10 coaches rated ChatGPT plans sub-optimal; quality rose with more input; authors advise expert review.
  • LLM-generated strength plans: Havers et al., Biol Sport 2025, two studies (PMC12492345, termedia 55336). Quality moderate; repeated prompts gave similar quality but different content (non-deterministic).
  • Together these show majority view, CORROBORATED (independent groups, two sports): LLM plans fall below expert standard and vary from run to run.
  • Optimisation or ML plans: evidence is simulation or case studies only. Example: an HES-SO cycling virtual coach that matched or beat control plans over 14 and 24 weeks (arodes). ALLEGED.
  • Injury, adherence and outcomes: No RCT/cohort on injury or adherence with adaptive running apps found. A 2025 narrative review flags homogeneous datasets, low transparency (PMC12566783). UNKNOWN.

Q4 — Deterministic, conflict-free, explainable, testable rule sets (prior knowledge, not re-checked)

  • Conflict resolution in production systems: priority/salience, specificity and recency strategies. Reference implementations: Rete (Forgy 1982), CLIPS, Drools documentation.
  • Decision tables: OMG DMN standardises "hit policies" (Unique, First, Priority, Collect), which make each table's outcome explicit and deterministic.
  • Knowledge-base verification: anomaly classes (redundancy, conflict, subsumption, circularity, missing rules) are standard checks (Preece & Shinghal 1994).
  • Explanation: rule traces (which rule fired, why) are the standard basis; reasoners check consistency.
  • Evidence strength: consensus in software engineering; CREDIBLE (two or more independent sources known, not opened this session). Not specific to sport.
  • Simulation testing: testing plans against a load model (CTL ramp, monotony) is the method the optimisation papers use (Kumyaito; UCD). CREDIBLE.

Q5 — Data model and interoperability (prior knowledge)

  • Common entities: the ontology and optimisation papers repeatedly include athlete profile/screening, goal/event, plan → phase → week → session → interval, and daily load (TRIMP/sRPE) with derived CTL/ATL/monotony. CREDIBLE.
  • Interoperability:
  • Garmin FIT SDK (has a structured "workout" file type)
  • TCX
  • GPX (tracks only)
  • HL7 FHIR Observation / physical-activity profiles
  • Open mHealth schemas

These are official specs, but none defines a cross-vendor training plan standard. CREDIBLE; plan-level standard UNKNOWN or absent.

Overall strength of evidence: emerging for formalisation methods; majority view that LLM plans are sub-optimal; unknown for real-world outcomes.

hearing-1-sys-crit.md

Hearing 1 — Knowledge modelling & adaptive systems (sys) — critical & independent base (crit)

Process note: I ran 5 searches; 4 returned results. Search then hit the shared turn budget, and every web-fetch failed on DNS. No primary source was opened, so nothing below is VERIFIED. Claims I hold from memory carry citations but no URL I could check here. Treat them as CREDIBLE at most until the fact desk checks them.

Q1 — Formalisms (rules, constraints, graphs, ontologies): trade-offs

  • My critical base holds no independent comparison of these formalisms for running-plan generation. Strength: unknown.
  • The critical literature attacks the quantitative cores that generators put inside these formalisms:
  • ACWR (acute:chronic workload ratio). The usual calculation counts the latest week in both the acute and chronic loads. This builds in a spurious correlation of about r ≈ 0.50 (Lolli et al., BJSM 2019;53:921; Windt & Gabbett's editorial, BJSM 2019;53:988, simulated n = 1,000, r = 0.52). Impellizzeri et al. (Sports Med 2021;51:581, "Time to dismiss ACWR…") replaced chronic load with made-up values and still got injury associations. They read the ACWR signal as a statistical artefact. One ECU case study found the coupling effects trivial (g = 0.04–0.21). Contested, majority of critics against. Grade: CORROBORATED (search snippets from ≥2 independent groups). URLs: link · link · link · link
  • Banister fitness-fatigue model. Marchal … Imbach (Sci Rep 2025) found the model ill-conditioned. Its fatigue parameters were poorly identifiable, and adding them did not improve prediction (p > 0.40), so the model overfits. Swinton et al. (2021) list linearity, independence and determinism as its core limits. An IJSPP 2022 commentary reports that fitted parameters depend on the starting values, the fitting technique and the input. Stephens Hemingway et al. (2020) found that performance measures with a coefficient of variation (CV) above ≈4% give unsatisfactory fits. Self-coached runners have no lab-grade tests, so their measures will usually be noisier than this. Emerging→majority view that the model is unreliable for individual prediction. Grade: CORROBORATED. URLs: link · link · link
  • Blind spot: critics test the load models, not the structure of rule bases, competence graphs or ontologies.

Q2 — How commercial products generate and adapt plans

  • Runna's published claim is a "43% lower self-reported injury rate per 1,000 h vs industry benchmarks". That is the company's own figure and has no peer review. Grade: ALLEGED. Runna has announced an algorithm review with Loughborough University (pace progression, intensity distribution, recovery). The trade press reports physiotherapists seeing several Runna-related injury cases a week (via WSJ). That report came second-hand and is single-origin, so ALLEGED. Runna has since added controls to reduce plan intensity (CREDIBLE, reported by ≥2 outlets: link · link · link).
  • For TrainAsONE, Garmin Coach and TrainingPeaks-based tools, I found no independent technical documentation and no evaluation. The algorithms are proprietary. Strength: unknown.

Q3 — Effectiveness and safety of algorithmic or AI plans

  • Düking et al. 2024 (J Sports Sci Med 23:56–72, doi:10.52082/jssm.2024.56):
  • Design: three 6-week ChatGPT plans, each from a more detailed prompt, rated by expert coaches on 22 criteria.
  • Result: the plan from the least detailed prompt was rated significantly lower than the most detailed one on 15 of 22 criteria. The authors advise against using such plans without review by an expert coach.
  • Outcome measure: expert ratings only. No runner outcomes were measured.
  • Grade: CORROBORATED (journal page, PMC and independent press). link · link
  • Dutch injury-prevention app RCT (n = 425 marathon trainees): no significant effect on running-related injuries (RRIs) or chronic fatigue, and low adherence. Grade: CREDIBLE (repository summary only). link
  • No peer-reviewed study compares injury rates of app-adaptive plans with static plans. Strength: unknown. A registered feasibility trial covers adherence to individualised recommendations (NCT06538363).
  • From memory, not re-checked: the RCT of the "10% rule" (Buist et al., AJSM 2008;36:33) found no difference in injury rate (≈20.8% vs 20.3%). Nielsen et al. (JOSPT 2014;44:739) linked weekly progression above 30% to more of some injury types. These matter because rule engines often hard-code such thresholds. Grade: CREDIBLE (two independent groups).

Q4 — Deterministic, conflict-free, testable rule sets

  • My base holds no running-specific critical literature on this. General points (rule priority/salience, the brittleness of expert systems, regression test suites) come from software-engineering practice, not this base. Strength: unknown.
  • The base implies one point, carried over from Q1. The thresholds rules most often use (ACWR 0.8–1.3, the 10% rule, Banister τ ≈ 42/7 days) have contested or weak validity. Thresholds can be deterministic and testable but still have no evidence that they protect runners.

Q5 — Data model and interoperability

  • Not covered by this base. Critical sources do not evaluate FIT, TCX, GPX or Open mHealth schemas. Strength: unknown.

What this base cannot see

  • Proprietary algorithms.
  • Internal company data.
  • Unpublished negative results.
  • Long-term outcomes for users of any app.
  • Formal-methods literature outside sport.
hearing-1-sys-field.md

Hearing 1 — Knowledge modelling & adaptive systems (sys) — FIELD base

Process limits: 8 WebSearch queries ran before the shared search budget ran out. WebFetch could not resolve any host, so no primary source was opened and nothing here is VERIFIED. The best grade is CORROBORATED: two independent sources surfaced by search agree.

Q1. Formalising training knowledge

  • The pattern visible in products is coach-authored templates plus algorithmic tailoring, not end-to-end learned models. Runna says its plans are "designed by experienced coaches", with technology in "a supporting role" (TechRadar; the5krunner). Garmin's plans carry coach names: Galloway, McMillan, Parkerson-Mitchell (Garmin blog). Grade: CORROBORATED; strength: majority view. TrainAsONE claims ML on real-world data plus "human-in-the-loop". That claim is vendor-only, so ALLEGED.
  • No field documentation was found for constraint-solver, ontology or competence-graph engines in running products. This is UNKNOWN, not proof that none exist.

Q2. How products adapt (public docs only; thresholds undisclosed)

ProductInputsAdaptationGrade
Runnarace time, experience, days/week, distance, dateOne race-time estimate sets every pace. Paces shift only after a consistent pattern of missing or beating targets; outlier reps are ignored. A "Not feeling 100%" path covers ill, ache or short of time. Adapt for Heat (Jul 2026) adjusts pace by feels-like temperature, duration and session.CORROBORATED (the5krunner ×2, 9to5mac, Wareable, Engadget)
Garmin Run Coach / DSWgoal date, days, VO2max estimate, acute/chronic load (Firstbeat EPOC), recovery time, HRV/sleepAdjusts both ways: harder after strong benchmarks, easier after misses or fatigue.Inputs CORROBORATED (BusinessWire 2020, the5krunner, wiki). Two-way logic ALLEGED (one review). Readiness bands (73–100 / <34) ALLEGED (one blog).
TrainAsONEindividual model; acute/chronic loadA user Risk Tolerance setting: lower means a slower ramp. "Injured" and "Return to running" modes. Full replan after interruptions.ALLEGED (vendor only)
TrainingPeaks PMCTSS/rTSSCTL is a ~42-day exponentially weighted average; ATL ~7 days; TSB = CTL − ATL. Ramp of ~4–8 CTL points per week (Friel 5–8); above 8 "often leads to injury".Definitions CORROBORATED. Ramp figures are CORROBORATED practitioner guidance; their empirical basis is UNKNOWN. TrainAsONE disputes TSB models: CONTESTED.

Q3. Effectiveness and safety

  • ChatGPT plans improve with richer input but are not rated optimal by coaches. They lack monitoring, motivation and adjustment (JSSM 2024; Marathon Handbook; SPB). Grade: CORROBORATED; strength: emerging, small samples.
  • No peer-reviewed comparison of injury rates between app users and static-plan users was found (the5krunner Feb 2026; TechRadar). Injury reports from app users are anecdotal, and the view that beginners are most exposed is opinion. Status: UNKNOWN. No adherence data was found.

Q4. Deterministic, explainable rule sets (engineering practice)

  • Rule engines resolve conflicts by salience (numeric priority, default 0), with agenda/activation groups as further controls. Rules with equal priority fire in arbitrary or "first found" order. The docs advise against relying on firing order (Red Hat Drools; Grule). Grade: CORROBORATED.
  • Oscillation and contradictory actions are named failure modes. One source says explanations should be bound to the exact rule version in force at evaluation (gravitee). Grade: ALLEGED.
  • Decision-table completeness and overlap checks (DMN hit policies), test suites and simulation were not reached: UNKNOWN.

Q5. Data model and interoperability

  • FIT (Garmin SDK) is the de facto device format. Its messages cover activities, courses and workouts, and it is designed for interoperability and forward compatibility (developer.garmin.com/fit). Developers report undocumented requirements outside run, bike and swim (Garmin forum). Vendor extensions do not carry across platforms. Grade: CREDIBLE.
  • Recurring entities across products: athlete profile (ability, experience, days available), goal event (date, distance, primary/secondary), structured sessions, load series (acute/chronic/balance), readiness (HRV, sleep), and status flags (ill, injured). CREDIBLE (inferred; no published schema). TCX, GPX and plan-level standards: UNKNOWN.

What this base cannot see

Proprietary algorithms and thresholds, internal validation, injury and adherence telemetry, academic prototypes.

Sources

Press: the5krunner.com (2026/09/28 is-runna-ai; 2026/07/29 Adapt for Heat; 2026/02/21 injury; daily-suggested-workouts), 9to5mac, Wareable, Engadget, TechRadar, shoulditrain.com. Vendor: trainasone.com/safety and risk-tolerance FAQ; garmin.com blog; businesswire.com 20200916005003; trainingpeaks.com glossary; developer.garmin.com/fit/overview. Practitioner: support.myprocoach.net 360040589772; roadmancycling.com; marathonhandbook.com. Peer-reviewed: jssm.org/jssm-23-56. Eng: docs.redhat.com (Drools); github.com/hyperjumptech/grule-rule-engine; gravitee.io; forums.garmin.com fit-sdk 425683.

hearing-2-coach-acad.md

Hearing 2 — Coaching practice 5K→100K road — source base: ACADEMIC & OFFICIAL

This witness informs only. It makes no recommendation and prefers no school. Process limits: I ran 8 WebSearch queries, the full shared budget. Every attempt to fetch a primary source failed with DNS errors (PMC, Frontiers, EUR repository). No claim below is VERIFIED. Grades rest on abstracts, repository records and search snippets. A claim with only one origin is graded ALLEGED.

Q1. A default 3-run week for self-coached 5K/10K runners

  • No academic study tests runners who train 3 days a week for 5K/10K. What the base holds is intensity-distribution trials in recreational runners who trained more often. Grade: UNKNOWN for the 3-run case.
  • Intensity split (recreational runners). Muñoz et al. 2014 (IJSPP, n=30, 10K) compared polarized training with between-thresholds training. 10K times improved by 5.0 % and 3.6 %, a difference that was not significant. Festa et al. 2019 (n=38, 8 weeks) used zone 1/2/3 splits of 77/3/20 (polarized) and 40/50/10 (focused endurance). Both groups improved, and the authors state that the optimal distribution for recreational runners is unknown. Evidence: contested, with small trials. Grade: CORROBORATED that both splits improve performance and that neither has been shown superior. PubMed 23752040; Festa 2019, Front Sports Act Living, doi:10.3389/fspor.2019.00070
  • Share of time at threshold or faster. The trial arms span about 10–20 % in zone 3 (above the second threshold) and 13–60 % at or above the first threshold. These are research protocols, not coaches' defaults. Grade: CREDIBLE as a range of tested doses.
  • The "two quality runs plus one long run" school (FIRST, Furman). FIRST prescribes 3 runs a week (speed, tempo, long) plus 2 cross-training sessions. Its own 2004–05 studies report better physiology and better marathon times in veteran marathoners. A secondary source gives about +4.2 % VO2max (n=21). The evidence comes from the originator and is marathon-focused. Grade: ALLEGED. furman.edu/first
  • The "one quality run" school (1 hard run plus 2 easy runs) appears in coaching literature (Norris) but has no academic test. Grade: ALLEGED. The field base will see what coaches actually prescribe better than this base can.

Q2. Long-run cap for slow marathoners (5.5–6.5 h finish): by time or by distance?

  • No study tests a time cap such as 2.5–3 h. The 3 h cap is coaching practice. The rationale usually given (glycogen depletion, muscle damage, slower recovery) is inferred from mechanistic studies: Nieman's lab, where greater glycogen loss went with more cytokine release and slower recovery after runs to exhaustion, and a small study of n=6 marathoners whose glycogen was still low at day 2 and back to normal by day 7. Neither study tests a cap. Grade: UNKNOWN for the cap itself and ALLEGED for the mechanism as applied to it.
  • The distance evidence is observational. Fokkema et al. 2020 (Scand J Med Sci Sports, n=997, of whom 441 were marathoners) found that a longest run <25 km was associated with slower marathon times, and that more than 65 km/week was associated with faster times. In the half-marathon group, a longest run >21 km was associated with a finish about 3.9 min faster. No training variable was associated with injury. Non-finishers were excluded and causality is unclear. A meta-regression (J Sci Med Sport 2020;23:182, 85 studies, 137 cohorts, 25 % female) found that the longest run and the number of runs ≥32 km were each associated with faster finishes (R² 0.38–0.81). A secondary summary says runners aiming for about 4 h "need not exceed 32 km". That last point is ALLEGED because I could not open the paper. Taken together: longer longest run goes with faster finish = CORROBORATED, majority view, associational only. PMC7496388; PubMed 31704026
  • Neither source reports subgroups at 5.5–6.5 h. For these runners, 25–32 km takes about 3–4.5 h, so the time cap and the distance targets conflict. No academic source resolves this conflict. Evidence: unknown.

Q3. Road ultra (50K/100K) practice

  • Weekly volume predicts 100 km time better than training pace does. The sources are Tanda & Knechtle 2015 and Knechtle et al. 2010 (n=66 recreational men). For elite athletes, Citarella 2021 found weekly km to be the only association that survived adjustment. Grade: CORROBORATED, although the Knechtle papers share a research group, so independence is partial. Evidence: majority view, observational. PMC4425319
  • A Knechtle-group study found that ultra and marathon runners train more often, longer and further than shorter-distance runners. This includes the NURMI survey (n=2,864) and a 2023 study (n=154). The per-group figures for weekly km and longest run were not retrievable. Grade: CREDIBLE. NURMI, doi:10.3389/fphys.2021.620404
  • Longest run, long-run share of the week, cadence of peak long runs, back-to-backs: I found no peer-reviewed data for road ultras. The only material is coaching media, for example a longest run of about 30 mi (about 60 % of the race) for a 50-miler on 40–50 mi/week, and divided opinion on back-to-backs. Grade: ALLEGED. Evidence: unknown.
  • Masters runners: age correlates positively with 100 km time (Knechtle 2010). I found no academic data on how masters runners train for ultras or how they recover. Grade: UNKNOWN.

What this base cannot see

  • Coaches' default week templates.
  • How time caps on long runs are used in practice.
  • Ultra-specific long-run structure.
  • Masters ultra practice.
  • Outcome data for runners slower than about 4.5 h.

The academic literature is observational, it excludes non-finishers, and it leans towards faster and male cohorts.

hearing-2-coach-field.md

Hearing 2 — Coaching practice 5K→100K road · base: FIELD

The witness informs only and prefers no school. Limits:

  • 8 searches (the full budget). The one page fetch failed (DNS), so nothing here is VERIFIED.
  • Sources are coach blogs, plan-store listings (TrainingPeaks, Final Surge), forums and book blurbs. They are not books or outcome data.
  • [mem] = the witness's own knowledge, graded ALLEGED.

Q1. The default 3-run week for self-coached 5K/10K runners

Two patterns are published, and they differ on purpose:

PatternWeekGrade
A. 1 quality + 1 long + 1 easyInterval or tempo, alternating week to week. Long run. Easy run. Active.com coaches describe an interval session, a long run and 2–3 easy runs as the full week; the 3-run version drops easy runs. Zwift's "3Run" plan (written for the half marathon) uses interval + tempo + long, with a 5K test every 4th week to update pacesCORROBORATED as common practice (active.com/running/Articles/Teach-Yourself-How-to-Run-Better-This-Year.htm; zwift.com/uk/news/10537-3-run-13-1-training-plan)
B. 3 quality runs (FIRST, "Run Less Run Faster")Track repeats + tempo + long run at set paces, plus 2 cross-training sessions. Plans cover 5K, 10K, half and marathonCORROBORATED that the structure exists (3rd ed., Rodale 2021; bookloft.com/book/9780593232231; worldcat 1151739146). It is a minority, named school, and it assumes 2 extra aerobic cross-training days

Share of time at threshold or faster:

  • No field source states this figure for a 3-run week: UNKNOWN as a published number.
  • Reference points:
  • 80/20 (Fitzgerald) puts about 20% at moderate-to-high intensity. Its 5K Level 3 plans expect at least 3 runs a week and target experienced runners, some training twice a day (trainingpeaks.com tp-107691). CREDIBLE.
  • Forum rule of thumb: 10–15% quality. ALLEGED (hearing 1).
  • Derived, not sourced: with pattern A, one session of 20–30 min of work in a 2.5–3.5 h week gives about 10–20%. Pattern B (FIRST) is plausibly 30–40%+ of running time, because the long run is also paced.
  • Strength of evidence: contested (two schools).

Q2. Long runs for slow marathoners (5.5–6.5 h finish)

  • Field practice caps by time, not distance. Majority view, CORROBORATED by:
  • coachtherun.com/?p=4984: plans are written in time; a 5 h marathoner given 20 mi would be out for 4 h or more.
  • marathonhandbook.com/how-youre-screwing-up-your-long-runs/: when 13 mi takes more than 3 h, think in time; 2.5–3 h is generally enough.
  • runningahead forum (post/1fb63d…): reports that McMillan and Hashizume advise 3 h or less, and that Daniels limits novices to 2.5 h. This is second-hand.
  • Hearing 1: Daniels's cap is the lower of 25–30% of weekly volume or about 150 min. CORROBORATED.
  • The standard band is 2.5–3 h (CORROBORATED). No field source sets a 30–32 km distance cap for slow runners. That distance cap belongs to time-unaware plans (Higdon's 20 mi ≈ 32 km), which apply it to every finish time. [mem] ALLEGED.
  • Contested figures:
  • McMillan (mcmillanrunning.com/?p=141438, fetch failed): the search summary says his longest run for 5 h+ marathoners stays below race time, roughly 4:00–4:30. This conflicts with the forum's "3 h or less" attribution, so it is CONTRADICTED until the page is opened.
  • Coachtherun allows an occasional 3–4 h confidence run. ALLEGED (one source).

Q3. Road ultra (50K/100K) practice

50K:

  • Higdon's 26-week plan mirrors his Intermediate-2 marathon plan, peaks with three 20 mi (32 km) runs, and pairs most long runs with a semi-long run the next day (trainingpeaks.com tp-117679). CREDIBLE.
  • Taylor (finalsurge.com/coach/AndrewTaylor/plan/45053): peak 50–52 mi (80–84 km) a week, longest run 26 mi (42 km) with a back-to-back progression, entry at 35–40 mi a week. CREDIBLE.
  • Synthesis: longest run 32–42 km, peak 65–85 km a week. CORROBORATED (two plans plus an unsourced outline of 30–40 mi with an 18–20 mi long run).

100K (plan listings; the terrain is often trail or "runnable"):

PlanLongest runBack-to-backPeak week
Intermediate5:002:55~14.1 h
Advanced5:303:30~16.5 h
Runnable/flat (RunForce)6:003:00~12.7 h
  • Sources: trainingpeaks tp-680866, tp-680867, tp-680383.
  • Synthesis: longest run 5–6 h, back-to-back day 2 at about 55–60% of day 1, peak week 12.5–16.5 h. CORROBORATED.
  • Koop's figure of 10–12 h a week for 100 mi (hearing 1) is consistent with this.
  • Long-run share of the week: derived as 5–6 h of 12.5–16.5 h ≈ 35–45%, or 55–65% with the back-to-back included. The weekend pair is the core of the week. This is derived, not stated.
  • Comrades (road, about 87–90 km), Coach Parry:
  • Finishers aiming for 10.5–12 h peak at 60–80 km a week.
  • The same blog gives an average of 39–45 km a week, and 850–1000 km in the 6 months before the race.
  • Beginner plans have runs up to 4 h, 4–5 runs a week and a 3-week taper.
  • Grade: CREDIBLE (blog.coachparry.com/?p=2708; trainingpeaks tp-371007). Coach Parry is effectively a single coaching house.
  • How often peak long runs come: the 50K plans show 3 peak long runs. The 3-up/1-down pattern with about 10% weekly increases is quoted by one coach (relentlessforwardcommotion.com). CREDIBLE. The exact spacing is UNKNOWN.
  • Back-to-backs: standard in ultra plans, though "not everyone believes" in them (same source). Majority view.

Masters runners:

  • A 50-mile masters plan for runners 40+ replaces the 7-day week with a 9-day rolling microcycle. Its stated reason: spacing hard sessions and back-to-backs, while keeping 6 training days (finalsurge.com/coach/fowlerendurance/plan/110925). ALLEGED (one source).
  • Running Past 50 (MacMahon, Human Kinetics) covers recovery, but its content was not retrieved.
  • No field source gives masters-specific caps on long runs or volume: UNKNOWN.

What this base cannot see

  • Injury or completion outcomes for any of these caps.
  • Plans from pure road 100K coaching outside the Comrades context.
  • Primary books (FIRST, McMillan, Koop).
  • Whether the time caps were tested, or are folklore that has converged.
hearing-2-med-acad.md

Hearing 2 — Sports medicine & injury prevention (acad)

Access limits: 6 searches were used. Every page fetch failed with a DNS error, so nothing here is VERIFIED. [S] means the item comes from this session's search snippets or abstracts. [M] means it comes from the witness's prior knowledge; these items are ALLEGED until the fact desk opens them. What this base cannot see: coaching folklore on time caps, app or wearable data that has not been published, and outcomes for runners who drop out.

Q1 Single-run DURATION and injury

  • No study was found that isolates single-run duration (for example >2.5–3 h or >3–4 h) as an injury exposure in marathon or ultra runners. Evidence: unknown. Any duration cap is therefore expert opinion or practice, not evidence.
  • The closest signal concerns single-session distance. In Garmin-RUNSAFE (Frandsen 2025, BJSM, n≈5,200), risk rose when a run exceeded the longest run of the previous 30 days by more than 10%. A secondary summary gives about +128% risk when that distance is doubled or more. Weekly ratios showed no association or an inverse one. [S] doi:10.1136/bjsports-2024-109380. Emerging (one cohort); CREDIBLE. Transfer to duration-based caps is untested.
  • In a Dutch cohort of 556 half-marathon and 441 marathon entrants, a higher training volume and long endurance runs were linked to faster finish times and not to more injury [S] (link authors not confirmed). ALLEGED (one source, not opened).
  • Ultra runners:
  • In Two Oceans (prospective, n=119, 16 weeks), injury ran at about 5 per 1000 training hours. Load was associated with injury only in runners doing less than 30 km/week, and with ACWR above 1.5 [S] (PMC9924505; SAJSM).
  • In Comrades (retrospective, n=106), injury ran at about 8 per 1000 h and the 14-week injury proportion was 40% [S].
  • Neither study analysed session duration. CREDIBLE for incidence; unknown for duration.

Q2 About 5 weeks from 1:2 run-walk to 30 min continuous, for true novices

  • No RCT tests the length of a run-walk progression. Unknown.
  • Couch-to-5K (9 weeks, modified version; Relph et al. 2023, IJERPH 20:6682, n=110):
  • 19% were injured during the programme.
  • Only 27% completed it. [S] doi:10.3390/ijerph20176682. CREDIBLE, single study.
  • Previous injury predicted new injury (OR 7.56, 95% CI 2.06–27.75).
  • GRONORUN (Buist 2008, AJSM, n=532) compared a 13-week graded plan following the 10% rule with an 8-week standard plan. Injury incidence was 20.8% vs 20.3%, so a slower ramp did not reduce injury [S] (PEDro 20220; PMC1821023 protocol). CORROBORATED.
  • Implication for evidence strength: no trial supports 5 weeks over 8–9 weeks, and none shows that it is unsafe. In novices, injury runs at about 20% across programme lengths and dropout at more than 70% in a real-world C25K cohort. Contested / unknown for the specific 5-week timeline.

Q3 Masters (≥50, ≥65): considerations for long runs and ultras

  • Atrial fibrillation: high lifelong endurance volume is linked to earlier AF, more in men. One older meta-analysis reports roughly 5× the odds versus non-athletes [S] (narrative review, Life 2025, 15:1095; PMC12300461). Majority view on the association; dose contested.
  • Coronary calcification (CAC): CAC scores are higher in masters athletes with high lifetime volume. Plaques may be more calcified and stable, so the net risk is contested [S] (Life 2025; USP Rev Med narrative review). The 2025 review proposes a preventive cardiac assessment framework (CAC, AF, mild aortic dilation) for masters athletes [S]. Expert opinion.
  • Bone: masters bone stress injuries come from two overlapping mechanisms: overload with insufficient recovery, and insufficiency fractures in age-thinned bone. The risk is highest in peri- and post-menopausal women. Prevention rests on adequate energy, calcium and vitamin D, resistance and impact loading, and medical review, including HRT or bisphosphonates where indicated [S] (Raiser et al. 2024, PM&R 16:363, doi:10.1002/pmrj.13175; AAPM&R CME summary). Expert narrative; prospective data are lacking.
  • Recovery and deload frequency: no academic source was found that sets a deload frequency by age (for example every 2nd or 3rd week). Unknown/emerging. Any age-based deload rule is practice-derived.
  • Screening: under ACSM 2015, age alone does not trigger clearance [M; see hearing 1]. In masters, the cardiac red flags (chest pain, syncope, palpitations or AF symptoms, disproportionate breathlessness) carry higher prior probability. Consensus on red flags.
hearing-2-med-crit.md

Hearing 2 — Sports medicine & injury prevention — CRITICAL base (med-crit)

Caveat. 7 searches. PMC unreachable (DNS / proxy 403): no primary source opened, nothing VERIFIED. (s) = confirmed by this session's searches.

Q1. Single-run DURATION and injury

  • No study found isolating single-session duration (> 2.5–3 h or > 3–4 h) as an injury exposure in marathon/ultra runners. Strength: unknown. Grade: UNKNOWN (absence of evidence, own searches only).
  • Closest signal is distance-relative spikes: Frandsen 2025 BJSM (n = 5,205): run > 100% above 30-day longest run → HR ≈ 2.28; week-to-week ratio not associated (s). Single cohort → ALLEGED; not a time threshold.
  • Ultra cohorts point to under-preparation: Burgess 2025 SAJSM (Comrades, n = 106, retrospective, GAM incl. duration): 8 injuries/1,000 h, 40% injured; lower 12-week distance → more injury (p = 0.02); load change minimal effect (s). Two Oceans 2019 (same group): 5/1,000 h; load–injury link only < 30 km/week (s). CREDIBLE (not independent).
  • ULTRA study (Hoffman & Krishnan 2014, n = 1,212): 77% injured in 12 months; highest in youngest, least experienced, most intense exercise; no duration analysis (s). CORROBORATED.
  • Rates per hour: novices ≈ 17.8, recreational ≈ 7.7 injuries/1,000 h (Videbæk 2015 meta-analysis) (s). CREDIBLE.
  • Base cannot see: time-on-feet dose–response; walk breaks as moderator.

Q2. ~5 weeks, 1:2 run-walk → 30 min continuous (true novices)

  • Reference: NHS Couch-to-5K = 9 weeks to 30 min (~20 min continuous at week 5). A 5-week ramp is ~2× faster. CREDIBLE.
  • Relph 2023 IJERPH (modified 9-week C25K, n = 110, mean age 47, 82% female): 27.3% completed, 19% injured; prior injury OR 7.56 (95% CI 2.06–27.75); cites a prior C25K incidence of 49% (s). CORROBORATED; 49% figure ALLEGED.
  • GRONORUN RCT (Buist 2008 AJSM, n = 532): graded 13-week (10% rule) vs standard 8-week to 6.7 km → 20.8% vs 20.3% injured (s). Critique: control arm already gradual; novice rates typically 29–58% (s). CORROBORATED. Neither arm tested ≤ 5 weeks.
  • Large relative jumps associate with injury (Nielsen 2014: > 30% over 2 weeks, CREDIBLE; Frandsen, ALLEGED), but slowing an already gradual plan showed no benefit.
  • Drop-out (~70% in C25K) is the larger documented attrition; no data link it to ramp speed.
  • Strength: unknown whether a 5-week ramp is defensible; this base neither supports nor refutes it.

Q3. Masters (≥ 50, ≥ 65)

  • Atrial fibrillation: long-term endurance exposure associated with higher paroxysmal AF risk, especially men; rises with years of exposure (Moser 2025 Life review; Churchill & Baggish 2020) (s). Majority on association, contested on size. CORROBORATED.
  • Coronary calcium: higher CAC in masters athletes; plaque stability contested; Boston MASTER: CAD linked to dyslipidaemia, not exercise dose; ESC/EAPC–ACC 2025 consensus acknowledges contested evidence (s). No volume threshold established. CORROBORATED (as debated).
  • Bone: Raiser 2024 PM&R: masters BSI may combine insufficient recovery and age-weakened bone; evidence "limited" (s). Conference abstract 2025 (n = 100, ≥ 35 y, ≥ 30 km/week): 26% injured, lower femoral-neck BMD in injured (ES ≈ −0.5) (s). ALLEGED.
  • Deload frequency by age (e.g. 2:1 vs 3:1): no trial evidence found; coaching convention. UNKNOWN.
  • Counter-signal: in ULTRA, younger/less experienced runners were injured more (s); age alone is not an established injury multiplier.
  • Base cannot see: ≥ 65 data, women masters (menopause/bone), prospective cardiac outcomes vs ultra volume.

Sources

  • Frandsen 2025: link ; link
  • Burgess 2025: link ; link ; Two Oceans: link
  • Videbæk 2015: link
  • ULTRA: link
  • Relph 2023: link ; link
  • Buist 2008: link ; critique link
  • Moser 2025: link
  • Masters bone: link ; Raiser 2024 doi:10.1002/pmrj.13175
hearing-2-nutr-acad.md

Hearing 2 — Nutrition & fuelling (nutr) — academic & official base (acad)

Access limits: 8 searches used. Every primary-source fetch failed (DNS failure or proxy 403 for bjsm.bmj.com, aafp.org and pubmed). Nothing below is VERIFIED. Grades rest on abstracts, repository records and secondary summaries. What acad cannot see: the evidence base is mostly races, not training runs. No study found measures EAH incidence or fluid behaviour in solo 4–5 h training runs. Cohorts are experienced or trained runners, and slow recreational runners are under-represented. How users follow (or ignore) a tool's advice is outside this base.

Q1. Heat and EAH in 4–5 h training runs: fluid, sodium, CHO

  • Cause. Overdrinking hypotonic fluid, combined with non-osmotic AVP (antidiuretic hormone) secretion, is the primary cause of EAH. EAH is defined as serum Na <135 mmol/L. Sources: 2015 International EAH Consensus (Hew-Butler et al., BJSM 49:1432, doi:10.1136/bjsports-2015-095004) and the 2017 update (Front Med doi:10.3389/fmed.2017.00021). CORROBORATED, consensus.
  • Risk factors (2015 consensus, via secondary summaries):
  • weight gain or weight maintenance during exercise
  • low body weight
  • exercise >4 h
  • easy access to fluids
  • NSAIDs (conflicting data)
  • The apparent female excess disappears after adjusting for BMI and finish time.

CREDIBLE. These come from LITFL and the 2017 update, not the full text. A 4–5 h training run with fluid access falls inside the >4 h band (inference).

  • Prevention. Drink to thirst. The Wilderness Medical Society guideline (Bennett et al., Wilderness Environ Med 2020;31:50–62, via AFP 2021 summary) also says to drink to thirst and avoid overhydration. It states that no specific fluid volume has been shown to prevent EAH. A figure of "generally ≤400–800 mL/h" appears in a secondary summary of the 2015 consensus. Drink-to-thirst: CORROBORATED, consensus. The 400–800 mL/h figure: ALLEGED (one secondary source).
  • Opposing framing. ACSM statements (Sawka 2007; Exertional Heat Illness statement 2021, republished by corrigendum 2023, doi:10.1249/JSR.0000000000000878) say to replace sweat losses at close to the sweat rate, estimated by weighing before and after exercise, and to avoid excessive drinking. CORROBORATED. "Thirst vs. programmed drinking" remains contested. The two camps share two bounds: no body-mass gain and sweat-rate self-test (consensus).
  • Sodium. In Western States 161 km observational data (Hoffman & Stuempfle, MSSE 2015; Sports Med Open 2015 1:39, doi:10.1186/s40798-015-0040-x), supplemental sodium correlated only weakly with serum Na (r=0.24). Sodium intake did not prevent EAH, cramping or nausea, and overhydration was the distinguishing feature. CORROBORATED (two papers, same group, so partly one origin). A counter-review (Front Nutr 2019, doi:10.3389/fnut.2019.00161) argues that the null data come from cooler conditions and that high-salt sweaters may benefit. Contested.
  • Sweat variability. In 157 marathoners at about 24 °C, sweat Na was 42.9 ± 18.7 mmol/L (range 7–95.5). About 20% exceeded 60 mmol/L, and women were lower (PMC4966593, JISSN doi:10.1186/s12970-016-0141-z). In a separate warm-dry cohort, sweat rate was about 0.9–1.0 L/h with ~3% body-mass loss (PubMed 26661748). CORROBORATED (same lab, two cohorts). Sweat rate does not predict sweat Na.
  • Heat-illness overlap. EAH and heat illness have overlapping symptoms. Rectal temperature is used to tell them apart (WMS via AFP). This is a clinical, not a self-care, distinction. CREDIBLE.

Q2. Fuelling during 2.5–5 h training long runs: progression and safe scope for a tool

  • Targets. 30–60 g CHO/h for prolonged exercise, and up to 90 g/h beyond 2.5 h with glucose:fructose blends (Burke et al. 2011; ACSM 2016; GSSI 2024). CORROBORATED, consensus for races. A tiered version (≤30 g/h at 1–2 h, ≤60 g/h at 2–3 h, ≤90 g/h at >2.5 h) is industry (GSSI): ALLEGED as a standard.
  • Gut training. The randomised trials found:
  • Costa et al. 2017 (n=25): 2 weeks of repeated CHO intake during runs at 30 g per 20 min (= 90 g/h). GI symptoms fell 60–63% and distance-test performance rose 4–5% vs placebo.
  • Miall et al. 2018 (n=18, blinded, 90 g/h): fewer symptoms and less malabsorption (breath H₂).
  • The 2023 SR (8 studies) agrees.

CORROBORATED, emerging (small n, trained runners, 2-week protocols). The trials did not set a dose-step progression.

  • Stepwise progression ("start at tolerated intake, +10–15 g/h every ~2 weeks", "2–3 sessions/week") comes only from field/commercial sources. No acad source was found: ALLEGED/UNKNOWN for acad.
  • Train-low. No position stand or RCT was found on periodised train-low for recreational runners. Studied restricted sessions were generally not 2–3 h long runs (secondary review). Low CHO availability is itself a REDs risk (IOC 2023, Hearing 1). Unknown/contested for this population.
  • Tool scope.
  • Position stands publish general ranges (g/h, drink to thirst, sweat-rate testing) for athletes.
  • EAH treatment (fluid restriction, hypertonic saline) and EAH/heat-illness differentiation are medical, per WMS and the EAH consensus.
  • Individual sodium prescription depends on sweat-Na testing, which the sources treat as individual.

CREDIBLE. No acad source evaluates automated or app-delivered fuelling advice: UNKNOWN.

Sources (type)

  • Hew-Butler 2015 consensus: link ; summary link (secondary)
  • EAH 2017 update: link (review)
  • WMS guideline via AFP: link (guideline summary)
  • ACSM EHI statement: link (consensus)
  • Hoffman & Stuempfle 2015: link ; link (observational)
  • Front Nutr 2019: link (review)
  • Sweat Na marathoners: link ; link (observational)
  • Costa 2017: link (RCT)
  • Miall 2018: link (RCT record)
  • GSSI 2024: link (industry)
  • Train-low review: link (secondary, non-acad; context only)
hearing-2-nutr-field.md

Hearing 2 — Nutrition & fuelling — FIELD base (practice & field)

Role: inform only; no recommendation. Searches: 6 of the shared 8. Attempts to open the primary pages (Korey Stringer Institute guide, RunnersConnect) failed (DNS), so nothing here is VERIFIED. Field sources mostly repeat the academic ranges, so agreement between them is often echo, not independent confirmation. Many of the pages are blogs or vendor content.

Q1. Heat and EAH risk on 4–5 h training runs (fluid, sodium, carbohydrate)

  • Mechanism. Practitioners agree that drinking more than you lose, especially plain water, is the main cause of exercise-associated hyponatremia (EAH). They name slower runners, long durations and drinking on a fixed schedule as risk factors (trainingplan.dev; HMRRC Pacesetter; Multiday Running; Hearing 1 sources). CORROBORATED; consensus.
  • Primary fluid rule. Field sources treat "drink to thirst, then adjust for heat, humidity, body size and urine" as the starting point (Multiday Running; trainingplan.dev). CORROBORATED; majority view. Hearing 1 found this contested for long or hot events.
  • Fluid numbers. One source gives 120–240 mL every 15–20 min in the heat, which works out to about 360–960 mL/h (HMRRC/coach blog). Another says sweat rates usually range from about 0.5–1.9 L/h (16–64 oz) (TrainRight/CTS). The suggested kidney ceiling of about 600–800 mL/h was single-source in Hearing 1. CREDIBLE (ranges) / ALLEGED (any fixed ceiling).
  • Sweat-rate test. Practitioners agree on the method: weigh nude before and after about 60 min, then correct for fluid drunk. They also say to repeat it in different conditions, because one test does not cover them all (TrainRight; trainingplan.dev; RunnersConnect). CORROBORATED; consensus.
  • Sodium. Practitioners agree that sodium-containing drinks beat plain water on runs over about 1 h (HMRRC; Multiday). A review relays Twerenbold: overdrinking sodium-containing fluid over 4 h produced less EAH than overdrinking sodium-free fluid. The same review says that in an Ironman, large salt-tablet doses did not protect serum sodium (PMC2657026). CORROBORATED (direction). Hourly numbers come from blogs without primary sources: 500–800 mg/h for marathons and ≥800 mg/h for ultras or heat, and 500–1,000 mg/h, up to 1,500 mg/h for "salty sweaters" (rundreamachieve ×2, same author, so not independent). ALLEGED; CONTESTED. Sweat sodium is quoted at about 400–2,000 mg/L (TrainRight) and 200–>2,000 mg/L (Precision, Hearing 1). CORROBORATED (wide range).
  • Warning signs. EAH can look like dehydration or heat exhaustion. Getting worse while drinking more water points to EAH. Confusion, severe nausea or worsening symptoms mean stop and get medical help (patcarroll / virtualbeauty, same text; HMRRC). CREDIBLE.
  • Heat adjustments in training. All field sources say to slow down or run by HR/RPE in heat. They prefer WBGT or dew point over air temperature. The numbers differ several-fold:
  • Heat index 24–28 °C → slow long runs about 1–2%; 28–32 °C → slow 3–5% or shorten (COROS coaches).
  • Dew point 15.5–18 °C → slow 2–3%; 24–27 °C → slow 12–15% and skip hard sessions (rundida).
  • Dew point >21 °C is called "dangerous" (rundida).
  • Elite WBGT data show slowdowns of about 1.7% to 4.5%, with slower runners hit harder (Outside).
  • Acclimatise over 10–14 days, starting with short easy runs and not long ones (Outside / COROS).

CORROBORATED (direction); CONTESTED (numbers).

  • Carbohydrate in heat. The field base found no heat-specific carbohydrate rule beyond the normal hourly targets.

Q2. Fuelling on 2.5–5 h training long runs; what a tool can safely say

  • Targets. For runs over 2–2.5 h the field consensus is 30–60 g/h (60 g/h for single-source glucose), and up to 90 g/h of glucose+fructose (about 2:1) only after gut training (trainingplan.dev; Black Toe/Maurten; Mother Runners citing AND; Hearing 1). CORROBORATED; majority view. trainingplan.dev calls 45 g/h the "sweet spot" for most runners: ALLEGED. A 2026 review relayed by Marathon Handbook questions 90 g/h as a ceiling but warns against assuming more is better: EMERGING.
  • Practical schedule. One gel has about 22–25 g. Field sources start about 30–45 min in and take a gel every 30–45 min, which gives about 30–50 g/h (RunnersConnect; RUN247). CORROBORATED.
  • Progression. Practitioners agree on the sequence: start at what you tolerate (about 30 g/h), raise the dose and frequency step by step, use the products you will race with, and cut back at the first sign of GI distress. Gut-train for at least 2–3 weeks before the event (RunnersConnect; Mother Runners; competitivecyclist; RUN247). CORROBORATED; consensus. The size and pace of each step are not standardised. Hearing 1 found +15 g/week, 4–6 weeks and 8–12 weeks: ALLEGED / inconsistent.
  • Fasted or low-carb long runs. Occasional fasted long runs are tolerated in practice. Making them routine or repeating them is linked to lower training quality, LEA, hormonal and immune effects (Laura Norris RD; RunnersConnect). CORROBORATED; majority view.
  • Real food. Real food has lower carbohydrate density and needs practice. Field sources favour liquids, gels or chews in the early hours (Hearing 1; RunnersConnect). CREDIBLE.
  • Automated tools. Calculator sites take finish time and body weight and give 50–90 g/h. These pages are unsourced, duplicated and have no disclaimers (several spam mirrors). ALLEGED. Field material repeatedly refers runners with GI history, or anyone wanting a personalised plan, to a registered dietitian (search summaries; Hearing 1 scope consensus). CORROBORATED. The field base did not find how commercial apps (Runna, Precision Fuel planner) word their fuelling outputs or disclaimers: UNKNOWN.

What this base cannot see

  • Controlled data on EAH incidence in training runs as opposed to races.
  • Validated sodium-per-hour rules.
  • Heat-pace tables tested in recreational runners.
  • Outcome data for automated fuelling advice.
  • How independent many of these blogs and vendor pages really are.

  • link (blog)
  • link (vendor coaches)

hearing-2-questions.md

Expert hearing 2 — questions from sub-council round 1

coach (Coaching practice 5K → 100K road)

  1. Default 3-run week for 5K/10K self-coached runners: what do coaches prescribe (one easy, one quality, one long? two quality?), and what share of time is at threshold or faster?
  2. Long runs for slow marathoners (5.5–6.5 h finish): do coaches cap the long run by time (e.g. 2.5–3 h) or by distance (e.g. 30–32 km)? What is standard?
  3. Road ultra (50K/100K, non-trail) coaching practice: longest run, long-run share of the week, peak weekly volume, cadence of peak long runs, back-to-backs; for masters runners too.

med (Sports medicine & injury prevention)

  1. Is there evidence linking single-run DURATION (not distance) to injury (e.g. runs > 2.5–3 h or > 3–4 h), in marathon or ultra runners?
  2. Is a ~5-week progression from 1:2 run-walk to 30 min continuous running defensible for true novices (Couch-to-5K-type programmes; injury and drop-out data)?
  3. Masters (≥50, ≥65) endurance runners: specific injury/cardiac considerations relevant to long runs and ultra training (recovery, bone health, deload frequency).

nutr (Nutrition & fuelling)

  1. Heat and exercise-associated hyponatremia risk during 4–5 h training runs (not races): practical guidance on fluid, sodium and carbohydrate for self-coached runners.
  2. Fuelling during training long runs of 2.5–5 h: progression and what an automated tool can safely recommend.

sys (Knowledge modelling & adaptive systems)

  1. Computational cost and design patterns for running session-level validation inside a feasibility "dry run" (simulate the plan generator to answer "how many weeks are needed?"): constraint propagation vs simulation; how products/academic systems do it.
  2. Deterministic re-planning: how to guarantee stable outputs (no oscillation) when inputs are noisy self-reports.
hearing-2-sys-acad.md

Hearing 2 — Knowledge modelling & adaptive systems (sys) — academic & official base

Access limits: 8 WebSearch queries (shared budget). I opened no primary sources, so no claim is VERIFIED. Grades rest on search abstracts. (prior knowledge) marks material I did not re-check this session. What this base cannot see: how commercial products (Runna, TrainAsONE, Garmin) actually run feasibility checks or re-planning, measured latencies, and user-facing churn data. No peer-reviewed source describes any of these.

Q1 — Session-level validation in a feasibility "dry run": cost and patterns

The two families in the literature:

  • Constraint propagation + search (constraint programming, CP). Propagation removes values that cannot appear in any solution. It is cheap but usually incomplete: it can miss infeasibility, so search is still needed to prove consistency. Enforcing full consistency on cumulative (resource-sum) constraints is NP-hard. Structural restrictions often make propagation tractable. Sources: Baptiste & Le Pape, IJCAI 1995; IJCAI 2013, abs 13/082 / UNSW pdf; Baptiste, Le Pape & Nuijten, Constraint-Based Scheduling (Springer 2001, link). Consensus in CS; CORROBORATED. Relevance to the method: weekly volume or long-run caps are "cumulative"-type sums.
  • Forward simulation (rollout). Training-planning research checks plans by running them through a load-response model. Examples:
  • Banister/fitness-fatigue as a linear dynamic system, with an observer and state feedback (arXiv 2609.26506)
  • optimal control on Banister by forward-backward sweep (NTU thesis, tdr.lib.ntu.edu.tw/123456789/97794)
  • optimal control by HJB with an explicit-Euler solver (Princeton thesis)
  • particle swarm with ε-constraints on monotony and ramp (Kumyaito 2018, doi:10.1186/s13104-017-3120-9, from hearing 1)

A 2025 paper finds that the unmodified Banister and Busso models do not reproduce tapering or periodisation (arXiv 2505.20859). Emerging; CREDIBLE.

  • AI planning (PDDL-type). Škeřík, Chrpa, Faber & Vallati (IEEE SMC 2018) and Škeřík's thesis generate plans from current level, available time and target. They report planning time "considerably shorter" than a coach's, with no runtime figures in the abstracts (Huddersfield; thesis). CREDIBLE (one group).
  • Computation cost figures: none of these sources reports runtimes for a receding-horizon or "minimum weeks" search. UNKNOWN.

Analytical note (reasoning, not evidence):

  • A deterministic generator replayed week by week costs roughly weeks × sessions × rules evaluated. For N ≤ ~200 weeks and ~10 sessions per week that is trivial (milliseconds to seconds).
  • Finding the minimum feasible N by bisection is valid only if feasibility is monotone in N: if N weeks are feasible, N+1 must be too. Phase templates or taper rules can break this. When monotonicity is not guaranteed, a linear scan over N is the safe fallback.
  • A propagation pre-pass can reject a candidate N early, but it can only reject. Because it is incomplete (see above), it cannot certify a plan as feasible.

Q2 — Deterministic re-planning with noisy self-reports

  • Plan "nervousness". The production-planning literature names the churn caused by rolling-horizon replanning. In simulation, Blackburn, Kropp & Millen (Management Science 1986, 32(4):413–429, RePEc) found a frozen horizon or a change-cost penalty dominant under most conditions. Later work adds move suppression (better robust-stability margins on a small problem: Braun & Schwartz 2012, scitepress). It also measures stability as the deviation between the old and new schedule (AIChE 2012; Demirel thesis, UNCC). Every lever trades stability against responsiveness. Majority view; CORROBORATED. None of this work is from sport.
  • Hysteresis and deadband. In control engineering, a deadband sized just above the measurement noise stops the system reacting to noise. A fixed deadband can itself cause limit cycles. Low-pass filtering adds lag. Sources: US4914564; Lee, Sung & Edgar, AIChE 2008. CREDIBLE (the sources are patents plus one conference paper).
  • Noise in the inputs (sizes the band):
  • Session-RPE is valid and reliable across 36 studies (Haddad 2017, PMC5673663). Repeat-bout r = 0.88; one study found ICC 0.95 with CV ≈ 17% (SAJSM). CORROBORATED.
  • Wellness questionnaires are noisier: item CV 11–30% and ICC 0.00–0.78 in youth soccer (Northumbria). Reliability is "little researched" and whether they detect change beyond typical error is unknown (UTS). The Hooper index showed TE/SWC 3.1 in n = 9 (PMC6390199). A ratio above 1 means single readings cannot separate a real change from noise. Majority view (noisy); CORROBORATED.
  • Smoothing. EWMA (decay constants 7 and 28 days) versus rolling averages: EWMA was more sensitive to high ACWR in n = 59 footballers (Murray 2017, BJSM, doi:10.1136/bjsports-2016-097152; Williams 2017, doi:10.1136/bjsports-2016-096589). ACWR as an injury predictor is itself contested (prior knowledge: Impellizzeri 2020). Contested.
  • Determinism in rule engines (prior knowledge, hearing 1). DMN hit policies, fixed rule priority and a pure function f(state, inputs) → plan give identical outputs for identical inputs. They do not stop oscillation when inputs fluctuate, which is what the stabilisers above address. Consensus; CREDIBLE.

Overall: the stabilisation patterns are well established in manufacturing and control engineering and have not been tested in running. Runtime data for feasibility search are UNKNOWN. Self-report noise is large enough that single readings fall within noise.

hearing-2-sys-field.md

Hearing 2 — Knowledge modelling & adaptive systems (sys) — FIELD base

Process limits: I ran 8 WebSearch queries, the full budget. WebFetch failed with DNS errors (ENOTFOUND for trainasone.com and support.runna.com), so no primary source was opened and nothing here is VERIFIED. Vendor statements that only one vendor makes are ALLEGED.

Q1. Feasibility "dry run": how many weeks are needed?

What products expose

  • Generators publish fixed plan-length ranges per distance, not a computed minimum. Garmin Coach: 5K 6–20 wk, 10K 10–21 wk, half 12–26 wk (Garmin blog, several regional copies of one origin, so ALLEGED as an independent claim). One user reported a 12-week minimum and ≥4 days/week, with a plan that could not be finished before the race (antyweb.pl): ALLEGED. Endorphins' generator "shows the earliest date that gives you enough time to train" and only offers plan lengths that fit: ALLEGED (one vendor).
  • Practitioner minimums are look-up tables keyed by distance × starting fitness. trainingplan.dev gives an experienced runner aiming only to finish 5K 4 wk, 10K 6, half 8, marathon 12, flagged "possible, not recommended". A complete beginner needs 8–10 wk for a 5K. Typical lengths are 8 / 10 / 12–14 / 16–20 wk (trailandkale). Grade: CORROBORATED as common practice; empirical basis UNKNOWN.
  • No product documents simulating its generator to derive the minimum length. The field signal is a precomputed table or range checked before generation: UNKNOWN beyond that.

Engineering patterns (outside running)

  • Constraint-based solvers are best at finding a feasible solution under many constraints and weaker at finding good ones. Optimisation schedulers aim for feasibility first, then improve (Simio whitepaper, vendor; Timefold docs on constraint satisfaction). Grade: CREDIBLE.
  • In simulation-based scheduling, profiling identified the feasibility check as a major runtime bottleneck. The fix was incremental evaluation: carry running totals forward from the parent node and compute only the new item's demand, not the whole set again (arXiv 2608.19487): ALLEGED (single source, speed-ups not seen). One dated study found constraint programming faster than branch-and-bound or simulated annealing on another problem class (Chalmers 2002): ALLEGED for this domain.
  • No benchmark comparing constraint propagation with full simulation for a weekly plan of about 100 weeks × 7 days was found: UNKNOWN. A forum case shows the "limit" in one plan editor was a data artefact (an empty date range), not a rule (runningahead): an anecdote.

Q2. Deterministic re-planning without oscillation on noisy self-reports

Product behaviour

ProductStability mechanismGrade
Runna Pace InsightsStates: on pace / ahead / review slower / "Variable Pace Detected… can't make a recommendation" when results are mixed. "Not enough data" right after a pace update or a new plan, until the next interval, tempo or time-trial session. Manual changes are advised in 20–30 s race-time steps. The user accepts changes; they are not automatic. A single fast session is not enough.CORROBORATED (Runna support plus the5krunner). Session counts and thresholds UNKNOWN.
TrainAsONERecalculates the whole future plan after edits, logged activities and missed runs (overnight). Changing a setting and reverting it yields a different plan. Some non-run activities wrongly trigger rebuilds, which the vendor admits. Mitigations: "Recent Plans" revert, plan lock up to 7 days, and a rolling lock still "in development".ALLEGED (vendor only), but these are self-reported admissions against interest.
Garmin / FirstbeatVO2max estimate uses only steady segments (HR > ~70% max, clean GPS). Users describe a slowly changing value. The race predictor sits downstream and inherits its errors.ALLEGED / anecdotal (blogs, forums); no primary documentation.

Engineering patterns

  • Deadband: a band of input in which no action occurs, used to stop "hunting" (repeated on/off switching). Hysteresis: the output changes only once the input passes a threshold, and the previous state affects the next one. The band must be wide enough to absorb noise but narrow enough to respond, and over-filtering adds harmful lag (Wikipedia Deadband; Microchip; brainmodular; Arduino forum). Grade: CORROBORATED as a general control practice; transfer to training plans is an inference.
  • Freeze windows / pinning: Timefold splits the horizon into immutable history | published window | draft. Normal replanning does not touch the published window. Exceptions use "non-disruptive replanning", where pinned entities are held fixed and the solver plans around them. Changes are applied as discrete ProblemChange events, never as live mutation. Load only the history that can still affect constraints (Timefold docs; OptaPlanner javadoc; Timefold blog). Grade: CORROBORATED (same vendor lineage, several documents). The pattern converges with TrainAsONE's 7-day lock.
  • An adaptive deadband whose width tracks the input's noise exists (EP 2663903 patent): ALLEGED.

Strength of evidence: majority view for the general patterns (deadband, hysteresis, pinning, minimum-data gates). No published thresholds for running products: UNKNOWN.

What this base cannot see

Internal thresholds, oscillation telemetry, how often users override, and any product's dry-run algorithm.

Sources

garmin.com/en-GB/blog/?p=7535; antyweb.pl/biegalem-z-trenerem-garmina-jakie-sa-efekty; endorphinsrunning.com/training-plan-generator; trainingplan.dev/tools/training-plan-length-calculator; trailandkale.com; runningahead.com forum; simio.com/whitepapers/simulation-versus-optimization-based-scheduling; docs.timefold.ai (planning-ai-concepts; responding-to-change/continuous-planning); timefold.ai/blog/continuous-planning-optimization-with-pinning; docs.optaplanner.org 8.4 PlanningEntity; arxiv.org/pdf/2608.19487; research.chalmers.se/publication/178219; support.runna.com/en/articles/10854865 and 14656203, 6205998; the5krunner.com/2026/09/28/is-runna-ai; trainasone.com/faqs/2026/reverted-settings-plan-is-different, /faqs/2022/revert-plan, /features/ai-training-plans; en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deadband; brainmodular.com hysteresis filter; data.epo.org EP2663903A1; marathonhandbook.com/how-accurate-is-garmin-vo2-max; forums.garmin.com race-predictor threads.

hearing-3-med-acad.md

Hearing 3 — Sports medicine (acad)

Access: 8 searches. The one fetch (Goom PDF) failed with a DNS error, so nothing here is VERIFIED. [S] = search snippet or abstract; [M] = the witness's prior knowledge, which is ALLEGED. This base cannot see: race-medical practice, unpublished registries, or ultra cardiac registries (none found).

Q1 Clinical check vs self-attestation (≥65; ≥35 previously sedentary)

Incidence:

  • RACER 2000–09 (Kim, NEJM 2012, PMID 23989386; 10.9 M runners) [S], CORROBORATED:
  • 0.54 arrests/100k overall;
  • marathon 1.01 vs half 0.27;
  • men 0.90 vs women 0.16; mean age 42.
  • RACER 2010–23 (JAMA 2025, doi:10.1001/jama.2025.3026; 29.3 M finishers) [S], CORROBORATED:
  • arrests unchanged (0.54 vs 0.60/100k);
  • deaths 0.20 vs 0.39/100k; case fatality 34% vs 71%;
  • marathon 1.04 vs half 0.47; men 1.12 vs women 0.19;
  • CAD is the leading known cause.
  • Age: no RACER age table was found. Proxies:
  • Roberts (BJSM 47:168), two marathons: 2.6/100k; 11 of 13 men were >39 [S]. CREDIBLE.
  • Harris 2017 triathlon: 1.74/100k overall; men ≥60: 18.6/100k; distance barely mattered [S]. CORROBORATED. Mostly swim events.
  • Ultra or >3 h runs: UNKNOWN.

Screening frameworks (contested which performs better):

  • ACSM 2015 (Riebe, MSSE) [M], ALLEGED. Age is not a trigger. Asymptomatic sedentary people start light to moderate and progress gradually. PAR-Q+ is similar.
  • ESC 2020 (Pelliccia) [M plus S], CREDIBLE. Risk is assessed individually before high-intensity exercise in sedentary or masters people; an exercise ECG is considered when risk is high.
  • AHA (per BCMJ) [S], ALLEGED. ECG for all masters athletes; an exercise test for men >40 or women >50 with a risk factor.
  • n=25 men aged 35–50 [S], ALLEGED. ACSM cleared all 25; ESC flagged 2; full work-up found a finding in 17.
  • Review [S]: there are no validated risk tools for masters athletes.
  • No study compares a clinical check with self-attestation before a first marathon or ultra. Evidence: unknown. Any rule here is policy.

Q2 Postnatal and pregnancy

Postnatal: Goom/Donnelly/Brockwell 2019 is Level 4 expert consensus; CSP and Moving Medicine cite it [S].

  • Timeline: low impact for weeks 0–12; running from 3–6 months at the earliest, after passing the tests. [S] CORROBORATED.
  • Tests [M], ALLEGED until the PDF is opened:
  • walk 30 min; single-leg balance 10 s; single-leg squat 10 per side; jog on the spot 1 min; bounds 10; hops 10 per side; running man 10 per side;
  • 20 reps per side each of calf raise, single-leg bridge and hip abduction.
  • Refer to a pelvic-health physio for [M]: incontinence, vaginal heaviness, pelvic or low-back pain, bleeding after lochia, diastasis concerns.
  • Starting dose and progression from 12 weeks: no evidence-based dose; unknown. A graded run-walk return is implied [M].
  • 2024 Delphi (Christopher et al.) [S, via a blog], ALLEGED: about 3 weeks of minimum rest, then an individualised graded return.

Pregnancy:

  • About 150 min/week moderate exercise (RCOG [S]; Canadian 2019, Mottola [M]). CORROBORATED.
  • ACOG 2020 (CO 804): running may continue if routine before pregnancy, with provider input. [S] CORROBORATED.
  • IOC (Bø 2016–18, BJSM 51:1724): evidence is very low to moderate. Elite athletes beyond the guidelines need individualised monitoring. [S] CORROBORATED.
  • Trimester-specific running rules: none found; unknown.
  • Stop signs (consensus [S]):
  • vaginal bleeding;
  • regular painful contractions;
  • fluid leakage;
  • chest pain;
  • dizziness;
  • breathlessness before exertion;
  • headache;
  • calf pain or swelling;
  • weakness affecting balance;
  • abdominal pain.

Q3 Recurring pain and referral

  • Yamato 2015 consensus RRI [S], CORROBORATED: pain that restricts running for ≥7 days or 3 consecutive scheduled sessions, or that needs a health professional.
  • Modified recreational version [S], ALLEGED: restriction in >66% of sessions over 2 weeks or >50% over 4 weeks. This is the only published rule that counts non-consecutive episodes.
  • BSI Delphi 2025 (PMID 39638438) and scoping review [S], CREDIBLE:
  • red flags: pain at rest or at night, limping, focal bony pain, high-risk sites;
  • return criteria: no bony tenderness, pain-free walking, imaging healing for high-risk sites.
  • No academic referral threshold based on episode count (e.g. 3 in 6 weeks). Unknown; any such rule is practice-derived.

Sources: PubMed 23989386 · RACER 2012 PDF · ACC 2025 · BJSM 47:168 · Healio triathlon · BCMJ · Frontiers 2025 · Goom PDF · CSP · Moving Medicine · Tommy's · IOC BJSM · Endocrinologist · RRI scoping · BSI Delphi

hearing-3-med-field.md

Hearing 3 — Sports medicine — FIELD base

Method. I ran 8 searches, the shared budget. I opened no pages, so nothing below is VERIFIED.

  • [search] means the claim comes from result snippets.
  • [prior] means I know the practitioner source but did not open it this session.

Blind spots of this base: it cannot see effect sizes, screening yield or validated thresholds. Practitioners hand on heuristics. Where the field relies on an academic number, I say so and refer it to acad.

1. Clinical check vs self-attestation (≥65; ≥35 previously sedentary; first >3 h run or marathon/ultra)

Race cardiac-arrest incidence (academic numbers circulating in the field):

StudyPopulationOverall rate (per 100,000 starters)By distance or sexGrade
Kim et al., NEJM 201210.9 M US runners, 2000–100.54 (death 0.39)Marathon 1.01, half 0.27; men 0.90, women 0.16. Male marathon rate 0.71 in 2000–04 rose to 2.03 in 2005–10. Mean age of cases 42.CORROBORATED [search: PubMed 22236223, BMJ Heart blog, SCA-Aware copy]
JAMA 2025 follow-upUS races, 2010–230.60 (unchanged)Death fell to 0.20 (case fatality 71% → 34%); men 1.12 vs women 0.19; marathon 1.04 vs half 0.47CREDIBLE [search: HealthDay/ACC report only]

Units not confirmed (the 5.5 vs 0.9 may be per 100,000 or a relative figure); acad should verify.
  • Ultra-specific or ≥65-specific race rates: not found. UNKNOWN in this base.
  • Older adults in general: in people ≥65, about 1.9% of cardiac arrests happen during or after exercise (about 2–3 per 100,000 per year). The risk is concentrated in people not used to exercise. CORROBORATED [search: Cedars-Sinai, TCTMD, SCA-Aware]. This matters for the "previously sedentary" group.

What clinical practice does:

  • There is no accepted standard for pre-participation screening of older or masters athletes. Many systems require none (Canada given as an example). Strength: consensus that no standard exists. CORROBORATED [search: PMC reviews on masters screening; Mitacs BC project].
  • Sports-cardiology practice, by risk level:
  • Resting 12-lead ECG is suggested for masters athletes over 40, but it is acknowledged to find little in people without symptoms.
  • An exercise ECG or maximal stress test is reserved for people with at least one risk factor (smoking, diabetes, family history of premature coronary disease, an abnormal ECG) or a high Framingham score.
  • Abnormal findings lead to sports-cardiologist work-up.
  • Strength: majority practice in reviews, small studies. CREDIBLE [search: PMC6089274, PMC4482301, PMC1725270 (n=88 men >60)].
  • Coaching and club practice: a PAR-Q+-style questionnaire, with medical clearance if anything is flagged. CREDIBLE [prior, hearing 1].
  • Has the field set a threshold for a first race or a run >3 h? No duration- or age-specific threshold for "clinical check vs self-attestation" was found. The field position splits:
  • Japanese authors recommend check-ups for men ≥60 (ALLEGED).
  • US authors stress how low the absolute risk is. Kim et al. reported that most arrests in older runners were coronary [prior].
  • Strength: contested / unknown.

2. Women's health

Postnatal (Goom, Donnelly & Brockwell 2019):

  • Gate: no running before about 12 weeks, then pass a readiness battery before the first run. The battery includes:
  • walk 30 min;
  • single-leg balance and squat;
  • jog on the spot for 1 min;
  • hops, and others.
  • Every test must be done without pain, heaviness, dragging or leakage.
  • Majority practice, adopted by NHS boards, Athletics NI and the CSP. CORROBORATED [search: Athletics NI PDF, CSP news, ABUHB NHS Wales, PTHB, NMH Dublin].
  • The authors call it "an aid to clinical reasoning, not a protocol." CORROBORATED [search].
  • Starting dose and progression: no fixed numbers were found in the snippets.
  • Field practice is a walk-run interval start (brisk walk alternating with light jogging) or a "Couch to 5K"-style plan.
  • Build volume before intensity, with recovery days between runs.
  • A shorter starting distance applies when risk factors are present (e.g. obesity).
  • If leaking or heaviness appears, step back a phase.
  • CORROBORATED [search: PTHB, NMH, Mid Yorks].
  • The exact week-1 intervals are UNKNOWN here. The guideline's progression chart (reproduced in the ABUHB PDF) should be opened.
  • Field evidence gap: a Deering et al. international Delphi exists because there is "little guidance" on how to progress a programme. CREDIBLE [search: Cardiff Met record].

Pregnancy:

  • Eligibility: running is generally considered acceptable for women who ran before pregnancy, have an uncomplicated pregnancy and have their clinician's agreement. Majority view. CREDIBLE [search: secondary blogs citing ACOG].
  • Intensity: practice uses the talk test or RPE rather than a heart-rate cap. One teaching deck cites <90% of HRmax (ALLEGED). CREDIBLE.
  • Trimester volume: no numeric field guidance was found.
  • Practice expects volume and intensity to fall, especially in the 3rd trimester, because of balance, joint laxity and pelvic-floor load.
  • Practice adjusts to symptoms, not to a calendar week. Some clinicians name stop weeks (e.g. 16 wk), but these are opinions.
  • Contested. CREDIBLE.
  • Stop signs (ACOG list, repeated across sources): vaginal bleeding or fluid leak, dizziness or feeling faint, breathlessness before exertion, chest pain, headache, muscle weakness, calf pain or swelling, regular painful contractions, reduced fetal movement. CORROBORATED [search: several secondary sources; ACOG not opened].
  • Pelvic-floor stop signs (physio practice): heaviness or dragging, leaking, urgency, or pain that does not settle after a run. These mean modify the run, and see a pelvic-health physio if symptoms persist despite changes. Leakage before pregnancy means lower-impact options are advised. CORROBORATED [search: physio Q&A, Running Mums Australia].

3. Recurring pain: when to refer

  • No field source gives a count threshold such as "3 episodes in 6 weeks". UNKNOWN.
  • Field triggers for physio referral:
  • the same site keeps recurring;
  • pain settles during the run but reliably flares afterwards;
  • pain lingers after activity.
  • Recurrence (e.g. shin pain) calls for a "structured approach" rather than load reduction alone.
  • Majority practice. CORROBORATED [search: Pogo Physio, PT Health, Physio Network].
  • Bone-stress pattern (stop running and refer):
  • Refer when ≥3 of these 4 are present: pain worsening during the run, pain lingering after, localised bony tenderness (<5 cm), pain on hopping.
  • Groin or hip pain that may be a femoral-neck stress injury means same-day or urgent referral.
  • CORROBORATED [search: Backcountry Physio, Physio Network; prior hearing 1].
  • A previous bone stress injury strongly predicts another. Recurrent bone stress injuries lead practice to screen for RED-S / low energy availability (menstrual history, fuelling). Majority practice. CORROBORATED [search: Backcountry Physio, Actively Fueled podcast].
