NO. The architecture holds; the faults are seams fixable by rule edits.

p1-roomB.md

P1 room B — adaptation, durations, safety (v3)

Session (condensed)

Contrarian: P-01: the 10-week first 5K fails safely (FEA-042), but the race simply disappears. Only 27% complete C25K [hearing-2 med-acad, CREDIBLE]. First Principles: An 11-week minimum just moves the zero slack. Runners with no date already get 12 weeks. Fix how the plan ends. Executor: P-03: Fatou's 182 min is an S_slow artifact. She reaches 21.1 km in about 164 min. INV-28 checks planned duration, not expected duration. Context Realist: For masters, duration evidence is UNKNOWN and age is not an established injury multiplier [hearing-2 med-crit, CORROBORATED]. The AF link is CORROBORATED, its size contested. ESC 2020 covers intensity only. First Principles: No new load gate; at most reuse the attestation. R4 4–1; FP dissents. Contrarian: P-02: a 50K's runs over 180 min are its demand, so a count cap would block its floor. Spacing is grounded instead: Galloway every other week [factdesk4 §4, CORROBORATED]; Koop 2-week peaks [wg2 §4, CREDIBLE].

Case walk-throughs

  • A. P1, parkrun fixed at week 10.
  • Week 3, runny nose: sessions cut to 0.75× still count as clean (SAF-082).
  • Busy week 5, unlogged week 7: on S8 at race week → PHASES_DO_NOT_FIT, no completion.
  • 12 days off mid-ladder: no rule covers it.
  • At 2 runs a week, SAF-084 refers her to a doctor for scheduling, not symptoms.
  • B. Fatou, 52, marathon finish.
  • 12-day fever in week 12: G1, long run 144 → 182 by week 17, still stretch ✓.
  • Then 2 busy weeks drop TOL to 3. INV-28 caps her at 180, and the re-plan fails over 2 phantom minutes.
  • C. Age 67, Achilles injury 8 months ago, 50K, easy 7:30, 420 min, 20 weeks.
  • Capped at 240 min (28.8 km ≥ 25 km) → stretch, LOW_SLACK.
  • About 6 runs over 180 min, guarded only by TOL 4.
  • Pain 4/10 next morning in weeks 11, 13 and 15 never counts as consecutive, so ADP-053 never refers.
  • D. Age 32, TOL 3, half marathon in 30 weeks.
  • Pregnancy in week 6 → SAF-051 maintain ✓.
  • At 12 weeks postpartum (about 24 weeks off), R0 sessions are 45 min because rtr.session_min follows prior TOL 3.
  • E. Age 17 y 4 m, marathon in 40 weeks.
  • The SAF-044 14 km cap has no per-date rule, so the result is infeasible or a silent failure.
  • Per date: feasible (14 km until week 33, then 5 steps).
  • F. Night nurse on a rotating roster.
  • Shifts can only be weekdays.
  • CTX-005 keeps displacing the long run, and ADP-023 fails the plan around week 9, not at onboarding.
  • G. Half marathon in 4 weeks, fever on day 3.
  • SHARPEN fails, the peak is credited → DEGRADED_COMPLETION ✓.

Required changes

  1. P-01, settled: 10 weeks stays allowed as stretch; no 11-week minimum.
  • 03 FEA-043 and 10 §4b, new LADDER_RUNWALK completion for 5K finish goals. Ladder unfinished in race week, rung ≥ S5, estimated time on feet ≤ spike.block_ratio × longest logged ladder session → race kept as run-walk at the rung's pattern (goal races are already warn-exempt, GEN-023); else earliest date.
  • A fixed date under 12 weeks gets "Most people repeat a week; that is normal" [factdesk4 §1, CORROBORATED].
  • C9, C6, C12.
  1. 10 §4b, SAF-082/084; 08 ADP-030/031: start-ladder adaptation.
  • A session counts toward its rung only if ≥ miss.partial_ratio of it was done.
  • Gaps: 8–14 days → repeat the last completed rung; 15–29 days → drop one rung; ≥ 30 days → SAF-080.
  • SAF-084 counts only weeks with an unclean session.
  • Delete the stale "5-week ladder… open for round 2" text.

  • TOL 4 then gates floor runs only slower than ~8:32/km.

p1-roomC.md

Plenary round 1 (round 3), room C: the runner's experience and build readiness (v3)

Session (condensed, speaker-labelled)

Outsider: I onboarded as five runners. MSG-FEA-STRETCH shows {reason_text}, but no reason code has text. Messages are spread over 10 files, and runners would see rule IDs (ADP-013) and false precision ("198 min").

First Principles: On day 0 the verdict carries no information. Every claim and every race before the app is estimated (INP-003, INP-005), and FEA-035 makes that a stretch reason, so every new user gets "stretch". Stretch changes no cap or dose (03 §5).

Executor: So 13's P2, P3 and d2-A case 4 cannot happen to a new user. There is also no expression grammar, the answer bands are undefined, and S1's "intended intensity" depends on the S7 plan, which is circular.

Status-quo: Keep the simple week view; put grades, ledger and IDs behind "why?". Grade-only labels mislead: a practice CORROBORATED only as description reads "well supported".

Expansionist: FEA-030 and FEA-031 already compute "+60 min" or "add a run day", but the runner gets no option to accept it.

Outsider (P-06): "Interim target 48:56" cannot be checked against a 3 km test.

First Principles: And in P2 the parkrun counts as noise for paces, yet it makes the verdict easier, only because the band widens from 4% to 7%.

Case walk-throughs

  1. Maya, 34, has never run; 5K finish in 10 weeks.
  • Output: start ladder, verdict stretch (LOW_SLACK, ESTIMATED_INPUTS).
  • Problems: the promised "{checkpoint}" does not exist; she gave no estimates; MSG-CTX-001 promises easier weeks that P1 does not have; a skipped week leaves only "+1 week" or "base plan".
  1. Tom, 38, ran a 10K in 52:00 six weeks before joining; wants 48:00 in 12 weeks.
  • Output: the anchor is estimated, so the band is 7% and the verdict is stretch (fast band). Two effort-only weeks, then a 3 km test.
  • Problems:
  • Nothing explains why he gets no paces.
  • If he later logs another 52:00 10K, the band shrinks to 4%. The verdict becomes "beyond current" although his fitness has not changed, and he is not told why.
  • MSG-FEA-BAND says "today's fitness" but includes the taper gain.
  1. Ana, first marathon, 6 h a week, claims only.
  • Output: stretch, not "feasible" as in 13.
  • Problems: no rule defines the speed behind "for you about 27.7 km"; "29 km or 198 min" is false precision; a missed run triggers a change list of rule IDs.
  1. Masters runner, 58, calf injury last year.
  • Problems: CTX-003/004 have no messages (the developer reuses "You're new to running"); MSG-SAF-061's why names "heart-rhythm problems" (breaks 00 §6.3, TST-015); ADP-052 (pain 4/10) has no message.
  1. Ben, marathon on 300 min a week.
  • Output: infeasible (+60 min); a half marathon is offered.
  • Problems: no "find more time" option; the option texts do not exist.

Required changes

  1. 03 §5/§8:
  • Give every reason code plain_text, a defined checkpoint and one of three headlines: ambitious, tight or uncertain.
  • Show at most 2 reasons.

  1. 03 FEA-035:
  • ESTIMATED_INPUTS becomes a confidence label, not a stretch reason.
  • The safety already sits in GEN-011, the slow profile and B_slow.

  1. 03 FEA-038 (P-06):
  • New MSG-FEA-INTERIM: "Your goal {target} is faster than your current fitness suggests (about {edge}). We'll test in week {n}. About {test_time} or faster on the {test_dist} test means you're closing the gap."
  • Round the edge up.
  • Add 3 checkpoint-outcome messages: improved, within the deadband, slower.

C12, C9.

  1. 03 §3, FEA-052:
  • If a new anchor is within pace.deadband_pct of the active one, keep the narrower B_fast.
  • Any change of verdict must say whether fitness or the precision of the estimate changed.
  • Basis: retest CV of 2.4–3.4% [factdesk4 §6, CORROBORATED].

  1. 01 §3: S1 treats intended intensity as vigorous, except for start-ladder, return-to-run and base entries. C5, C7.

  1. 00 §5, 15: Derive the grade label from both basis and grade:
  • convention → "common coaching practice"
  • design default → "our cautious choice, not tested"
  • VERIFIED/CORROBORATED evidence → "well supported by research"
  • CREDIBLE → "some research support"

Fact desk

  1. How accurate are recalled race times compared with official results [factdesk2 §6, UNKNOWN]?

P2 room B — implementation readiness (v4)

Members: EXP, OUT, EXE, SQ, SA. Checker: tools/check_rules_p2b.py.

Session (condensed, speaker-labelled)

EXE: rules.json: 315 records, 117 formal rules (all T0–T2), no duplicate IDs or keys. But all 285 actions are "detail": "see then_text", with 37 ad hoc ops outside 12 §3.3. The engine can decide whether a rule fires, not what it does.

SA: "Formal" is partly nominal. 13 T0–T2 conditions are only event.type == …, 3 are true. Expressions use 13 undeclared roots, 62 leaf paths absent from 12 §2 (state.gate, goal.distance_km) and 16 functions outside the grammar (preset_cap, rule('SCR-004')…). All 41 p() ids resolve.

EXE: Conditions live in a dict inside build_rules.py, not in module texts; TST-090's "regenerated from the module texts" is false where it matters.

Instead: (a) an oracle for the 03 §2/05 numeric core, as a table by a different author; (b) differential tests of the evaluator against reference CEL plus a Kleene truth table; (c) INV-13/15 moved S→B using the raw preset table.

p2-roomC.md

Plenary 2 (round 4), room C: red team (v4)

Members: Contrarian, First Principles (FP), Outsider, Status-quo (SQ), Systems Architect (SA).

Session (condensed)

SA: I ran Q-03 through the Kleene semantics in rules.json. INP-001 says every field accepts "I don't know", but SCR-002 stores PAR-Q+ answers as booleans. If a symptom item is unknown, answers_yes leaves it out, so SCR-004 is false. SCR-005 (exists a == true → unknown → on_unknown fire) gives proceed_restricted. Someone with chest pain who taps "not sure" gets easy runs instead of a pause. Contrarian: The opposite problem appears mid-plan. A runner logs pain 1/10 and dismisses the symptom form. SAF-001…005 then fire on unknown and show "Call emergency services now" for a skipped form. Users learn to stop logging pain. Outsider: They need no trick to avoid logging it. "Done as planned" (INP-022) asks only for RPE, and pain sits under "Anything else?". Most of my weeks would never touch the pain model. FP: Lying about age, pregnancy or race times can't be prevented. The method's duty is narrower: a blank answer or an honest error must never unlock load, and the method must never escalate a blank to an emergency. SQ: C25K apps let you mark sessions done too. Rung credit with no date spacing is different, though: it lets someone finish a 9-week convention in one evening. SA: INV-01 exempts the goal race only. After ADP-080, nothing stops a chain of H1 goal races a week apart, each a 1.6× spike. FP (Q-02): The 65+ gate reads only age and outputs a training-suitability state. That fits checklist (a)–(e) [factdesk2 §2, CORROBORATED boundary / UNKNOWN device status]. The texts around it are the risk. FP keeps his dissent on the gate itself.

Attacks that FAILED (method held):

  • Fake recalled PB: estimated → no paces (INP-005, INV-15).

  • Backfill to unlock runs over 180 min: INV-28 needs TOL L4 at measured.
  • Goal-distance hop to carry long runs: L_hold formula (ADP-080, LOD-005). Switching does not reset the deload counter.
  • Never-runner with 12 h a week: start ladder ≥ 9 weeks; availability never sets volume (INP-031, SAF-085).
  • Ultra on 2 h a week: AVAILABILITY_TIME → infeasible, with options.
  • 85-year-old marathon: SAF-060 restriction, INV-28, INV-29.
  • 100 weeks: near-equal seasons (PHS-012).
  • Under 14: no plan (SAF-040).
  • 17-year-old marathon: SAF-043; per-date caps (INV-30).
  • Pregnant never-runner (0 runs): SAF-050.
  • Same-day clearance: SAF-021.
  • Absurd target time: paces come from the anchor (INV-15); FEA-038 sets interim checks.

Fact desks

factdesk1.md

Fact Desk 1 — Report (20 Oct 2026)

Method: 14 WebSearch queries. No primary paper was opened (publisher sites are blocked), so CORROBORATED is the highest grade used. One source = ALLEGED. Each item ends with a "rule use" line for the architect.

1. Bone stress injury (BSI) — gap #6

  • High-risk sites: femoral neck (superior/tension cortex), anterior tibial cortex, navicular, 5th metatarsal (also often listed: sesamoids, talus). They may need surgery or long unloading (up to ~6 months). Low-risk sites: posteromedial tibia, fibula, metatarsals 2–4. These heal with modified activity. — CORROBORATED (Warden interview: link ; Hoenig/Warden 2023 SR, n=2974 BSIs, 11/16 multi-site comparisons found site predicts return to sport: link , link)/export.html). The SR notes that the low/high split "has not been sufficiently validated".
  • Return-to-run (low-risk sites): first pain-free walking and daily activity. Then four stages: walk–jog intervals → easy running with rest days in between → running on consecutive days → full training. Symptoms are checked during the run and the next day; if symptoms appear, rest a day and go back one stage. Progression depends on symptoms, not on fixed weeks. — CORROBORATED (BJSM blog table adapted from Warden et al.: link ; Warden interview above; Warden/Edwards/Willy 2021: link).
  • Warning signs (focal bony tenderness, pain that rises with load, night or rest pain): CREDIBLE only. I did not retrieve them from Warden 2021.
  • Consensus: a 2025 BJSM modified-Delphi consensus exists (41 panellists, 3 rounds) and states that international consensus had been lacking (link). Its return-to-run content is UNKNOWN.
  • Rule use: suspected BSI → stop running and refer. A suspected high-risk site → mandatory medical clearance, and the app runs no protocol of its own. The staged return in the app applies only after a clinician confirms a low-risk BSI.

2. Pain-monitoring model (Silbernagel/Thomeé) — gap #8

  • Thresholds: CONTRADICTED / UNRESOLVED. One secondary source gives pain during activity ≤5/10. Pain must settle soon after stopping and be gone by the next morning (link). Another gives "pain not allowed to reach 5" (i.e. ≤4/10) (link). No source found supports ≤2/10. The next-morning rule appears in both: CORROBORATED. Another commonly reported rule is that pain or stiffness should not increase week to week, but here it is ALLEGED.
  • Use while running: Silbernagel 2007 RCT (n=38): continuing running and jumping under the model did no harm compared with 6 weeks of active rest. — CORROBORATED (PEDro record: link ; thesis: link ; Physiopedia toolkit: link). The evidence covers Achilles tendinopathy only. That it carries over to other complaints is CREDIBLE extrapolation.
  • Rule use: set the conservative default at ≤3/10 during running, settled within 24 h, no worse the next morning. Do not use 5/10 until the primary paper is opened. Never apply the model to suspected BSI.

3. Intensity anchoring accuracy — gap #17

  • Critical speed (CS) estimates 10 km race speed well in recreational runners (n=34, CV from 400 m + 2000 m trials) — CORROBORATED (link ; link). CS sits faster than lactate threshold and the two should not be used interchangeably — CORROBORATED (n=31: link ; n=13 Bland–Altman, no agreement: link).
  • %HRmax zones: HR at VT ≈83% of lab HRmax but ≈77% of race/training HRmax. During a marathon the HR–%VO2max ratio drifted from 1.01 to 1.19 (link ; link). That HR zones are unreliable for individuals is CORROBORATED. I found no quantified misclassification rate (UNKNOWN).
  • Talk test / RPE: a 2022 Sports Medicine review says the talk test and RPE are valid for VT1 (equivocal speech, RPE ~10–11 on the 6–20 scale) and VT2 (speech impossible, RPE 13–15) — CORROBORATED (link ; Persinger/Foster 2004 via link). Runner caveat: training at the equivocal stage gave HR and RPE that were too high, so last-positive is the safe easy-run anchor (JSCR 2011, per review).
  • VDOT tables vs lab thresholds: UNKNOWN. I found no validation study.
  • Rule use: anchor paces on recent race results or CS. Treat derived "threshold" pace as a slight overestimate. Use the talk test (can still speak in sentences) as a ceiling for easy runs. HR is secondary.

4. Daniels' return-after-break rules — gap #18

  • "Less than 5 days off → resume 100% of prior training": ALLEGED (single secondary source: link). The book's chapter on breaks exists (Goodreads notes: link). The 6–28 day and >28 day volume and VDOT reductions are UNKNOWN. The book must be opened before the figures are used.
  • Rule use: write the return rules as the method's own conservative defaults, labelled "expert-practice, unverified".

5. Adaptive/app plans vs static plans — gap #20

  • No RCT or cohort comparing adaptive and static plans was found (UNKNOWN). NCT06538363 and an n≈425 app RCT were not found.
  • Runna–Loughborough (Blagrove) is a commissioned review of plan logic announced in 2026, with no published outcomes. Runna's "43% fewer injuries" claim is self-reported (link ; link) — CORROBORATED that no evidence exists.
  • TU/e self-regulation app trial in marathon runners: no significant effect on running-related injuries or fatigue, and adherence was low — ALLEGED (link). Runfitcheck RCT: changed prevention behaviour, but no injury effect was shown (link). GRONORUN: a graded plan was not protective (link).
  • Rule use: do not claim that adaptation prevents injuries. Present adaptivity as a design choice.

6. Realistic improvement rates / goal stretch

  • UNKNOWN for annual improvement by level. No longitudinal recreational dataset was found. Context only: top-10 Berlin recreational finishers improved across eras while age groups overall slowed (link). In 40 sub-3 h veterans, speed fell about 0.67%/yr (link). Age decline for individuals differs from the curves implied by world bests, which is ALLEGED (link).
  • Rule use: goal-stretch caps must be labelled expert-default parameters, not evidence.

7. Minimum effective training

  • FIRST (Furman), 3 key runs/week plus cross-training. Reported gains: VO2max +4.8%, LT pace +4.4%; participants improved their marathon times. Secondary sources only, from the same originators — CREDIBLE (link ; link ; link).
  • Dutch cohort (556 half-marathon, 441 marathon runners): >32 km/week and a long run >21 km were associated with faster half-marathon times (link ; link) — CORROBORATED (one study, two records; associational).
  • I found no federation minimum for days or hours per week (UNKNOWN).

Priority opens for a web-fetch agent: Silbernagel 2007 AJSM (thresholds); Warden 2014 JOSPT / 2021 (BSI stages); the 2025 BJSM BSI Delphi; Daniels' chapter on breaks.

factdesk2.md

Fact Desk 2 — 20 Oct 2026

Method: WebSearch only (16 queries). Publisher PDFs and the mirror copies of Frandsen 2025 were blocked by the proxy (403), so nothing here is VERIFIED. Grades follow the charter: two independent sources make a claim CORROBORATED, one source makes it ALLEGED. "Desk inference" means my own reasoning, not a sourced finding.

1. Frandsen et al. 2025 (BJSM 59:1203–1210, Garmin-RUNSAFE)

  • Exposure: the distance of a single session divided by the longest session in the previous 30 days. The categories are ≤10% (reference), >10–30%, >30–100% and >100%. The hazard rate ratios were 1.64, 1.52 and 2.28. A 1–10% increase gave a non-significant rate about 19% higher. CORROBORATED (SDU repository PDF record, ResearchGate, Smagpie, Pheidi).
  • Exposure was distance only, not duration or intensity. The paper's own limitations name "use of running distance as an exposure" and its binned exposure states. CORROBORATED (two search summaries of the paper and Running with Rock critique). Weekly ACWR and week-to-week ratios showed no association, or an inverse one. CORROBORATED.
  • Handling of sessions with no run in the prior 30 days, after a gap, or the first session: UNKNOWN. No accessible source describes it. Mathematically the ratio is undefined when the denominator is 0, so these sessions were probably excluded or censored. That is desk inference. The design must not assume the paper covers returns from a break.
  • Run-walk or time-based sessions: UNKNOWN. No statement was found. The cohort used Garmin GPS running activities, so walk segments would probably count toward distance (desk inference). The 10% cut-point was partly chosen because the "10% rule" already existed. It is not a discovered biological threshold. ALLEGED (Aeris Substack).

2. Regulation (FDA general wellness / EU MDR)

  • FDA: the 2019 guidance was superseded on 6 Jan 2026 by a revised General Wellness guidance, which also explicitly covers non-invasive wearables. CORROBORATED (Covington, Cooley, CRS IN12657). Cite the 2026 version, not the 2019 one. Section 3060 of the Cures Act excludes from the device definition software for a healthy lifestyle that is "unrelated to diagnosis, cure, mitigation, prevention or treatment of a disease". CORROBORATED (FDA PDF, Innolitics).
  • EU: under MDCG 2019-11 (Rev.1, 2025), qualification depends on the manufacturer's stated intended purpose. Lifestyle and well-being software is outside the MDR. Rule 11 applies only once software has a medical purpose, with Class IIa as the default for information used in diagnostic or therapeutic decisions. CORROBORATED (OpenRegulatory, Emergo, Mason Hayes & Curran).
  • Whether a PAR-Q+-style screen is a device: UNKNOWN (no regulator text on this case found). Desk view, which needs legal review: the screen is low risk if it (a) shows a fixed, published questionnaire unchanged; (b) answers any "yes" with a generic, non-individualised message such as "Before starting or continuing this training plan, talk to a doctor or qualified health professional"; (c) never names, scores, predicts or rules out a condition ("you may have…", "risk of cardiac event"); (d) frames the output as a training-suitability decision ("this plan pauses until you confirm clearance"), not a health assessment; (e) carries a "not medical advice" statement. Risky features include symptom triage, cardiac-risk scores, and injury diagnosis or rehabilitation claims. Rev.1 adds "prognosis/prediction" wording, which widens Rule 11's reach.

3. Vickers & Vertosick 2016 (BMC Sports Sci Med Rehabil)

  • The study collected an internet survey of N = 2,303 runners, split 2:1 into training and validation sets. Riegel-type predictors were well calibrated up to the half marathon but underestimated marathon times. CORROBORATED (MSKCC record, Slate, Marathon Handbook). The model also uses weekly mileage.
  • Coefficients in usable form: UNKNOWN. No search result reproduced the equation. Third-party calculators claim to implement it (runhive, rundida), which is ALLEGED. The claim of "about 40% more accurate than Riegel" has no source. Action: the fact desk should open the open-access BMC paper or its PMC copy before encoding anything.

4. Run-walk and 3 days/week: completion, adherence, injury

  • No controlled or cohort evidence found that run-walk or 3-day/week plans improve completion or adherence, or reduce injury, in novices. UNKNOWN.
  • Related evidence: in the GRONORUN RCT (n = 532, 4-mile event), a graded 13-week programme built on the 10% rule gave about 20.8% injured versus about 20.3% on the standard 8-week programme. The difference was not significant. CORROBORATED (RUG, ACU). A 4-week walk-and-hop preconditioning phase gave 15.2% versus 16.8%, also not significant. ALLEGED (single summary). Novice injury incidence is 17.8 per 1000 h versus 7.7 for recreational runners. CREDIBLE (meta-analysis, PMC4473093). In NYC first-time marathoners, running at least 4 times a week and having no prior half marathon were associated with injury. ALLEGED (Weill Cornell).

5. Talk test and RPE

  • In 16 healthy adults, the talk test's "equivocal" stage approximates the ventilatory threshold on both treadmill and cycle (Persinger/Foster 2004). A review found the talk test and RPE valid against both VT and the respiratory compensation threshold. CORROBORATED (ACE, Sports Med review, UW thesis).
  • Specific to novice runners or run-walk intervals: UNKNOWN. No studies were found. Use the talk test as a continuous-run default only.

6. Accuracy of self-report

  • Weekly self-reported distance is reliable against a GPS watch (good reliability, no systematic bias). Pace is only moderately reliable. CREDIBLE (one study, Bullock 2024 JMIR, with multiple repository records).
  • Accuracy of self-reported race times: UNKNOWN.
  • HRmax: age formulas have a typical individual error of about 11–12 bpm (HERITAGE SEE: 12.4 bpm for 220−age, 11.4 bpm for Tanaka). Bias varies by population. CREDIBLE (PMC4831892, SciELO). No runner-specific study was found. Rule implication: never hard-gate on a formula-based HRmax.

7. Long-run progression in static plans

  • Higdon Novice 1 (18 weeks): starts at a 6-mile long run and peaks at a single 20-miler. CORROBORATED (TrainingPeaks, Running with Rock). The commonly cited sequence is 6, 7, 5, 9, 10, 7, 12, 13, 10, 15, 16, 12, 18, 14, 20, 12, 8, race. It is ALLEGED because it is not confirmed by any source, and one reader report conflicts with it. If the sequence is correct, the step-ups compared with the 30-day longest run are 1.17, 1.29, 1.11, 1.20, 1.08, 1.15, 1.07, 1.13 and 1.11. That means 7 of 9 increases exceed 1.10× (desk computation).
  • Hansons beginner: the longest run is 16 miles, alternating with moderate runs of about 10–12 miles. CORROBORATED (Marathon Handbook, Running with Rock). Week-by-week figures are UNKNOWN.
  • Pfitzinger 18/55: starts at 33 mi/week with a 12-mile long run. ALLEGED (single source, Running with Rock). Week-by-week figures are UNKNOWN and need the book.

8. Outcomes of following static plans as written

  • UNKNOWN: no cohort reports completion or injury by named published plan. The nearest data: in the NYC first-time marathoner RCT, 7.1% versus 7.3% had injuries preventing completion (PMC6931177). In the NYC cohort (n = 675), 9.5% had a major injury and 49.2% a minor one. A school programme reported 96% completion and about 40% injured. All of these are ALLEGED because each rests on a single source.

Bottom line for the design: the 1.10× single-session cap is backed by one observational, distance-only study whose handling of gaps is undocumented. Treat it as a default, not a law. Encode explicit rules for return after a gap and for run-walk sessions, and record them as UNKNOWN-based.

factdesk3.md

Fact Desk 3 — plan structures, long runs, variability, ultra cohorts

Date: 2026-10-21. Method: 18 WebSearch queries; publisher pages not opened (proxy). Ceiling = CORROBORATED. One source = ALLEGED. Nothing here is VERIFIED.

1. 5K/10K plans: share of time at threshold or faster; longest run vs race distance

  • Daniels quality caps per session (T ≤ lesser of 10% weekly volume or 60 min; I ≤ lesser of 8% or 10 km; R ≤ 5%): CORROBORATED as the commonly quoted rule (forum quote of the book plus Active.com), but these are per-session caps, not measured weekly shares. link ; link ; link
  • Measured share of weekly time at threshold or faster across published 3–5 runs/week 5K/10K plans: UNKNOWN. No audit found. Implied from the caps: roughly 15–25% of volume in 2 quality days. Treat that as a design default, not a finding.
  • Longest run relative to race distance in 5K/10K plans: UNKNOWN (not found in budget). The marathon finding (item 5) does not carry over.

2. Couch-to-5K structure, completion/injury; novice improvement rates

  • NHS C25K: 9 weeks, 3 runs/week; week 1 is 5 min brisk walk then 60 s run / 90 s walk alternated for 20 min. CORROBORATED (NHS podcast transcript feed plus ASICS guide). Week 9 (3 × 30 min continuous) is CREDIBLE (widely known, not confirmed in results). link ; link
  • Completion 27.3%, injury 19% (21/110); prior injury OR 7.56 (UK modified C25K, IJERPH 2023): ALLEGED (one study; it cites another C25K injury rate of 49%). link
  • Structured plan safer than self-devised plan in UK novices: ALLEGED (one study). link
  • 8–16-week improvement in novices (RCT arms): UNKNOWN for 5K/10K. Only a 4-week sprint-interval trial in untrained young men was found (+4.5% 5K vs control): ALLEGED. link . Claims like "20–30% in 12 weeks" come from an uncited retailer: discard.

3. Test-retest variability of 5K/10K times

  • Recreational runners, 5K track time trial: CV 3.44%, ICC 0.94; 10K was more reproducible (Machado et al. 2015, n=31 men): ALLEGED (single recreational study). link
  • Trained/competitive runners: CV about 1–1.5% (Hurst & Board 2016, CV 0.95%, typical error 14.7 s; Fisher 2017, 1.5%; Stevens 2015, 1.2% on a treadmill): CORROBORATED. link ; link
  • Implication: a self-coached recreational runner's 5K retest can move about ±3% (≈±45–60 s at 25–30 min) with no fitness change. Pace-zone updates should require a change larger than this. (Inference.)

4. Daniels' long-run cap

  • Lesser of 25% of weekly volume or 150 min (2.5 h): CORROBORATED (several secondary summaries and forum quotes; Running Magazine quotes Daniels: "2.5 h ... seems just too long to me"). link ; link ; link
  • A 30% variant for runners under about 40 mi/week: ALLEGED (one summary). "2.5–3 h": CONTRADICTED as Daniels' wording; the sources conflict, and the 150-min figure is the majority reading. "22 miles" cap: discard.
  • Specific guidance for slower-than-4 h marathoners: UNKNOWN in the primary text. The time cap implicitly limits them (≈15 mi at 2.5 h for a ~50 mpw runner, forum anecdote: ALLEGED). Book not opened.

5. First-time marathoners: longest run and outcomes

  • No "Doherty 2020" marathon arm was found. The closest match is Toresdahl et al. (NYC Marathon 2017 first-timers, n=675 completers). Longer longest training run was associated with lower race-day injury; not completing an 18-mile run was associated with more minor race-day injuries; ≥4 runs/week and no half-marathon experience were associated with training-phase injury; race-day injury: 8.9% major, 48.5% minor. CORROBORATED (Cornell record + HSS press + Healio + SPB). link ; link ; link
  • Fokkema 2020 (INSPIRE, n≈441 marathoners): longest run <25 km was associated with a slower finish and weekly volume >65 km with a faster one; there was no link to injury. ALLEGED (single study; one record). link
  • Longest session predicts completion (OR 1.21; finishers vs non-finishers, 2001): ALLEGED. link
  • Meta-analysis (85 studies): longest run, volume and frequency all associated with faster finish; DNF ≈7.3%: CREDIBLE (one meta-analysis). link
  • Long-run share of week versus outcomes: UNKNOWN.

6. FIRST and Galloway marathon plans

  • FIRST: 3 runs/week (long, tempo, intervals) plus 2 cross-training sessions; long runs up to 18–20 mi, often 60–72% of weekly running mileage. CORROBORATED for structure (Marathon Handbook, Coach The Run, RunnersConnect). The exact 20-mi peak and the share are CREDIBLE: they come from a critic's summary and the book was not seen. Peak weekly volume: UNKNOWN (inference: about 30–40 mi). link ; link ; link
  • Galloway: 3 days/week (2 × 30 min weekday plus a weekend long run), long runs ≥2 min/mi slower than marathon ability with run-walk; long runs up to 26 mi (and beyond in some plans). 3 days and slow pace: CORROBORATED. Peak of 26 mi: ALLEGED (forum report; schedule not seen). Peak weekly volume: UNKNOWN. link ; link
  • Hansons 16-mi cap: CREDIBLE (well known; only one secondary source in results).

7. Injury and long-run share / single runs ≥3–4 h

  • Frandsen et al., BJSM 2025;59:1203–10 (RUNSAFE, n≈5,200, Garmin): risk rises when a session exceeds the longest run of the prior 30 days. HR about 1.64 for +10–30%, 1.52 for +30–100%, 2.28 for ≥+100%, versus ≤10%. Weekly-load measures were not significant. CORROBORATED (LIH record, BJSM blog, Physiotutors, PhysioExtra). link ; link ; link
  • Frandsen subgroups (absolute length, ≥3–4 h runs, marathon/ultra): UNKNOWN. They were not in any accessible summary.
  • Injury versus long-run share of the week, or single runs ≥3–4 h: UNKNOWN.

8. Road 50K / 100K plans

  • 50K: peak about 45–55 mi/week; longest run about 3:15 (+2 h the next day) to 26 mi; back-to-backs common. CORROBORATED as a range across TrainingPeaks, Sunrise Run Co and Marathon Handbook. These are mixed road/trail plans, not road-only. link ; link ; link
  • 100K: peak about 14–16.5 h/week; longest run 5:00–5:30 with a 2:30–2:55 run the next day; 3 build weeks then 1 recovery week. ALLEGED (one vendor family; stated as adaptable to flat courses). Long run ≈ 30–35% of peak-week time (computed). link
  • Road-only plans and the cadence of peak long runs: UNKNOWN.

9. Comrades / road-ultra cohorts

  • Comrades 2018, n=106: lower weekly distance over the 12 pre-race weeks was associated with higher injury risk (mostly during or after the race); the ACWR effect was small; 8 injuries/1000 h; injury proportion 40%. ALLEGED (one study; small, retrospective). link
  • SAFER XXXII (n=5,770 entrants): 12-month gradual-onset injury prevalence 11.6%; fewer sessions/week was a risk factor; women PR 1.6. ALLEGED (single study). link
  • Two Oceans 2019, n=119: 5 injuries/1000 h; load–injury link for runners under 30 km/week. ALLEGED. link
  • Taken together, the three ultra studies point the same way: under-preparation is associated with injury. The direction is CREDIBLE. Finish rate versus peak km or longest run at Comrades: UNKNOWN.

Gaps to send to web-fetch

Frandsen full text (subgroups); Daniels' 3rd/4th edition long-run page; the FIRST and Galloway book tables; NHS C25K week 9; the Machado PDF (redalyc, may be open).

factdesk4.md

FACT DESK 4 — beginner progressions, long runs, run-walk, retest noise, ultra prediction

Method: 19 WebSearch queries. The proxy blocked publisher sites, so no primary PDF was opened and nothing below is graded higher than CORROBORATED. Grades follow the charter: two independent sources = CORROBORATED; one = ALLEGED. Two mirrors of the same press release count as one source.

1. NHS Couch-to-5K and GRONORUN schedules

C25K (3 runs/week; each run starts with a 5-min brisk walk). Running minutes per session:

WkStructureRun min
160 s run / 90 s walk, 20 min8
290 s run / 2 min walk, 20 min~9
32 × (90 s run, 90 s walk, 3 min run, 3 min walk)9
43-5-3-5 min runs with walks between16
5R1 3×5 min; R2 2×8 min; R3 20 min continuous15 / 16 / 20
6R1 5-8-5; R2 2×10; R3 25 continuous18 / 20 / 25
725 min continuous ×325
828 min continuous ×328
930 min continuous ×330
  • CORROBORATED: weeks 1–5, 7 and 9, and week 6 run 3. Sources: healthwell.eani.org.uk/node/2247, pita.org.uk/images/Couch_to_5k_29_July_2020.pdf, utoledo.edu/med/wellness/docs/couch-to-5k-training-plan.pdf, webdoctor.ie/health-hq/couch-to-5k.
  • CREDIBLE: week 6 runs 1 and 2. These come from memory of the NHS podcast; the snippets only said "intervals".
  • CREDIBLE, with a variant: week 8 at 28 min (webdoctor; utoledo). One US copy uses 30 min (point32health.org/documents/couch-5k-challeng).
  • All sources are reproductions of the NHS plan. NHS says the plan may take longer than 9 weeks and that a week can be repeated (healthwell; pita).
  • The schedule is equivalent to week-on-week jumps in continuous run time of 20 → 25 → 28 → 30 min.

GRONORUN (Buist et al. 2008, AJSM 36:33–39).

  • CORROBORATED (design): RCT, n = 532 novices training for a 4-mile (6.7 km) event. The standard plan ran 8 weeks; the graded plan ran 13 weeks and was built on the 10% rule. Each week except the last had 3 sessions mixing running and walking, with 5-min walk warm-up and cool-down. Injury = lower-limb or back complaint restricting running for at least 1 week. Injury incidence: 20.8% graded vs 20.3% standard, no difference. Sources: PMC1821023 / bmcmusculoskeletdisord 10.1186/1471-2474-8-24; pubmed 17940147; bjsm.bmj.com/content/43/2/155.
  • ALLEGED: the graded plan had 44 min of running in week 5 (one search snippet).
  • UNKNOWN: the week-by-week minutes. They are in Table 1 of the BMC 2007 protocol, which is open access (PMC1821023). It should be opened via web-fetch.

2. Toresdahl et al., NYC Marathon first-timers

Paper: Phys Sportsmed 2022;50(3), online 2021, doi 10.1080/00913847.2021.1907257. Cohort: 720 runners enrolled for the 2017 race, 583 started. Data were self-reported every 2 weeks from 12 weeks out. CORROBORATED: news.hss.edu; amssm.org/going-the-distance:-takin-p-254.html.

  • "Minor injury": UNKNOWN. Only the major-injury definition was found: "kept them from being able to participate in the race or finish the race". That is ALLEGED, because the AMSSM, HSS and Newswise items are one press release.
  • Adjustment: multivariable models were used (prior half marathon OR 0.40, 0.22–0.76). Whether they adjusted for weekly mileage or pace is UNKNOWN.
  • Longest run: both forms appear. The abstract treats it as continuous for race-day injury (OR 0.87, 0.81–0.94, probably per mile). The press release treats it as binary ("did not complete an 18-mile run" → higher risk). Grade: CREDIBLE. The units and which model is which still need checking.
  • ≥4 runs/week: associated with training-period injury (abstract plus press: CORROBORATED). Whether this was adjusted for volume is UNKNOWN. Treat it as possibly confounded by volume.
  • Do not confuse this paper with Toresdahl, BJSM 2023;57:146 (pubmed 36113976), a separate NYC cohort on mileage increases.

3. Frandsen et al. 2025, BJSM 59:1203–10 (doi 10.1136/bjsports-2024-109380)

  • CORROBORATED (blogs.bmj.com/bjsm/?p=11751; pure.au.dk; physiotutors.com/?p=26053):
  • 5,205 runners from 87 countries, 588,071 Garmin sessions, followed for 18 months.
  • Exposure: each session's distance compared with the longest run in the previous 30 days.
  • Categories: ≤10% increase or regression (reference); small spike >10–30%; moderate >30–100%; large >100%.
  • CREDIBLE: hazard ratios of 1.64 (1.31–2.05), 1.52 (1.16–2.00) and 2.28 (1.50–3.48). These come from secondary summaries only.
  • CORROBORATED: the week-to-week change was not associated with injury. Two summaries report that the ACWR spike looked "protective".
  • CREDIBLE: the exposure is distance only, with no pace or duration (runningwithrock.com/critique-too-much-running/; physiotutors).
  • Subgroups for ≥3 h sessions, run-walk, or slow runners: UNKNOWN. None were found. The method should not assume the 10% session rule was validated for time-based, run-walk or very slow runners.

4. Galloway and McMillan for 5 h+ finishers

Galloway:

  • CORROBORATED: 3 days a week (two ~30-min weekday runs plus a weekend long run). Long runs on alternate weeks. Beginner plans peak at a 26–26.2-mile long run. Plans last 26–30 weeks. Sources: finalsurge.com/coach/JeffGalloway/plan/39110; foxnews.com/health/training-for-your-first-marathon-you-can-do-this; forum.slowtwitch.com/t/358395; en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Galloway.
  • ALLEGED: long runs at about 2 min/mile slower than current ability, with walk breaks. Last long run 3 weeks before the race.
  • Derived estimate, not sourced: for a 5–6 h runner, a 26-mile long run is about 6–7 h on feet.
  • UNKNOWN: the number of runs over 3 h and their exact spacing.

McMillan (mcmillanrunning.com/?p=141438): for 5–6 h marathoners, the longest run should be shorter than goal marathon time, because recovery costs interfere with the rest of training.

  • Grade: ALLEGED, one first-party source. An exact cap or count of runs over 3 h is UNKNOWN.
  • Contrast: other coaches cap the long run at 2.5–3 h (ALLEGED). Galloway and McMillan therefore form two opposed "schools" that the method should expose as a parameter.

5. Longest run as a share of weekly time

  • C25K: about 33% in weeks 7–9, because the three sessions are equal. In week 5 the longest run is 20/51 ≈ 39%, and in week 6 it is 25/63 ≈ 40%. Derived from item 1; CREDIBLE.
  • Higdon 10K Novice: three runs a week, about 9 mi/week, longest about 6 mi. Unclear whether these are averages or peaks. ALLEGED (trainingpeaks.com/.../tp-139211).
  • UNKNOWN: published 4-run/week 5K/10K figures. No data were found.

6. 5K/10K retest variability

Machado et al. 2015 (Rev Bras Ciênc Esporte 37:207–13).

  • CORROBORATED (redalyc.org/pdf/4013/401341527002.pdf; doaj.org/article/e52fb675a88341bba04d91dc8b7564ea): 31 male recreational runners each ran 2 × 5K and 2 × 10K on a track. No significant test-retest difference.
  • 5K: ICC 0.94, CV 3.44%.
  • 10K: ICC 0.97, CV 2.43%.
  • UNKNOWN: the interval between trials.

Other studies:

  • Fisher et al. 2017 (johk.pl/?p=4268; ueaeprints.uea.ac.uk/id/eprint/90082): 6 competitive runners, 48 h apart, CV 1.5%. CORROBORATED.
  • Sunderland study (sure.sunderland.ac.uk/6900): moderately trained runners, 2 weeks apart, typical error 14.7 s, CV about 0.95%. ALLEGED.

Implication: a 5K change smaller than about 3–4% in recreational runners may be noise.

7. Run-walk vs continuous running and injury

  • Hottenrott et al. 2016 (J Sci Med Sport). CORROBORATED (edoc.unibas.ch/57171; nbcnews.com/news/amp/wbna56811438):
  • 42 runners randomised to run-walk or continuous running.
  • Finish times 4:14 vs 4:07 (ns).
  • Less muscle pain and fatigue with run-walk (p = 0.006).
  • Similar rises in cardiac biomarkers in both groups.
  • No injury outcome was measured.
  • Hottenrott walk protocol: CONTRADICTED. Reports say either 1 min walk about every 1.5 mi / 2.5 km, or 2 min run / 30 s walk.
  • UNKNOWN: any controlled study with injury as the outcome. None was found.
  • ALLEGED: a Couch-to-5K cohort (Relph 2023?) with about 19% injury incidence, from a blog only.
  • Context: NLstart2run (n = 1,696) did not test run-walk.

8. Road 50K plans for runners at ≥7:00/km — UNKNOWN

No searches were left; this needs a follow-up.

9. Frandsen: is a pre-illness run inside the 30-day reference window? — UNKNOWN

The window is defined as the previous 30 days. Whether gaps from illness or injury were handled specially was not found. The method should not assume it.

10. Ultra finish times vs marathon PB

  • CORROBORATED: Riegel's standard exponent is 1.06 (runnersconnect.net/race-calculators/; thieme-connect.de/products/ejournals/html/10.1055/a-2401-6234).
  • ALLEGED:
  • A fit to Comrades finishers gave 1.05 (95% CI 1.029–1.062) (r-bloggers.com/2015/04/comrades-marathon-finish-predictions/).
  • A half-to-marathon fit on 520 runners gave 1.16 (allendowney.substack.com).
  • A commercial Garmin analysis reports a median of 1.13, not peer-reviewed.
  • ALLEGED: Sports Med Int Open 2024 found Riegel about 80% accurate on 60 ultra races, using 50K as the reference race.
  • UNKNOWN: recreational 50K/100K exponents relative to marathon PB. The method should treat the exponent as a parameter, with a default of ≥1.06, and flag predictions as low-confidence.

Priority follow-ups (web-fetch)

  1. PMC1821023, Table 1 (GRONORUN minutes).
  2. Frandsen full text: subgroups and handling of gaps.
  3. Toresdahl full text: adjustment set and definition of minor injury.
factdesk5.md

Fact Desk 5 — findings (21 Oct 2026)

Method: 18 WebSearch queries, no primary pages opened (publisher sites blocked). Highest grade available is CORROBORATED (two independent sources agree). A claim with one source is ALLEGED. Several copies of the same paper (PubMed, repository, preprint) count as one source.

---

1. Recalled or self-reported race times vs official results

  • Finding: In 1,320 marathon runners, survey-reported finish times differed from official bib times. Runners who missed their personal best were much more likely to report a flattering, wrong time (p < 0.01), and the error grew as the miss grew. — ALLEGED (single study; several mirrors of it) — Hyun et al., Front Psychol 2022, link ; link
  • Not found: two targeted searches turned up no general validation study (agreement statistics, typical error in seconds or %) for self-reported PBs at 5K–marathon. — UNKNOWN
  • Design implication (desk view, not evidence): treat a typed-in time as possibly optimistic. Prefer a dated official result or a recent device-recorded effort, and ask "when, which race" so that old or downhill PBs can be down-weighted.

2. How lay readers read ranges and evidence labels

  • Verbal probability words are read very differently by different people. In a systematic review of 33 studies, mean readings of "rare" ran from 7 to 21% and "common" from 34 to 71%. Lay readings are often higher than the EU drug-label standard. Most people (10 of 12 samples) prefer numbers, alone or with words. — CORROBORATED (systematic review plus an independent Dutch study showing similar variability) — link ; link
  • Numeric ranges: in five UK experiments (n = 5,780), giving a numeric range slightly lowered confidence in the number but did not lower trust in the source. A follow-up on COVID statistics (N = 10,519) found that a vague verbal note such as "there is uncertainty" can lower trust in both the number and the source, so a numeric range is the better choice. — CORROBORATED (two separate papers from the same group, both reported by independent summaries; independence is partial) — van der Bles et al., PNAS 2020, doi 10.1073/pnas.1913678117, link ; link
  • Evidence-strength labels (GRADE-style): in a Norway/US trial, plain-language statements helped people understand uncertainty, but GRADE wording ("moderate certainty") did no better than no label. Policymakers read the levels as roughly 90% / 75% / 45% likely to hold for high / moderate / low. Hedged verbs ("likely results in", "may result in") were acceptable to more than 60% of review users. — ALLEGED (each point rests on one conference abstract or paper) — link ; link
  • Implication: show predictions as numeric ranges, and avoid bare labels like "well supported". If labels are used, pair each with a plain sentence and, where possible, a number.

3. 50K and 100K time relative to marathon time

  • Marathon PB predicts 100 km time. In 93 recreational men, marathon PB correlated r = 0.72 with 100 km time. In 66 runners, marathon PB (r² = 0.33) and weekly training volume (r² = 0.22) were the significant predictors. Volume matters relatively more for the 100 km than for the marathon. — CORROBORATED (two Knechtle-group cohorts plus a Tanda & Knechtle review; same research group, so independence is limited) — link ; link
  • No published empirical ratio or exponent for converting marathon time to 50K or 100K was found. — UNKNOWN
  • Riegel's model: Riegel's published running exponents are about 1.05–1.08. A 2024 study found plain Riegel was about 80% accurate for ultras, against 87.5% for UltraSignup and 90.4% for a machine-learning (LSTM) model. One 100 km championship study gave 100K(s) = 788.96 + 2.16 × 50K(s). — ALLEGED (one source per figure) — link ; link
  • Implication: any ultra prediction from a marathon time needs a wide range, should be adjusted for training volume, and should be labelled as low-certainty.

4. 5K/10K training at 2 runs per week

  • No trial was found that reports 5K completion for a 2-runs-per-week programme. — UNKNOWN
  • Related evidence: an RCT in novices (<2 years running, <20 km/week) ran two supervised running sessions a week for 24 weeks; the strength-training arm had fewer lower-limb injuries. A 74-person pilot of 2 supervised sessions plus 1 free session a week saw 8 vs 3 injuries, which was not significant. The Dutch "Start to Run" 6-week programme builds to 5 km run 2–3 times a week. — ALLEGED (one source each) — link (C25K context) ; link ; link
  • UK survey: first-year runners on self-devised plans were injured more often than runners on structured plans such as C25K. — ALLEGED (observational, one source) — link

5. Frandsen 2025: is the 30-day reference valid for runs over 2 h?

  • Core result: 5,205 Garmin users followed for 18 months. Injury rate rose when one session exceeded the longest run of the previous 30 days by more than 10%. Hazard ratios were 1.64 for >10–30%, 1.52 for >30–100% and 2.28 for >100%. Week-to-week ratio showed no relationship, and ACWR showed a negative one. — CORROBORATED — link ; link ; full text: link (not opened)
  • Runs over 2 h, marathon or ultra subgroups: no subgroup or limitation statement was found in any search result. — UNKNOWN. A critique exists (link) but its points were not retrieved.
  • Secondary-source conflicts: RunnersConnect's band description and Pheidi's "588,000 sessions across 87 countries" do not match the paper as reported elsewhere. Treat them as unverified.
  • Action: a web-fetch of the SDU PDF (limitations section, distribution of session lengths) is needed before using ">10% of the 30-day longest run" as a hard rule for long runs.

6. Relph 2023 (Couch-to-5K)

  • Study: IJERPH 20:6682. 110 UK participants on a modified, instructor-led 9-week C25K, not the app alone. 81.8% were female, mean age 47.1, mean BMI 28.1, and 15 drop-outs were interviewed. Drop-out was linked to musculoskeletal injury and the programme's fixed progression; the authors recommend injury-prevention advice and more flexible designs. — CORROBORATED — link ; link
  • Completion rate 27.3%: — ALLEGED (one search summary and a blog; not seen in the abstract)
  • Time taken to complete, and share of people repeating weeks: not found. — UNKNOWN. The app does allow repeating weeks (link).

7. Goom, Donnelly & Brockwell 2019, postnatal return to running (brief; overlaps with the medical hearing)

  • What it says: running is not advised before 3 months postnatal; return between 3 and 6 months, after screening. Screening covers pelvic floor, diastasis, load-and-impact tests, strength, psychological state and RED-S. After that comes a graded return, for example C25K. The authors say it supports clinical reasoning and is not a protocol. — CORROBORATED — link ; link ; guideline PDF: link
  • Exact test doses (for example a 30-min walk or 10 hops): not confirmed. — UNKNOWN

8. Race cardiac arrest by distance and age

  • RACER-1 (Kim et al., NEJM 2012; US, 2000–2010; 10.9 M runners): 59 arrests, 0.54/100k overall. Marathon 1.01 vs half-marathon 0.27; men 0.90 vs women 0.16. 71% were fatal. Mean age was 42; non-survivors were younger than survivors (39 vs 49). — CORROBORATED — link ; link
  • RACER-2 (JAMA 2025; US, 2010–2023; ~29.3 M finishers): 176 arrests, 0.60/100k. Men 1.12 vs women 0.19; marathon 1.04 vs half-marathon 0.47. Death rate fell to about 0.19/100k and survival rose to 66% (from 29%), credited to CPR and AED use. There was a slight rise in arrests since 2020. — CORROBORATED — link ; link
  • By age (Japan, Resuscitation 2024; 516 marathons, ~4.1 M runners): 1.7/100k overall. By age: <40 0.9, 40s 0.9, 50s 2.6, 60+ 5.5. No age gradient in women. The authors advise pre-race check-ups for men aged 60 and over. — ALLEGED (one institutional press release) — link
  • 5K/10K and ultra distances: no rates were retrieved. — UNKNOWN

--- Overall: Q8 and the core of Q2 are solid. Q1, Q3, Q4 and the >2 h part of Q5 are evidence gaps. The method should treat them as stated defaults with wide uncertainty, not as evidence-based rules.

factdesk6.md

Fact Desk 6 — race recovery, heat ≥65, prompt compliance, CEL unknowns, test oracles

Method: 13 WebSearch queries. Publisher pages were blocked and no primary source was opened, so the highest grade possible is CORROBORATED. Nothing here is VERIFIED. Grades: CORROBORATED (two or more independent sources agree), CREDIBLE (one good source plus plausibility), ALLEGED (one source only), UNKNOWN.

1. Minimum recovery between races

  • Convention: "one easy day per mile raced" (Jack Foster), meaning no hard sessions or races for about 3 days after a 5K, 6 after a 10K, 13 after a half and 26 after a marathon. CORROBORATED as a coaching convention, not as physiology. active.com, Inquirer. Some sources give 1–2 days per mile instead (trainingplan.dev), so the range itself is a parameter.
  • Ultra conventions differ by a factor of 10. One version is one day off per 10 miles, or per 10 km on vertical or high-altitude courses (iRunFar). Another applies one day per mile, which gives about 100 days after a 100-miler (Inquirer). CONTRADICTED (no convention). Sources agree that these rules apply only to A-race efforts. CORROBORATED (iRunFar, active.com).
  • Marathon markers: easy 40-minute runs at 48, 96 and 144 h after a marathon did not slow CK or LDH recovery over 8 days, and squat-jump performance was better at 96 h. ALLEGED (one RCT, found through PEDro/UJI records: PEDro, UJI). Popular sources say CK can stay raised for about 6–10 days (InsideTracker, Marathon Handbook): CREDIBLE at best.
  • Half marathon: two half marathons 7 days apart gave the same performance, although CK was raised after each. ALLEGED (Int J Sports Med 2000). CK was still raised 48 h after a half-distance triathlon (e-balonmano): related evidence only.
  • Ultra (100 mi, Western States): soreness and fatigue were back to baseline by day 5, but 400 m speed was still 12.4% slower at day 5 (Newswise). Inflammation returns to normal more slowly than muscle damage markers (Martínez-Navarro PDF). CREDIBLE.
  • Design implication: use the convention as a conservative default with the range exposed as a parameter, and allow easy running from about 48 h after the race. No marker evidence gives a hard minimum for 5K or 10K: UNKNOWN.

2. Heat in runners aged 65 and over

  • Ageing reduces heat loss. Sweating starts later and is less effective, and skin blood flow control is changed, so older runners reach higher core temperature and heat strain. CORROBORATED (PMC6098859, GSSI SSE 44, PSU review).
  • Fitness offsets much of the decline. Fit older exercisers (55–70) respond much like young adults of the same fitness and can acclimatise, but with more variation between people, and data on masters athletes are sparse. CORROBORATED (PMC8222413, GSSI). Most of these participants were under 70, so applying the finding to runners over 75 is UNKNOWN.
  • Short-term heat acclimation in people over 50: 8 studies report lower core temperature after it, but the authors call the data limited. CREDIBLE (PMC9980817).
  • Guidance: I could not confirm a figure of 10–14 days to acclimatise from these results: UNKNOWN. The 2021 ACSM consensus statement on exertional heat illness was replaced in full by a corrigendum and drew a dissenting letter (ACSM, Buffalo letter). Cite the current version only after opening it. Sources emphasise drinking enough for older exercisers (GSSI): CREDIBLE.
  • Design implication: for runners aged 65 and over, apply a stricter heat downgrade and a slower acclimatisation ramp by default. Treat these as precautionary defaults, not values measured in that age group.

3. Compliance with mandatory short prompts

  • EMA baseline: pooled compliance was 75% (95% CI 72–78%) across 126 studies, below the usual 80% target. Compliance was not linked to prompt frequency or study length in that set. CORROBORATED (PMC6492133 and its Erasmus copy; the copy is the same paper, so a second population is cited next). EMA compliance in older adults is reported as high (PMC10347474).
  • Prompt fatigue: compliance often falls over time, but not always. A 12-month smartphone study found falling completion odds (JMIR mHealth 2025). In children, response fell from 74% on day 1 to 40% on day 7 (summary, secondary). Other studies found no decline (PMC6492133, m-Path review). CORROBORATED that the evidence is mixed.
  • One-tap micro-EMA: in a pilot of 33 people, smartwatch one-tap prompts got higher compliance than phone EMA despite about 8 times more interruptions (UbiComp 2016): ALLEGED. Over 6 months or more, micro-EMA compliance was 67.6% (SD 24.4) and completion 79% (JMIR mHealth 2021). Taken together: plan for about 65–80% response. CREDIBLE. Whether one-tap answers are valid is barely tested (PMC7991987).
  • No data on running or training apps, or on "mandatory" prompts: UNKNOWN. Design implication: treat a missing answer as unknown, never as "no pain".

4. CEL partial evaluation and unknowns

  • Yes, with nuance. CEL logic has four values (true, false, error, unknown), not three. True, false and error are definitive results. Unknown means more input could change the result. && and || are commutative, so false && unknown gives false and true || unknown gives true. CORROBORATED (cel-go README, Google OSS blog 2024).
  • Absent variables are not unknown automatically. A variable becomes unknown only when it is declared as such: in cel-go through a partial-activation or attribute-pattern mechanism (NewPartialAttributeFactory, godocs), and in Java through UnknownContext patterns (javadoc). Otherwise the variable is reported as missing, which is an error. CREDIBLE. The exact error text is not confirmed.
  • Residuals: with OptTrackState set, interpreter.Prune produces a residual expression, for example name.startsWith(...) (pkg.go.dev). CORROBORATED (README and godoc). I did not reach the normative cel-spec text: UNKNOWN.

5. Differential vs metamorphic testing: detection rates

  • No published head-to-head detection rates were found: UNKNOWN.
  • Static analysers: metamorphic testing found 10 of 14 issues that differential testing could not, and differential testing found the other 4. The authors say they cannot rank the two and call the techniques complementary. ALLEGED (arXiv 2408.13855).
  • Compilers: a comparison of random differential testing, different optimisation levels and EMI (a metamorphic method) found each detects different kinds of bug, with efficiency the strongest factor in effectiveness (NSF PAR). That the techniques complement each other is CORROBORATED (two independent papers).
  • Metamorphic testing depends on its relations: existing relations detected only 14.8% of 709 mutants in ML classifiers (NSF PAR 10112172): ALLEGED.
  • Design implication: for testing the rule engine, use both kinds of test, and do not claim either is superior.

6. Reading level for health and safety instructions (added at the coordinator's request)

  • Target: about US grade 6, with grade 8 as the ceiling. Many studies attribute the grade-6 target to the AMA. The NIH figure is given as grade 6 in some sources and as grades 6–8 or grade 8 in others. CORROBORATED that grades 6–8 is the norm. The exact AMA and NIH wording is UNKNOWN because the primary documents were not opened (PMC6334864, PMC4869433, PMC8205335, Springer CORR).
  • Most published materials miss the target. For example, only 2.1% of one sample met grade 6. CORROBORATED (same sources). The US federal backdrop is the Plain Writing Act. Design implication: aim for grade 6 or below on red-flag and safety text, and test it automatically with readability formulas. Note that a formula score is a proxy for understanding, not proof of it.

7. Showing a prediction range that excludes the user's target

  • No study tests this exact situation: UNKNOWN.
  • Related evidence: lay readers tend to read wide intervals as uncertainty rather than as reliability, and the wording of the question changes this (Essex, Løhre et al., Reading): CORROBORATED. Readers also make systematic errors with predictive intervals. They take them as point-like (deterministic) values, and their probability estimates for "inside" and "outside" the interval add up to more than 100% (core.ac.uk): ALLEGED. Readers overestimate effects when shown confidence intervals compared with prediction intervals (CHI 2020): ALLEGED.
  • Design implication (judgement, not evidence): state in words whether the target falls inside the range and what the range means, give the probability of reaching the target, and keep the target visible.

D. The final report must disclose

  1. Internal audit, same process.
  2. No primary source was opened, so CORROBORATED is the ceiling.

  4. No evidence that adaptive plans prevent injury. The method has never been run; its plan values are hand-computed and contain errors (§A).
  5. Release blockers B-1…B-5 are open: PAR-Q+ licence, regulatory reading, clinical sign-off, about 30 MVP rules still text_only, and fixtures and oracle not encoded.
  6. Self-report only: a false "no" defeats the pain rules.
  7. Only method_default is executable. The other school presets, marathon (R2) and ultra (R3) are specified but not ready.
  8. Dissents stay on record: the Contrarian (ultra stretch, 50K AER 3), First Principles (65+ gate) and Room C (0.80 threshold).
  9. Not medical advice.
